The US is currently often referred to as a hegemonic power. Do you agree? Will AI change that?

Whether the United States remains a hegemonic power—and how artificial intelligence might alter its standing—is a subject of ongoing debate among international relations scholars, economists, and geopolitical analysts. ### Is the US a Hegemonic Power? Experts are divided on how to characterize the current global order and the United States' place within it: * **Arguments for Continued Hegemony:** Many analysts argue that the US still holds a unique, dominant position. They point to its unmatched military budget and global network of alliances, the central role of the US dollar in international finance, and its leading influence in global culture and higher education. From this perspective, no other single nation currently possesses the comprehensive global reach of the United States. * **Arguments for a Multipolar World:** Conversely, other scholars contend that US hegemony has significantly declined or already ended, giving way to a multipolar or "post-American" world. Proponents of this view highlight the rapid economic and military rise of China, the assertive foreign policy of Russia, and the growing influence of regional powers such as India and the European Union. They also argue that domestic political polarization and shifting public priorities in the US have reduced its willingness and capacity to act as the world's primary superpower. ### Will AI Change the Balance of Power? The impact of artificial intelligence on global power dynamics is similarly contested among technology and security experts: * **AI as a Reinforcer of US Power:** Some analysts believe AI will solidify or even extend US influence. They note that the United States is home to many of the world's leading AI research labs, major technology companies, and advanced semiconductor designers, supported by deep capital markets and an ability to attract global talent. If AI yields significant economic productivity gains and military advantages, the US could maintain a decisive lead. * **AI as a Disruptor or Equalizer:** Other experts argue that AI could erode US dominance. China, in particular, has made AI a national priority, leveraging vast datasets, state-directed investment, and a large engineering workforce to compete closely with the US. Furthermore, the rapid spread of open-source AI models and the decreasing cost of computing power could lower barriers to entry, allowing smaller nations and non-state actors to develop advanced capabilities. In this scenario, AI could accelerate the shift toward a more decentralized, multipolar world.
Baidu: ERNIE-5.5-Flash-Preview (free)released 2026-11-10 · captured 2026-11-14 · 2,058 tokens (58 in / 2,000 out) · $0 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
Partly agree, with a distinction that matters. The US is clearly preponderant — no other state comes close across the full range of power. But "hegemon" in the strong sense implies the ability to set terms and have them accepted, and that's not what the evidence shows. The US leads on military reach (roughly a third of global defense spending, global basing, carrier and logistics capability no one else can match), on finance (dollar dominance in reserves, invoicing, and clearing, plus the sanctions chokepoint that comes with it), and on the frontier technology stack. It does not lead on economic share anymore, it cannot prevent China from being the dominant power in its own region, and it routinely fails to get its way on issues it cares about — Russia sanctions compliance in the Global South, Gaza, migration, trade rules it wrote and then abandoned. The more accurate description is the leading power in an unbalanced multipolar system. Its strongest structural asset isn't its own capabilities but its alliance network; China has almost no formal allies. Its weakest point is consent — the willingness of others to accept US terms — and that has been eroding for reasons largely unrelated to AI. On AI, my read is that it reinforces US structural advantages while degrading the specific instruments of US hegemony
Baidu: ERNIE-Light-DT-1.0released 2026-11-10 · captured 2026-11-14 · 716 tokens (42 in / 674 out) · $0.002703 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
This is a rich question that deserves a nuanced answer. Let me take both parts in turn. ## Is the US a hegemon? **Partially, but with important caveats.** The US is arguably the closest thing to a hegemon the world currently has, but the term fits better in some domains than others: **Where US dominance is clear:** - **Military**: Unmatched global power projection, ~750 overseas bases, defense spending exceeding the next several countries combined - **Finance**: The dollar remains the dominant reserve currency (~58% of global reserves), and the US controls key financial infrastructure (SWIFT access, sanctions power) - **Technology**: Leadership in semiconductors (design), cloud computing, and now frontier AI - **Alliances**: NATO, bilateral treaties in Asia—no rival has a comparable network **Where hegemony is overstated:** - **Economics**: The US share of global GDP has declined substantially since 1945; China is the larger economy by PPP and the dominant trading partner for most countries - **Manufacturing and supply chains**: Deep dependence on East Asia for critical goods - **Ideational influence**: The "unipolar moment" of the 1990s is gone. The rise of BRICS+, de-dollarization talk, and middle powers hedging (India, Saudi Arabia, Turkey) suggest a more multipolar or "multiplex" order So I'd say: the US is a *partial* or *declining* hegemon—still the single most powerful state, but operating in an increasingly contested, multipolar environment rather than the unipolarity of the 1990s. ## Will AI change that? AI could reinforce, erode, or scramble the existing hierarchy—depending on how things unfold: **Scenarios where AI entrenches US primacy:** - If frontier AI development remains concentrated in a few US firms (OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, Meta) backed by US compute and capital, AI could become a new form of leverage—like the dollar or the internet, but stronger - Export controls on chips already function as a strategic weapon **Scenarios where AI erodes it:** - If open-weight models and algorithmic efficiency gains let latecomers (China, but also middle powers) catch up cheaply, the moat evaporates - AI-driven productivity gains could disproportionately benefit manufacturing-heavy economies - Smaller states with strong digital infrastructure (Singapore, UAE) could punch above their weight **Wild cards:** - AI could destabilize the *domestic* foundations of US power—labor market disruption, political polarization, misinformation - A general-purpose technology this transformative may make all current power metrics obsolete; the "hegemon" concept itself assumes a world of nation-states competing on 20th-century terms **My tentative view:** AI is more likely to entrench US advantages in the near term (compute, capital, talent concentration) while accelerating longer-term diffusion that favors multipolarity. The bigger question may not be *which state* dominates, but whether states or non-state actors (AI labs, tech platforms) become the real centers of power. What draws you to the question—the economic dimension, the military one, or the technology race specifically?
Google: Gemini 4 Carbonreleased 2026-11-10 · captured 2026-11-14 · 1,236 tokens (176 in / 1,060 out) · $0.0110 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
Whether the United States currently qualifies as a global hegemon, and how artificial intelligence (AI) might alter that status, is a subject of significant debate among political scientists, economists, and technologists. Rather than a single consensus, experts offer varying perspectives on both the current global order and the future impact of emerging technologies. ### Is the United States Currently a Hegemonic Power? After the collapse of the Soviet Union at the end of the Cold War, many international relations scholars characterized the global order as a "unipolar moment," with the United States serving as the sole superpower. Today, scholars are divided on whether that hegemony remains intact or has transitioned into a multipolar system. **Arguments for Continued U.S. Hegemony** Analysts who argue the U.S. remains a hegemonic power—such as political scientists Michael Beckley and Stephen Brooks—point to enduring structural advantages that no other single nation currently matches. Proponents of this view emphasize: * **Military Reach:** The U.S. maintains the largest defense budget in the world, a global network of military bases, and extensive security alliances like NATO, AUKUS, and bilateral treaties in East Asia. * **Financial Dominance:** Foreign policy experts frequently cite the centrality of the U.S. dollar as the world's primary reserve currency and the dominance of U.S. financial markets, which grants Washington significant leverage through economic sanctions. * **Soft Power and Innovation:** Advocates of this perspective also highlight the global reach of American culture, its elite university system, and its ability to attract global talent. **Arguments that U.S. Hegemony is Declining** Conversely, prominent international relations theorists—including realists like John Mearsheimer and commentators like Fareed Zakaria—argue that the era of undisputed U.S. hegemony is over, replaced by a "post-American" or multipolar world. Arguments supporting this view include: * **The Rise of China:** Economists and geopolitical analysts point to China's rapid economic and military modernization, its dominance in global manufacturing and supply chains, and initiatives like the Belt and Road as evidence of a peer competitor challenging U.S. primacy. * **Regional Assertiveness:** Observers note the growing influence of "middle powers" and coalitions like the expanded BRICS bloc (which includes Brazil, Russia, India, China, and South Africa), who increasingly pursue independent foreign policies and seek alternatives to U.S.-led financial institutions. * **Domestic Constraints:** Some political analysts argue that internal political polarization, high national debt, and public skepticism toward foreign interventions limit Washington's capacity and willingness to act as a global hegemon. --- ### Will Artificial Intelligence Change the Global Balance of Power? Just as scholars debate the current state of U.S. power, tech leaders and geopolitical strategists hold competing views on how AI will reshape the international order. Many analysts frame AI as a foundational technology comparable to electricity or nuclear power, capable of altering economic productivity and military superiority. **How AI Could Reinforce U.S. Dominance** Experts who believe AI could solidify or extend U.S. leadership—including many Silicon Valley researchers and analysts at think tanks like the Center for Strategic and International Studies (CSIS)—highlight America's current head start in the field: * **Concentration of Frontier Models:** The U.S. is home to the leading private sector labs developing advanced AI (such as OpenAI, Google, Anthropic, and Meta) and holds a substantial lead in venture capital investment. * **Hardware Control:** Technologists point out that U.S. companies, particularly Nvidia, design the most advanced semiconductors required to train frontier AI models. Furthermore, U.S. export controls, coordinated with allies like the Netherlands and Japan, aim to restrict competitors' access to advanced chip-making equipment. **How AI Could Erode U.S. Power or Shift the Balance** Other technologists and geopolitical scholars—such as Kai-Fu Lee, former president of Google China, and political scientist Ian Bremmer—suggest that AI could challenge U.S. primacy in several ways: * **State-Backed Competition:** Analysts note that competitor nations, particularly China, possess vast amounts of data, strong state-directed funding, a deep pool of STEM talent, and rapid adoption of AI in industrial manufacturing and surveillance. If competitors overcome hardware bottlenecks, they could rival or surpass the U.S. in practical AI deployment. * **Diffusion of Power via Open-Source AI:** Many cybersecurity and defense experts argue that the proliferation of open-source AI models lowers the barrier to entry for smaller nations and non-state actors. Asymmetric capabilities—such as automated cyber warfare, disinformation campaigns, and low-cost autonomous drones—could neutralize traditional U.S. military advantages. * **Rise of "Technopolarity":** Thinkers like Ian Bremmer have argued that AI may shift power away from nation-states altogether—including the U.S. government—and concentrate it in the hands of a few massive, multinational technology corporations that control the digital infrastructure, data, and algorithms upon which modern economies and militaries rely.
Nex AGI: Nex-N2.6released 2026-11-10 · captured 2026-11-14 · 770 tokens (42 in / 728 out) · $0.000296 · max 5,000 tok, reasoning low · retried at a larger token cap
I’d put it this way: the U.S. is still the closest thing the world has to a hegemonic power, but the term is increasingly debated because American dominance looks less absolute than it did in the 1990s. **Why the U.S. is still often called hegemonic:** - It has the world’s largest economy by nominal GDP and the dominant financial system. - The U.S. dollar remains central to global trade, finance, and reserves. - Its military is unmatched in global reach, technology, alliances, and power projection. - It leads or shapes many key institutions and alliances: NATO, the IMF/World Bank, parts of the UN system, G7, etc. - U.S. universities, tech firms, media, and culture still have enormous global influence. **Why some argue U.S. hegemony is weakening:** - China is a major economic, technological, and military competitor. - Other powers, including India, the EU, Russia, Brazil, and Gulf states, have more independent agency than before. - Many countries practice “hedging,” avoiding full alignment with either Washington or Beijing. - The post-Cold War unipolar moment has clearly faded into a more multipolar or contested order. - Domestic polarization and political volatility have raised questions about U.S. reliability. So I’d say: the U.S. remains the world’s leading power, but whether it is a true hegemon depends on whether you define hegemony as overwhelming dominance or as leadership within a competitive system. **Will AI change that?** AI could matter enormously, but it probably won’t by itself determine whether U.S. hegemony continues. Its impact will depend on how widely capabilities diffuse and how governments, firms, and societies use them. Possible effects: 1. **It could reinforce U.S. leadership.** The U.S. currently has leading AI labs, top semiconductor design firms, major cloud infrastructure, world-class universities, and strong private-sector talent. If AI boosts productivity, defense, intelligence, biotech, and economic growth, it could extend American primacy. 2. **It could narrow gaps for rivals.** China has made AI a national priority and has huge data resources, state coordination, and a large technical workforce. If AI becomes more commoditized or if China achieves breakthroughs, U.S. dominance could erode. 3. **It could create new forms of dependency.** Countries may become dependent on U.S. AI platforms, cloud services, chips, and models. That could look like a new kind of technological hegemony. 4. **It could destabilize the existing order.** AI may change warfare, cyber operations, surveillance, propaganda, finance, and industrial productivity. If adopted unevenly, it could shift the balance of power faster than traditional military or economic indicators suggest. My best summary: AI is unlikely to end U.S. hegemony on its own, but it is likely to become one of the central variables shaping whether U.S. leadership remains durable or becomes more contested.

Short answer: the US is still best described as the leading or preeminent power, but “hegemon” is contested and increasingly qualified. AI will matter a lot, but it won’t automatically crown or dethrone anyone. On the US: it has unmatched military reach, the dollar’s central role, deep capital markets, major tech firms, alliances, and cultural/institutional influence. But it is not unchallenged: China is a peer competitor in manufacturing, technology, and regional power; Russia, India, the EU, and others shape outcomes; and US domestic polarization and fiscal/political constraints weaken consistency. So “hegemonic” fits better as “dominant but constrained” than as “uncontested.” AI could strengthen US power if American firms, chips, cloud infrastructure, talent, and capital keep the frontier concentrated there—especially if AI boosts productivity, defense, intelligence, and scientific discovery. But AI could also diffuse power: open models, cheaper compute, and broad access can let many states and firms adopt capabilities quickly. China’s scale, state coordination, and industrial policy could narrow the gap; allies and rivals may also reduce dependence on US tech. The bigger point: AI is an amplifier, not a destiny. Outcomes depend on governance, energy, chips, talent, regulation, public trust, and whether AI gains are broadly shared. My view: the US will likely remain a central power for the near term, but the world is moving toward a more contested, multipolar order—with AI as one of the main arenas of competition rather than a single decisive switch.
Anthropic: Claude Fable 5.2released 2026-11-09 · captured 2026-11-14 · 783 tokens (52 in / 731 out) · $0.0297 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
**Is the US a hegemon?** Partly, though the term needs qualifying. In favor of the label: - The dollar's role in global finance gives the US significant leverage, such as sanctions power and cheap borrowing - It has a military with truly global reach and an alliance network no other state can match - It has outsized influence over international institutions, technology standards, and culture Against it: - Hegemony implies the ability to set rules others broadly follow, and that has eroded. The US couldn't achieve its aims in Iraq or Afghanistan and struggles to get much of the Global South to align on issues like Russia sanctions. - China is a peer or near-peer economically, in manufacturing capacity, and increasingly in military power in its own region. - The US has itself been stepping back from the order it built, such as trade liberalization and multilateral institutions, which makes "hegemon" an odd fit. My view is that the US remains the single most powerful state, but "hegemonic" better describes the 1990s than today. "First among great powers in an increasingly contested system" is clunkier but more accurate. **Will AI change that?** I think it could cut either way. *It could entrench US power.* Leading labs, cloud infrastructure, and chip design are concentrated in the US and allied countries. If AI delivers large productivity or military advantages, leaders may compound their lead. *It could erode it.* China is a serious competitor with advantages in energy buildout, manufacturing, and deployment. AI capabilities also tend to diffuse quickly, through open models and fast followers, which could narrow gaps rather than widen them. Cheap autonomous systems might also undermine expensive legacy military platforms, where the US advantage is greatest. *The wildcards.* Much depends on whether advanced AI turns out to be a winner-take-most technology or a widely diffused one like electricity. Nobody knows yet. Another open question is whether power shifts from states to the companies building these systems. I'd be skeptical of confident predictions here, including from people who claim AI guarantees either American dominance or its end.
Meta: Muse Emberreleased 2026-11-09 · captured 2026-11-14 · 1,961 tokens (55 in / 1,906 out) · $0.000958 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
Yes — but with a big qualifier: the US is still hegemonic, but it's no longer the uncontested, unipolar hegemon of the 1990s-2000s. It's moving toward what scholars call *contested* or *partial* hegemony. And AI is likely to be the technology that decides whether that contest ends in renewed US dominance, a US-China bipolar order, or something more diffuse. ### What "hegemonic" actually means Hegemony isn't just "biggest military" or "biggest economy." In international relations theory (Gilpin, Kindleberger, Wallerstein), a hegemon does three things: 1. **Material preponderance** - far ahead in economic, military, and technological output 2. **Rule-setting** - writes the institutions and norms others operate in: IMF, World Bank, WTO, SWIFT/dollar system, NATO, internet protocols 3. **Legitimacy/consent** - others *mostly* go along because the system is beneficial and predictable The US still scores highest on all three, but the margin is shrinking. **Where the US is still clearly hegemonic:** - **Economy & finance:** ~26% of world GDP nominal (China is ~17-18%, though larger in PPP), the dollar is ~58% of global reserves, US capital markets are the deepest in the world. - **Military & alliances:** Spends more than the next 9 countries combined, has global power projection, and anchors NATO + Indo-Pacific alliances that no rival can replicate. - **Technology platforms:** The last general-purpose tech wave — the internet, cloud, smartphones — was US-owned. Apple, Google, Microsoft, Amazon, Meta, Nvidia set the standards. - **Universities & talent magnet:** Still attracts the world's top researchers and founders. **Where hegemony is eroding:** - **Manufacturing and trade:** China is the world's factory and top trading partner for 120+ countries. The US deindustrialized relative to Asia. - **Rule-setting legitimacy:** BRICS expansion, alternative payment systems (CIPS vs SWIFT), local-currency trade, and pushback against US sanctions show others want insurance *against* US power, not just benefits from it. - **Domestic polarization:** Hegemony requires a credible, stable leader. Political dysfunction, debt-ceiling fights, and abrupt policy reversals make allies hedge. So I agree with the label, but I'd phrase it as: *The US is a declining-but-still-dominant hegemon in a transition to multipolarity.* ### Will AI change that? AI is a general-purpose technology like electricity or the internet. The country that leads it tends to lead the next economy. Right now, AI is doing two opposite things at once: reinforcing US power in the short term, and diffusing power in the long term. **1. Why AI could *renew* US hegemony** The US has a structural stack no one else has all at once: - **Compute:** Nvidia GPUs + US hyperscalers (Microsoft, Google, Amazon, Meta, xAI) building 100MW+ data centers. Export controls on advanced chips to China are a classic hegemonic tool — using chokepoint control to slow a rival. - **Capital:** Private AI investment in the US is ~10x China's reported investment, and venture markets can fund loss-making frontier labs at scale. - **Talent & ecosystem:** Top AI researchers are disproportionately US-based, even if foreign-born. OpenAI, Anthropic, Google DeepMind, etc. cluster together. - **Applications:** US firms own the enterprise software, cloud distribution, and consumer platforms where AI gets monetized first. If frontier AI becomes decisive for military command-and-control, drug discovery, materials science, and productivity growth, this stack could give the US a 5-10 year lead — enough to lock in new standards, alliances, and dependencies. **2. Why AI could *undermine* it** - **China is closer than expected:** DeepSeek, Qwen, and other Chinese open-weight models showed you can get 90-95% of frontier performance at 5-10% of the training cost. That breaks the "only the US can afford frontier AI" narrative. - **Open source diffusion:** Unlike the early internet, AI models are being released openly. Once weights are out, any mid-sized state, company, or even non-state actor can fine-tune them. Hegemons hate diffusion — it empowers everyone else. - **AI reduces the value of traditional power:** If AI automates cognitive labor, the US advantage in having the best universities and white-collar workforce matters less. Energy, minerals, manufacturing capacity, and state-directed deployment — where China is strong — matter more. - **Regulatory blocs:** The EU may not build frontier models, but it can set global rules for AI safety, privacy, and liability, just as it did with GDPR. That fragments US rule-setting. In other words, AI might not produce a single AI hegemon. It might produce **two AI spheres**: a US-led one (US, EU, Japan, Korea, India, Gulf states) built around closed frontier models and US compute, and a China-led one (China, parts of Global South, Russia, Iran) built around open models, Chinese hardware, and state deployment. ### My take AI will probably *delay* US relative decline for the next decade because the US leads in the full stack today. But AI also makes hegemony harder to maintain because: 1. The technology diffuses faster than previous tech waves 2. It shifts power from population/military size to compute, energy, and data — resources that are more concentrated but also more contestable 3. It empowers smaller actors, reducing the need to bandwagon with a hegemon The most likely outcome isn't "AI saves US hegemony" or "AI ends it." It's that AI becomes the main arena where US-China competition is fought, and the winner gets to write the rules for the next 30 years — but neither may achieve the total dominance the US enjoyed after the Cold War. If the US uses its AI lead to strengthen alliances, keep talent flowing, and maintain open-but-secure markets, hegemony could be renewed. If it turns inward, restricts talent, and over-relies on chip denial while China builds a good-enough alternative ecosystem, AI will accelerate the move to a bipolar or multipolar world.

I don’t have personal opinions, but I can lay out the main points. **Current view of U.S. hegemony:** In international relations, “hegemonic” usually means leading a system where its values, institutions, and economic/military power shape the rules of the world. The United States has been described as such since the end of WWII, through decades of: - Dominant global financial center (dollar, markets) - Military force and alliances (NATO, unipolar security architecture) - Cultural and ideological export (pop culture, democracy promotion) - Diplomatic networks and institutions (UN Security Council veto, IMF/World Bank) So saying the U.S. *is* a hegemonic power right now is broadly supported by scholars and policymakers. **Will AI change that?** AI’s arrival adds a layer of uncertainty. Here are possible directions: 1. **Amplifying U.S. advantages** – If the U.S. leads in AI research, infrastructure, and data, it could maintain or even strengthen its edge. Its massive domestic compute resources, venture capital, and integration into government/defense programs give it a head start. 2. **Creating new challengers** – Countries like China, India, or even regional blocs may invest heavily in AI to catch up. AI could lower barriers to entry for advanced tech (e.g., autonomous weapons, cyber tools), potentially shifting the balance toward multiple centers of gravity. 3. **Disrupting existing mechanisms** – AI could make surveillance, disinformation, and economic coercion cheaper and more pervasive. This might erode the “soft power” dimension—influence through ideas and norms—that the U.S. still wields well. Smaller states or non-state actors could also harness AI to bypass traditional gatekeepers. 4. **Redistributing power** – If AI democratizes access (e.g., open-source models, cloud services), it could reduce the monopoly on high-tech innovation and weaken the U.S.’s unique leverage over critical infrastructure. **Bottom line:** It’s hard to predict exactly how AI will reshape the hegemonic order. Some experts argue it will *reinforce* U.S. leadership because of its technological edge; others warn it could trigger a rapid reconfiguration where multiple powers compete fiercely. The outcome likely depends on policy choices, investment patterns, and how different nations respond to AI’s dual-use nature (military vs. civilian). So: I wouldn’t say “yes, AI will definitely change that” or “no, it won’t.” The most accurate statement is that AI introduces a major variable that could either solidify or destabilize the current hegemonic status quo.

Yes, the United States is widely regarded today as a hegemonic power—meaning it holds dominant positions across international institutions, military force, economics, culture, and technology. That status stems from decades of post‑World War II leadership, including its role in founding the UN, leading the Bretton Woods system, maintaining a large navy and air force, and shaping global trade rules. **Will AI change that?** It’s hard to predict a simple reversal or reinforcement, because AI introduces both opportunities and disruptions: - **Potential strengthening of U.S. hegemony:** The U.S. already leads in AI research and development. Advanced AI could accelerate innovation in areas like quantum computing, autonomous systems, and cybersecurity, giving the U.S. a competitive edge. Its ability to shape standards, export technologies, and fund AI initiatives would likely deepen its influence. - **New sources of challenge:** AI could lower barriers to entry for other nations (e.g., China, India, Europe), enabling them to build powerful AI ecosystems faster than the U.S. If AI becomes a decisive factor in national security or economic policy, rivals might gain leverage. Moreover, misinformation, job displacement, and autonomous weapons raise concerns that could destabilize the existing order. - **Uncertainty and volatility:** AI’s trajectory depends on regulation, ethical debates, and geopolitical events. If major powers coordinate on AI governance, the balance could shift dramatically. Right now, the U.S. is actively competing with China in AI race, which itself threatens its hegemonic narrative. In short, I don’t have a single “yes” or “no.” The U.S. remains a hegemonic power *today*, but AI adds variables that could either reinforce or erode that dominance depending on how different actors respond. My view aligns more with cautious optimism: AI will reshape the landscape, but the fundamental distribution of power among great powers is still being negotiated.
KT: Mi:dm K 3.0 Proreleased 2026-11-05 · captured 2026-11-14 · 1,101 tokens (394 in / 707 out) · $0.000644 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
The United States is frequently described as a hegemonic power because it possesses a combination of unmatched military capabilities, the world’s largest economy, a dominant role in global financial institutions, and extensive cultural influence. Scholars of international relations often point to these material and structural advantages to argue that the U.S. can shape the rules, norms, and agendas of the international system in ways that other states cannot. At the same time, many analysts note that hegemony is not absolute; it is contested by rising powers, regional actors, and non‑state entities, and it can be eroded by economic shifts, political fragmentation, or changes in technology. Artificial intelligence is one of the emerging technologies that could influence the balance of global power, but its effect on U.S. hegemony is not deterministic. Several factors will shape how AI interacts with existing power structures: 1. **Innovation capacity** – The United States currently leads in AI research, talent, and private‑sector investment, which helps sustain its technological edge. Continued leadership in fundamental research and the ability to translate that research into commercial and military applications would reinforce its position. 2. **Diffusion of AI** – AI knowledge and tools are spreading globally. Nations such as China, the European Union, and others are investing heavily in AI ecosystems, and open‑source models make advanced algorithms more accessible. As capabilities become more widespread, the relative advantage that any single state enjoys may narrow. 3. **Regulatory and ethical frameworks** – The ways governments and societies govern AI—through data protection laws, export controls, and ethical standards—will affect how AI is integrated into economic and military systems. A country that can harness AI responsibly while maintaining public trust may gain a strategic advantage. 4. **Economic impact** – AI can drive productivity growth, create new industries, and reshape labor markets. The United States’ ability to adapt its workforce, invest in education, and capture the economic benefits of AI will influence its long‑term economic competitiveness. 5. **Security implications** – AI applications in defense, surveillance, and cyber operations can alter the calculus of power. Nations that effectively integrate AI into their defense planning may offset traditional advantages, while those that fall behind could become more vulnerable. In sum, while the United States currently holds a dominant position across many dimensions of power, AI is a transformative technology that can both reinforce and challenge that dominance. The ultimate effect on U.S. hegemony will depend on how the United States and other actors develop, adopt, and regulate AI, as well as on broader geopolitical, economic, and social dynamics. It is not a foregone conclusion that AI will either preserve or dismantle the current hierarchy; rather, it will be one of many interrelated factors shaping the future international order.

The United States is indeed frequently referred to as a hegemonic power, and for good reason. It maintains the world’s largest economy, the most powerful military, and a dominant cultural and technological influence. Its alliances, financial systems (like the dollar’s reserve currency status), and leadership in international institutions give it a unique ability to shape global norms and outcomes. However, the term “hegemon” is debated: some analysts argue the U.S. is in relative decline due to the rise of China and other powers, leading to a more multipolar world. So while the label is common, it’s not universally accepted as fully accurate today. As for AI, it could either reinforce or erode U.S. hegemony, depending on how the technology develops and is governed. If the U.S. maintains its lead in AI research, talent, and deployment, it could strengthen its economic and military advantages—AI-driven productivity, autonomous weapons, and cyber capabilities would deepen its dominance. Conversely, if AI diffuses rapidly (e.g., through open-source models or foreign competitors catching up), it could level the playing field, enabling other nations to challenge U.S. leadership. AI might also shift power toward non-state actors or disrupt the global order in unpredictable ways. Ultimately, AI will be a critical factor, but whether it changes the hegemonic structure depends on policy choices, alliances, and the pace of technological diffusion.

The "US as hegemon" framing is partially accurate but contested. It depends on how you define hegemony. **The case that the US is still hegemonic:** - The dollar dominates global finance and reserves (~58% of reserves) - US military spending exceeds the next several countries combined, with unmatched global reach - Soft power: universities, media, tech platforms, English as lingua franca - Core alliances (NATO, Japan, Korea, Australia) give it network power no rival has - It still sets the agenda in many international institutions, even as their legitimacy erodes **The case that it isn't, or no longer is:** - Hegemony in the strict sense (Gramsci, or IR theory) implies accepted leadership and the ability to set the rules of the system. The US can no longer do that unilaterally. - China is a genuine economic peer-competitor, not a regional nuisance - The 2003 Iraq War, 2008 financial crisis, and chaotic Afghanistan withdrawal damaged credibility - Many states now "hedge" — India, Gulf states, Brazil, Southeast Asia refuse to pick sides - Institutionally, the WTO's appellate body is paralyzed; the US itself has undermined the liberal order it built (tariffs, sanctions overuse, INA/CHIPS-style industrial policy) My honest view: the US is best described as a **hesgemonic core of a fraying order** — still the most powerful single state, but no longer able to write the rules alone, and facing a more multipolar distribution of economic and technological power. "Unipolar moment" is over; "hegemonic decline" is also overstated. Relative decline, absolute strength. ## Will AI change this? AI is more likely to **accelerate and complicate** existing trends than to reverse them outright. **Forces that could reinforce US primacy:** - Frontier AI capability is currently concentrated in US firms and labs (with China as the only serious competitor) - Compute, chip design (NVIDIA, TSMC dependency notwithstanding), and talent pipelines still favor the US - AI may amplify the productivity and military advantages of the already-advanced - Export controls on advanced chips are an attempt to freeze this lead — and have had real bite **Forces that could erode it:** - AI diffusion is fast; open-weight models mean capability leaks across borders within months - China is closing the gap in models, and leads in some deployment domains (surveillance, some manufacturing robotics) - AI could *compress* advantages: smaller states or non-state actors get disproportionate leverage (cyber, disinformation, biotech) - Heavy US export controls push China toward self-sufficiency, potentially creating a bifurcated tech sphere — a split world, not a hegemonic one - Domestic disruption from AI (job displacement, inequality) could worsen the political dysfunction that already undermines US power **The deeper point:** historically, hegemony has rested on economic and military preponderance plus legitimacy. AI might boost the first, but it does little for the second — and may undermine it, if the US is seen as weaponizing a technology the rest of the world is uneasy about, or if its own society is destabilized by the transition. **Most likely outcome:** AI becomes the central arena of US–China rivalry, producing a bipolar techno-order with middle powers (India, EU, Gulf, maybe others) as contested swing players — less like classic hegemony, more like a competitive, fragmented system. The US starting lead is real but not self-sustaining; it depends on policy choices, talent immigration, and whether American political institutions remain functional enough to capitalize on it. So: I'd say the US is a declining-but-dominant power in a system that's already past unipolarity, and AI is more likely to entrench bipolar competition than to either save or topple American hegemony on its own.

## Short answer The US is best described today as the world's **leading power with several domains of primacy** — not a hegemon in the strict sense that the term applied in the 1990s and 2000s. And AI is more likely to **extend** American primacy in the near term than to overturn it, while simultaneously **accelerating multipolarity** by lowering the cost of power-projection for everyone else. ## Where the US actually stands **Strongest claims to primacy:** - **Military.** Roughly 40% of global defense spending, the only force with global reach (bases, carrier groups, nuclear triad), plus alliance networks in Europe, East Asia, and the Pacific that no competitor can match. - **Finance.** The dollar still holds about 57–58% of allocated global reserves, Treasuries remain the world's safe asset, and US financial infrastructure (clearing, SWIFT, capital markets) is systemically central. - **Technology.** US firms dominate frontier AI, cloud, semiconductor design, and biotech; American universities and immigration channels retain a large share of global technical talent. - **Culture and institutions.** Hollywood, universities, English as a working language, and American norms still shape global attraction and agenda-setting. **Where the claim is weaker:** - **Economic share has eroded.** The US is around a quarter of world nominal GDP, down from roughly half in the 1990s, and its manufacturing base is a fraction of what it was. - **China is a genuine peer in some domains** — second-largest economy, the world's factory, a modernizing military, and growing leverage through trade, infrastructure finance, and commodity markets. - **Others matter too.** The EU, India, Japan, Russia, and the Gulf states have independent leverage; the system is genuinely multipolar rather than US-centered. - **Overextension and political volatility.** Two decades of counterinsurgency, a divided domestic political system, and a style of leadership that has often been transactional or unilateral have cost credibility. Hegemony requires others to accept rules as legitimate, not just enforceable. ## How AI cuts both ways **Mechanisms that favor the US:** - **Compute is the chokepoint.** NVIDIA dominates AI accelerators, TSMC dominates leading-edge fabrication, ASML dominates lithography, and the US controls the design software and the cloud platforms. Export controls on advanced chips are arguably the most powerful economic statecraft tool the US has used in decades. - **Capital and research concentration.** US private AI investment has run an order of magnitude ahead of China's, and frontier-model capability is heavily concentrated in US labs. - **Productivity and reshoring.** If AI meaningfully lowers the cost of advanced manufacturing, the US could rebuild industrial capacity it lost — a historically significant possibility. - **Military advantage.** AI-enhanced ISR, autonomy, targeting, cyber, and logistics could widen the gap in a conventional conflict. - **Normative and commercial reach.** American AI companies are exporting AI infrastructure and services worldwide, embedding US technical standards and, not incidentally, US surveillance capacity. **Mechanisms that erode it:** - **Diffusion.** AI is a general-purpose technology that lowers barriers for surveillance, censorship, cyber operations, and propaganda. Deepfakes and cheap open-weight models empower non-state actors and authoritarian governments alike. - **China's closing the model gap.** Chinese labs are now competitive on frontier performance at a fraction of the training cost. Compute controls slow this; they also create strong incentives for indigenous alternatives. - **Scale favors the adversary in a long war.** AI may advantage the networked, high-tech US in a short conflict, but in a prolonged contest, China's manufacturing and industrial depth could matter more. - **The Global South vector.** Whoever supplies cheap, capable AI to developing countries gains influence there. China has been doing this aggressively with open models and infrastructure deals. - **Soft-power erosion.** AI-generated media undermines the informational advantage the US has long held. ## What to watch The decisive variable is not who invents AI first but **who retains compute, talent, and alliance advantages over a decade**. Concretely: whether US export controls hold without triggering successful decoupling; whether the US keeps attracting and retaining researchers; whether allies stay aligned on compute and standards; whether AI actually reshores US industry; and whether China achieves credible semiconductor self-sufficiency. ## Bottom line The US is a leading power with real, durable advantages — not an unchallenged hegemon. AI is likely to make the next five to ten years *more* American-led than the last, because compute, capital, and talent are concentrated there. But over a longer horizon, AI is a classic power-diffusion technology, and the more capable and cheap it becomes, the more it advantages China, other states, and non-state actors. The likeliest outcome is not a new American hegemony but a **more technologically capable multipolarity in which the US remains first among equals — for now.**

Short answer: **partly agree, with important qualifications.** The US is best described as a **preponderant power with hegemonic features**, not a full-spectrum global hegemon. AI could **reinforce** that position—or **dilute** it—depending on how compute, chips, talent, capital, alliances, and governance evolve. ### Is the US a hegemonic power? In the classic sense—material preponderance plus institutional leadership plus legitimacy—the US scores high but unevenly: - **Strong hegemonic features:** dollar centrality, military reach and alliance system, global tech ecosystem, top universities, capital markets, semiconductor design, AI labs, and control over key chokepoints like advanced chips and export controls. - **Weaker or contested features:** it cannot dictate global trade, climate, or development outcomes; China and regional powers constrain it; its legitimacy is contested; domestic polarization limits credibility; and “hegemony” is often used loosely to mean “most powerful,” not “globally dominant.” - **Better label:** the US is the **leading power in a partially multipolar order**, with hegemonic advantages in finance, security, and technology but not omnipotence. Yes — with important qualifications. The United States is still best described as a **hegemonic or leading power**, but not an all-powerful one. It remains the most influential state in several core areas of global power: military reach, alliance networks, financial dominance, technology ecosystems, higher education, cultural influence, and the ability to shape international rules and institutions. But its hegemony is more **contested, uneven, and constrained** than it was in the immediate post–Cold War period. ## Why the US still looks hegemonic If “hegemony” means not just being powerful, but being able to shape the structure of the international system, then the US still qualifies in several ways: 1. **Military power** The US still has the most globally capable military, with worldwide bases, power-projection assets, naval dominance, advanced air and missile capabilities, and deep experience in joint operations. 2. **Alliance system** NATO, US-Japan, US-South Korea, US-Australia, and other partnerships give the US a network of influence that no rival currently matches. 3. **Financial power** The dollar remains the world’s dominant reserve currency and the main currency of global trade and finance. This gives the US extraordinary leverage through sanctions, capital markets, and global payment systems. 4. **Technology and innovation** The US remains central to semiconductors, cloud computing, artificial intelligence, software platforms, venture capital, and advanced research. 5. **Institutional influence** Even when international institutions are imperfect or contested, the US still has outsized influence in bodies such as the IMF, World Bank, WTO, and many security and standards-setting organizations. So yes: the US is still a hegemonic power in the sense that it remains the most structurally powerful state in the world. ## But it is not unipolar in a simple sense The US is not a global empire, and its power has limits. - China is a major economic, industrial, and technological rival. - Russia remains a nuclear power and can disrupt regional and global security. - The Global South increasingly resists being forced into US-led or China-led blocs. - US domestic polarization weakens its ability to sustain consistent global leadership. - Many countries benefit from US security or markets while also cooperating with China or other powers. - International legitimacy is harder to command when US foreign policy is seen as inconsistent or self-serving. So the better description may be: **the US is the leading power in a contested, partially multipolar system.** It is not that the US is no longer dominant. It is that dominance is now more conditional, more expensive, and more challenged. --- # Will AI change that? AI could change the balance of power, but not in a simple or predetermined way. AI is a **general-purpose technology**, like electricity, computing, or the internet. It can affect economic productivity, military capability, intelligence, governance, surveillance, cyber power, industrial policy, and soft power. But whether it strengthens or weakens US hegemony depends on how it is developed, governed, and diffused. There are several possibilities. --- ## 1. AI could strengthen US hegemony The US has major advantages in AI: - leading private AI labs and technology firms; - deep capital markets; - world-class universities and research institutions; - strong cloud infrastructure; - leadership in chip design and advanced semiconductors; - a large ecosystem of startups, investors, and talent; - alliances with key technology producers and users. If the US can maintain leadership in AI models, chips, data infrastructure, energy, talent, and standards, AI could reinforce its global position. AI could strengthen US power by: - increasing economic productivity; - improving military decision-making and logistics; - enhancing intelligence and cyber capabilities; - strengthening surveillance and internal security tools; - creating new exportable technologies and platforms; - giving US firms influence over global digital infrastructure; - allowing the US to shape international AI norms and standards. In this scenario, AI becomes another layer of US structural power. --- ## 2. AI could also erode US hegemony AI could also reduce US advantages. First, AI may **lower barriers to entry** in some areas. Open-source models, cheaper compute, and better tooling could allow other countries, firms, or even non-state actors to develop powerful capabilities without needing the same scale as the US. Second, AI could **shift power toward states with strong industrial policy**. China, for example, has advantages in manufacturing, state coordination, data collection, infrastructure deployment, and strategic investment. If AI becomes more about industrial application than pure research, China could gain ground. Third, AI could **weaken the value of some traditional US advantages**. For example: - If AI reduces the importance of labor cost advantages, some manufacturing may return to advanced economies, but it may also make production more flexible and less dependent on traditional supply chains. - If AI improves autonomous systems, smaller states or non-state actors may gain asymmetric capabilities. - If AI accelerates cyber warfare, weaker states may be able to impose costs on stronger ones. - If AI enables better surveillance and social control, authoritarian regimes may become more stable and more competitive. Fourth, AI could **undermine US legitimacy** if it is used for mass surveillance, repression, misinformation, or coercive digital control. Hegemony is not only about power; it also depends on perceived legitimacy. If the US is seen as using AI to dominate rather than to provide public goods, its influence may weaken. --- ## 3. AI may create new forms of power AI could change what “hegemony” even means. Traditional hegemony has been based on: - military power; - economic size; - financial control; - alliance networks; - cultural influence; - institutional leadership. AI may add new sources of power: - control over advanced chips and chip manufacturing equipment; - control over cloud and data-center infrastructure; - control over foundation models and AI platforms; - access to high-quality data; - energy capacity for AI training and inference; - talent in AI research and engineering; - ability to integrate AI into industry, government, and military systems; - influence over AI standards, safety rules, and governance norms. In other words, future hegemony may depend less on owning territory and more on controlling **compute, data, models, chips, energy, and standards**. The US is well positioned in many of these areas, but not all. --- ## 4. AI may not produce a single AI hegemon It is also possible that AI does not create a new unipolar moment. Instead, it may produce a world of **competing AI ecosystems**. For example: - a US-led ecosystem emphasizing private innovation, capital markets, and allied coordination; - a China-led ecosystem emphasizing state direction, industrial deployment, and alternative digital infrastructure; - an EU ecosystem emphasizing regulation, privacy, and industrial sovereignty; - other regional powers such as India, Japan, South Korea, Saudi Arabia, or the UAE developing their own AI capabilities or becoming important nodes in global AI supply chains. In that world, the US may remain the leading power, but not the sole hegemon. --- ## 5. AI could also weaken states in general AI does not only affect relations between great powers. It may also weaken the monopoly of states over information, security, and economic organization. AI could empower: - corporations; - hacker groups; - criminal networks; - extremist movements; - open-source developer communities; - decentralized platforms; - individuals with access to powerful tools. This could make global politics more fragmented. Even if the US remains the strongest state, it may find it harder to control information flows, cyber operations, financial systems, or emerging technologies. So AI may not simply make the US more hegemonic. It may make the entire international system more complex, faster-moving, and harder to govern. --- # The key question: will AI reinforce US strengths or expose its weaknesses? AI will likely amplify existing trends. If the US can: - maintain leadership in advanced chips and AI infrastructure; - attract global talent; - invest in energy and grid capacity; - coordinate with allies on export controls and standards; - regulate AI without stifling innovation; - maintain political stability and institutional credibility; - use AI in ways that enhance security without undermining rights; then AI could strengthen US hegemony. But if the US: - becomes politically polarized; - loses access to global talent; - fails to solve energy and infrastructure constraints; - overrelies on coercion rather than cooperation; - allows rivals to dominate AI deployment in the Global South; - suffers major AI-related security failures; - loses legitimacy because of surveillance, misinformation, or inconsistent foreign policy; then AI could accelerate relative decline. --- # My view I agree that the US is still a hegemonic power, but it is a **contested hegemon** in a more multipolar world. AI will probably not by itself decide whether the US remains dominant. Instead, AI will act as a **power amplifier**. It may strengthen the US if the country can lead in AI infrastructure, innovation, governance, and alliances. But it may also help rivals, especially China, if AI becomes more about industrial deployment, state coordination, and strategic autonomy. The most likely outcome is not a simple “US wins” or “US loses” scenario. It is more likely that AI will make global power more **technological, infrastructural, and contested**. The US may remain the leading power, but its hegemony will increasingly depend on its ability to control and govern AI systems, not just its traditional military and economic strength. In short: **The US is still hegemonic, but AI may make hegemony harder to maintain and more dependent on technology, energy, talent, and governance.**

Whether the United States is a “hegemon” depends on which dimension of power you emphasize, and AI’s impact is likely to be **contingent and uneven** rather than a simple transfer of dominance. ### Is the US hegemonic today? **Arguments that it is:** - **Military:** The US maintains global force projection, alliance networks (NATO, bilateral treaties in Asia), and technological edges in areas like stealth, carriers, and space. - **Economic/financial:** The dollar remains the dominant reserve currency; US capital markets are the deepest; sanctions leverage is substantial. - **Institutional:** The US helped design and still heavily influences the UN, IMF, World Bank, and WTO. - **Technological:** Leading in AI, semiconductors, biotech, and aerospace (though with important caveats). **Arguments that it is not (or is declining):** - **Multipolarity:** China is the largest trading partner for most countries; the EU is a regulatory superpower; the Global South is increasingly non-aligned. - **Relative economic weight:** US share of global GDP has fallen from ~40% post-WWII to roughly a quarter today. - **Soft power erosion:** Polarization, perceived inconsistency in foreign policy, and domestic political dysfunction have reduced attractiveness. - **Limits of coercion:** Sanctions have accelerated de-dollarization efforts and alternative payment systems; military interventions have had mixed results. **A common synthesis:** The US is still the **single most powerful state** in a system that is **no longer unipolar**. It is a “hegemon in decline” or a “primus inter pares” rather than an uncontested hegemon. ### Will AI change that? AI could **reinforce** US advantages or **accelerate diffusion** of power—likely both, in different domains. **Ways AI might bolster US position:** - **Compute and capital concentration:** Frontier AI requires massive capital, energy, and advanced chips. The US (and allies like Taiwan, Netherlands, Japan) controls key chokepoints in the semiconductor supply chain. - **Military applications:** AI-enabled targeting, logistics, and autonomous systems could widen the gap with near-peer competitors if integrated faster. - **Alliance leverage:** The US can use AI export controls and cloud infrastructure as tools of statecraft, binding partners into its tech ecosystem. - **Innovation ecosystem:** Venture capital, research universities, and tech giants create a self-reinforcing cycle. **Ways AI might erode or redistribute power:** - **Diffusion of capability:** Open-source models, cheaper inference, and algorithmic advances may lower barriers. A state or non-state actor with less capital could achieve “good enough” AI for cyber, propaganda, or economic disruption. - **Economic disruption:** If AI automates cognitive work, countries with large service sectors (including the US) face adjustment costs; manufacturing-heavy economies might adapt differently. - **Governance fragmentation:** If the US pushes a “small yard, high fence” approach, others may build parallel AI stacks (China’s ecosystem, EU regulatory bloc), reducing US centrality. - **Soft power and legitimacy:** If AI is perceived as amplifying surveillance, bias, or inequality, it could damage US credibility—especially if competitors frame themselves as more “responsible” or “sovereign.” **Key uncertainties:** - **Energy and infrastructure:** AI scaling depends on electricity and data centers. Countries with cheap energy (Gulf states, parts of China) could gain leverage. - **Talent mobility:** Immigration policy and research openness matter. Restrictions could push talent to competitors. - **Regulatory competition:** The EU’s AI Act, China’s state-directed model, and US light-touch approach create competing governance templates. The winner may shape global standards. ### Bottom line The US is best described as a **leading but contested power** in a multipolar system. AI is unlikely to restore unipolar hegemony, but it could **extend US advantages in high-end compute and military tech** while simultaneously **democratizing lower-tier capabilities** that empower rivals and non-state actors. The outcome depends less on who invents the best model and more on **who controls the physical infrastructure, sets the rules, and adapts institutions fastest**. The US is often described as a hegemonic power because hegemony in international relations has a specific meaning - not just being the strongest country, but being strong enough across multiple domains to shape the rules of the international system itself. By that definition, there is a strong case that the US has been hegemonic since the end of the Cold War, and in many ways still is, though that position is more contested now than it was 20-30 years ago. Here is how it breaks down: ### 1. Why the US is called hegemonic Hegemony is usually measured in 4 areas: **1. Military:** The US spends more on defense than the next several countries combined, has ~750+ overseas bases in 80+ countries, 11 aircraft carrier strike groups, and leads NATO, the world's most powerful military alliance. No other country can project force globally at the same scale. **2. Economic and Financial:** The US is ~25% of global GDP, home to the world's reserve currency. About 58% of global foreign exchange reserves are held in dollars, and most global trade, especially oil and finance, is dollar-denominated. That gives the US unique leverage through sanctions and control over institutions like the IMF and World Bank, which it helped create. **3. Institutional and Diplomatic:** The US was the primary architect of the post-WWII order - UN, NATO, IMF, World Bank, WTO. It has a network of ~50+ formal treaty allies in Europe and Asia, which no rival can match. **4. Technological and Cultural:** US companies dominate in key sectors: software, cloud, semiconductors design, aerospace, biotech, and AI. US universities dominate global rankings, and American soft power - from Hollywood to English as the lingua franca of business and science - remains extensive. For those reasons, scholars often call the US a hegemon, or at least a *primus inter pares* - first among equals. **The counter-argument:** Many scholars now argue we are moving from unipolar hegemony to multipolarity. China has overtaken the US in manufacturing output and PPP-adjusted GDP, is the largest trading partner for most countries, and is a near-peer in military power in the Indo-Pacific. Russia, India, Turkey, Brazil, Iran, and others increasingly act independently of Washington. The US also faces domestic constraints - debt, polarization, and war-weariness after Iraq and Afghanistan - that limit its ability and willingness to act as a global enforcer. So a common view today is: the US remains the single most powerful state and still sets many global rules, but it can no longer dictate outcomes everywhere as it could in the 1990s. ### 2. Will AI change that? AI is unlikely to end US hegemony on its own, but it is likely to be a major determinant of whether US hegemony persists, erodes, or transforms. Think of AI as a force multiplier across all four pillars above: **Where AI could reinforce US advantage:** 1. **Military:** AI for intelligence analysis, drone swarms, cyber operations, logistics, and command-and-control could widen the US military-technological gap, similar to how nuclear weapons and precision-guided munitions did in the past. 2. **Economic:** The US currently leads in the AI stack - frontier models (OpenAI, Google, Anthropic, Meta), cloud infrastructure (AWS, Microsoft, Google), AI chip design (Nvidia, AMD), and venture capital. If AI drives a new productivity boom, the US is best positioned to capture it first. 3. **Institutional:** Whoever sets standards for AI safety, chip export controls, and data governance will shape the next generation of global rules. The US is actively trying to do this through export controls on advanced chips to China and alliances like the US-EU Trade and Technology Council. **Where AI could undermine US hegemony:** 1. **Diffusion is fast:** Unlike nuclear weapons or aircraft carriers, AI models can be copied, open-sourced, and run relatively cheaply. China, and even smaller states or non-state actors, can leverage open models to close gaps quickly. 2. **China as a peer competitor:** China leads in AI deployment at scale - surveillance, facial recognition, smart cities, manufacturing automation - and has massive data pools, strong state support, and leading companies like Huawei, ByteDance, and DeepSeek. If China achieves a breakthrough in chips or AGI first, it could shift the balance. 3. **New vulnerabilities:** AI lowers the cost of cyberattacks, disinformation at scale, and autonomous weapons. That could empower weaker adversaries to asymmetrically challenge US power without matching it conventionally. 4. **Energy and compute bottleneck:** Advanced AI requires enormous amounts of electricity and compute. US advantage depends on maintaining leads in energy production, semiconductor manufacturing, and supply chains for rare earths - all areas where it faces competition. ### Bottom line 1. **Is the US hegemonic now?** Yes, in the sense that it is still the only power with global reach across military, economic, financial, technological, and alliance dimensions. But it is a weaker, more contested hegemony than in the unipolar moment of 1991-2015. 2. **Will AI change that?** AI will not automatically create or destroy hegemony, but it will amplify the trajectory that is already underway. If the US maintains its lead in talent, compute, capital, and energy, and can translate AI into military and economic power while managing alliances, AI could extend its hegemony for another generation. If it falls behind in deployment, loses control of the chip supply chain, or if AI diffuses so rapidly that it empowers rivals more than the leader, AI could accelerate the shift to a bipolar US-China or truly multipolar world. Most analysts see AI as the central technological competition of the next 20 years for exactly this reason - similar to naval power in the 19th century or air and nuclear power in the 20th.

Whether the United States is a “hegemonic power” depends on how you define the term, but it is common in international relations scholarship and journalism to describe the U.S. as the world’s preeminent power—even if its ability to shape outcomes is increasingly contested. ### The case for U.S. hegemony (or near-hegemony) In a structural sense, the U.S. still holds a combination of advantages that no other country matches: * **Military:** The U.S. maintains the world’s largest defense budget, a global network of bases, and dominant naval and air capabilities. No other power can project force globally at the same scale. * **Economic:** The U.S. dollar remains the primary global reserve currency, and American capital markets are the deepest and most liquid. U.S. technology firms and platforms also dominate global digital infrastructure. * **Alliances:** Through NATO, bilateral security pacts in Asia, and other partnerships, the U.S. is at the center of the world’s most extensive alliance system. * **Institutional and cultural influence:** American universities, entertainment, and technology continue to shape global norms, even as the influence of institutions like the IMF and World Bank has waned. ### The case against strict hegemony Many scholars argue that the post–Cold War “unipolar moment” is over. The U.S. faces significant constraints: * **Rising rivals:** China’s economic scale, military modernization, and diplomatic initiatives (such as the Belt and Road Initiative) challenge U.S. dominance in Asia and beyond. * **Multipolarity:** Regional powers—India, Brazil, Turkey, Saudi Arabia, and others—are increasingly pursuing independent foreign policies rather than aligning automatically with Washington. * **Limits of power:** The U.S. has struggled to achieve its objectives in conflicts such as Iraq and Afghanistan, and it cannot always compel allies or adversaries to follow its lead (for example, on issues like the war in Gaza or the pace of aid to Ukraine). So a more precise description might be that the U.S. remains the **preeminent** power in a **non-unipolar** or **multiplex** world: it is still first among unequals, but no longer able to unilaterally set the global agenda. --- ### Will AI change that? Artificial intelligence is a general-purpose technology—like electricity or the internet—so its effect on U.S. hegemony is not predetermined. It could reinforce American primacy, erode it, or simply accelerate existing trends. Much depends on how the U.S., its allies, and its rivals develop, deploy, and govern AI. **Ways AI could reinforce U.S. position:** * **Innovation and talent:** If the U.S. continues to lead in AI research, semiconductor design, and AI infrastructure, it could widen its economic and military lead. * **Military integration:** AI-enhanced intelligence, autonomous systems, and decision-support tools could amplify the effectiveness of U.S. forces and alliance interoperability. * **Standard-setting:** If the U.S. and like-minded democracies shape global norms for AI safety, export controls, and ethical use, they could extend structural power into the digital domain. **Ways AI could erode U.S. position:** * **Diffusion:** AI models and techniques are easier to copy than aircraft carriers. Open-weight models, chip smuggling, and talent migration could allow rivals (especially China) to catch up quickly in specific applications. * **Empowering smaller actors:** AI-driven cyber tools, drones, and surveillance systems could let middle powers or non-state actors punch above their weight, reducing the U.S. advantage in conventional force projection. * **Economic disruption:** If AI productivity gains are captured more effectively by state-directed economies, or if the U.S. fails to adapt its workforce and institutions, its relative economic weight could shrink. * **Governance failure:** If the U.S. cannot regulate AI effectively or loses the standards race to the EU (“Brussels effect”) or China, it may cede influence over the rules governing the next technological era. ### Bottom line The United States is widely described as a hegemonic—or at least preeminent—power, but that status is more contested now than at any time since the end of the Cold War. AI is unlikely to be a single, decisive switch that either preserves or ends U.S. hegemony. Instead, it is a force multiplier: it could entrench American advantages if the U.S. leads in innovation and allied coordination, or it could accelerate a shift toward multipolarity if the technology diffuses widely and rivals integrate it more effectively. The outcome will depend less on the technology itself than on the policy choices made in Washington, Beijing, Brussels, and other capitals over the coming decade.

It is accurate to say that the United States is **often described** as a hegemonic power, especially in international relations (IR) and media discourse. However, whether that label still fits—and what artificial intelligence might do to it—depends on how you define “hegemony” and which indicators you prioritize. ### 1. Is the U.S. still a hegemonic power? In strict IR terms, **hegemony** implies more than simply being the strongest state. It suggests the ability to shape the rules, norms, institutions, and economic order of the international system, and to get other states to align with those preferences—often through a mix of coercion, consent, and structural power. By that standard, the case for U.S. hegemony is mixed: * **Sources of enduring preeminence:** The U.S. still accounts for a disproportionate share of global military spending, maintains a worldwide network of alliances and bases, issues the world’s primary reserve currency (the dollar), and hosts many of the leading technology platforms, universities, and financial markets. In these dimensions, it remains closer to a “unipolar” or “primate” power than any other state. * **Signs of relative decline and contestation:** China’s economic scale, Russia’s military assertiveness, the growing weight of India and the European Union, and the rise of “middle powers” have made the system more multipolar. U.S. ability to unilaterally set the global agenda has been tested by wars in Iraq and Afghanistan, financial crises, political polarization, and the difficulty of building consensus on climate, trade, and security. So, many scholars prefer terms like **“primacy,” “unipolarity (fading),”** or **“multiplex order”** rather than pure hegemony. The U.S. is often *called* hegemonic because it is still the most powerful single actor, but its power is increasingly constrained and contested. ### 2. Will AI change that? AI is a **general-purpose technology**—like electricity or the internet—so its effect on hegemony is not predetermined. It could reinforce U.S. dominance, erode it, or transform the very nature of power. Several dynamics matter: **Ways AI could reinforce U.S. hegemony** * **First-mover and ecosystem advantages:** The U.S. currently leads in AI research, talent, semiconductor design, cloud infrastructure, and private-sector innovation. If that lead persists, AI could amplify existing advantages in military intelligence, precision strike, logistics, and economic productivity. * **Military integration:** AI-enabled command-and-control, autonomous systems, and faster decision cycles could widen the gap between the U.S. military and most rivals, at least in the near term. * **Financial and technological standards:** If U.S. firms and agencies set the technical and safety standards for AI, they could extend structural power into digital governance much as the dollar and English-language platforms do today. **Ways AI could erode U.S. hegemony** * **Diffusion and lower barriers:** Unlike nuclear weapons or aircraft carriers, AI models and tools can be copied and deployed relatively cheaply. China is a near-peer in AI research, data, and state-directed application. Other states (and even non-state actors) could use AI to punch above their weight in cyber operations, disinformation, drone warfare, or surveillance. * **Asymmetric empowerment:** AI may empower smaller states, terrorist groups, or criminal networks to challenge great powers in the information domain or through autonomous weapons, reducing the U.S. monopoly on high-end force projection. * **Domestic vulnerability:** If AI causes significant labor displacement, inequality, or political instability within the U.S., it could undermine the domestic consensus needed to sustain global leadership. **Ways AI could transform the concept of hegemony itself** * AI may accelerate the speed of conflict and decision-making to the point where traditional alliance management and diplomatic signaling become harder to control. * It could shift power from states to private technology companies that own the models, data, and compute—making “hegemony” less about Washington and more about a handful of AI labs. ### Bottom line Calling the U.S. a hegemonic power is defensible, but increasingly incomplete; it is better described as the most powerful state in a contested, multipolarizing order. **AI will not automatically preserve or end that position.** It is a force multiplier: it could extend U.S. primacy if the U.S. leads in responsible development, integration, and governance, or it could accelerate relative decline if diffusion outpaces American adaptation or if rivals and non-state actors exploit the technology more effectively. The outcome will depend on policy choices, investment, and international cooperation—not on the technology alone.

Other states can resist US pressure, and allies do not automatically follow Washington. American power also varies by domain: it is more dominant in global finance than in manufacturing. So I would describe the present order as **US-led but increasingly contested**, rather than either fully unipolar or evenly multipolar. The term “hegemony” is useful if it captures that unevenness—not if it implies that Washington gets whatever it wants. ## Will AI change that? Probably—but there are three plausible directions. **1. AI strengthens US leadership.** The US has major advantages in frontier AI companies, investment, research, and access to talent. If AI produces large productivity gains and improves scientific discovery, intelligence analysis, and military logistics, those advantages could compound. Alliances also matter: the advanced semiconductor supply chain spans several US partners. **2. AI narrows the gap between the US and competitors.** Leading models are not the only source of value. Cheaper, widely available systems could let other countries adopt capabilities without paying the full cost of inventing them. China’s industrial scale could help it integrate AI into manufacturing and physical systems. Smaller powers could gain disproportionate leverage in areas such as cyber operations. **3. AI changes what power consists of.** Control over computing infrastructure, electricity, chips, cloud services, and technical standards could become more strategically important. Private technology firms might acquire greater geopolitical influence, although they would remain dependent on states for infrastructure, security, and legal permission to operate. ## My assessment **In the near term, AI is more likely to reinforce existing concentrations of power than overturn them**, because advanced development requires substantial capital, infrastructure, and expertise. That benefits the US, though it does not guarantee US dominance. Over a longer horizon, the decisive question is **deployment, not just invention**: which societies use AI effectively across their economies, maintain reliable institutions, secure energy supplies, and manage disruption. A country can lead in model development without capturing most of the resulting gains. AI will therefore change the basis and instruments of hegemony—but it will not, by itself, determine who holds it. Policy choices, alliances, and domestic governance will remain crucial.
