**Is the US a hegemonic power?** Yes, by most definitions, the United States is currently the world's primary hegemonic power. It holds dominant influence across several dimensions: - **Military:** The US has the largest and most technologically advanced military, with global power projection capabilities (bases, carrier groups, logistics) that no other country matches. - **Economic:** The US has the largest nominal GDP, the dominant reserve currency (the US dollar), and leading positions in global finance and trade. - **Institutional:** The US shaped the post-WWII international order — the UN, IMF, World Bank, NATO — and continues to wield outsized influence in these institutions. - **Cultural:** American media, technology platforms, and higher education attract talent worldwide. That said, "hegemonic" is a contested label. Some scholars argue the US is in relative decline, and that China's economic and military rise is challenging US primacy. The consensus view is that the US remains the dominant power, but the gap is narrowing. **Will AI change that?** AI is a transformative technology, and it will likely reshape global power dynamics — but whether it strengthens or weakens US hegemony is an open question: **Arguments AI could strengthen US dominance:** - The US leads in AI research, talent, and venture capital. Companies like OpenAI, Google DeepMind, and Meta are at the forefront. - AI could enhance US military capabilities (intelligence, logistics, autonomous systems), maintaining a qualitative edge. - The dollar's role as the reserve currency could be reinforced if AI-driven financial systems increase demand for dollar-denominated transactions. **Arguments AI could erode US dominance:** - China has made aggressive AI investments and may close the gap quickly, especially in applications like surveillance, manufacturing automation, and military AI. - AI could enable "small" powers to punch above their weight — a country with strong AI research and manufacturing (e.g., Israel, South Korea, or even certain European nations) could become a critical node in global AI supply chains. - AI could democratize certain capabilities, reducing the advantage of traditional military and economic power. - If AI-driven productivity gains boost the US economy, the US could grow faster than competitors, reinforcing hegemony. **The honest answer:** AI will almost certainly change the distribution of power, but whether it makes the US more or less hegemonic depends on many variables — investment, immigration policy (to attract AI talent), international cooperation or competition, and how other powers respond. The most likely scenario is a transition from a unipolar moment (US hegemony) toward a more multipolar order, with AI as one of the key technologies shaping that transition. The US is unlikely to lose its primacy quickly, but it will face increasing competition from China and others in the AI race, which will gradually erode the asymmetry that defined post-Cold War unipolarity.

The question of whether the United States is currently a hegemonic power—and how Artificial Intelligence (AI) will alter that status—is one of the most fiercely debated topics in modern geopolitics. Here is a breakdown of the current state of US hegemony and how AI is poised to reshape the global order. --- ### Part 1: Is the US currently a hegemonic power? **The short answer:** Yes, the US remains the world’s preeminent power, **but its hegemony is no longer absolute.** It is shifting from the "unipolar moment" of the 1990s (when it had no peer competitors) to a more contested, multipolar, or "asymmetrical bipolar" world. In international relations, hegemony means having the power to shape the rules of the international system and the ability to coerce or persuade other nations to follow them. The US still holds this power in several key areas, though it is eroding in others: **Where US Hegemony Remains Strong:** * **Military:** The US spends more on defense than the next 10 countries combined. It maintains a global network of roughly 750 military bases in 80 countries and commands the world’s oceans. * **Finance:** The US dollar remains the world’s primary reserve currency. About 88% of all foreign exchange transactions involve the dollar. Because the US controls the financial plumbing of the world, it can levy devastating sanctions on rogue states. * **Alliances:** The US leads a vast network of wealthy allies (NATO, Japan, South Korea, Australia). In contrast, its primary rivals (China and Russia) have very few true allies and rely mostly on transactional partnerships. * **Technology and Soft Power:** From Hollywood to Silicon Valley, US cultural and technological influence remains the global default. **Where US Hegemony is Eroding:** * **The Rise of China:** Economically, diplomatically, and militarily, China has become a "peer competitor." Measured by Purchasing Power Parity (PPP), China’s economy is already larger than the US's, and it is the top trading partner for over 120 countries. * **The Assertive "Global South":** Middle powers like India, Brazil, Saudi Arabia, and Turkey are increasingly refusing to take sides, hedging their bets between the US and China to maximize their own interests. * **Domestic Polarization:** Political dysfunction and a growing isolationist streak within the US make allies question the long-term reliability of American leadership. **Conclusion on Current Status:** The US is a "wounded hegemon" or a "primus inter pares" (first among equals). It cannot dictate global outcomes the way it did in 1995, but no other country—including China—yet has the combined military, financial, and diplomatic reach to replace it. --- ### Part 2: Will AI change that? **Yes, profoundly.** AI is what military strategists and economists call a "General Purpose Technology" (like the steam engine, electricity, or the internet). It will be the primary determinant of national power in the 21st century. AI has the potential to either **cement US hegemony for another generation** or **accelerate its decline.** Here is how: #### Scenario A: AI Cements US Hegemony (The "Super-Unipolar" Outcome) Right now, the US holds a significant lead in the AI race. If it maintains this lead, the gap between the US and the rest of the world could widen dramatically. * **Control of the "AI Stack":** The US currently dominates the foundational layers of AI. American companies (OpenAI, Google, Anthropic, Meta) build the best models, and an American company (Nvidia) designs the vast majority of the advanced chips required to run them. * **Economic Productivity:** If AI sparks a massive productivity boom, it could help the US outgrow its massive national debt and outpace China, which is currently facing a severe demographic decline (a shrinking workforce) and a real estate crisis. * **Chokepoint Diplomacy:** The US is already using export controls to deny China access to advanced semiconductors and the lithography machines (made by ASML in the Netherlands) needed to make them. If the US successfully starves China of compute power, the US could achieve an insurmountable technological lead. #### Scenario B: AI Erodes US Hegemony (The Great Equalizer) Conversely, AI could undermine the traditional pillars of US strength in several ways: * **Asymmetric Warfare:** The US relies on expensive, exquisite military hardware (like $13 billion aircraft carriers). AI enables the creation of cheap, autonomous drone swarms, cyber-weapons, and hypersonic missiles. This lowers the cost of lethal force, allowing smaller nations (like Iran or North Korea) or non-state actors to successfully challenge the US military. * **China’s Data and Implementation Advantage:** While the US leads in cutting-edge AI research, China excels at *implementation* and state-directed mobilization. China has a massive population, fewer privacy laws (meaning more data to train AI), and a government willing to pour limitless subsidies into domestic chip-making to bypass US sanctions. * **The Open-Source Wildcard:** Because many powerful AI models (like Meta's Llama) are open-source, the US cannot easily hoard the technology. Rivals can take American open-source models and fine-tune them for their own military or economic use at a fraction of the cost. * **Erosion of the Social Fabric:** As an open democracy, the US is uniquely vulnerable to AI-driven misinformation, deepfakes, and algorithmic polarization. Rivals can use AI to wage cognitive warfare on the US electorate, paralyzing the country from within. #### A Third Twist: The Rise of "Techno-Polar" Hegemony Historian Ian Bremmer argues that AI might not shift power from the US to China, but rather **from nation-states to tech companies.** The resources required to build frontier AI—hundreds of billions of dollars, massive data centers, and gigawatts of energy—are concentrated in the hands of a few US tech giants (Microsoft, Google, Amazon, Meta). In the future, the US government may find itself dependent on—and negotiating with—these quasi-sovereign corporate entities to maintain its global power. ### Summary The US is currently a hegemon in transition, fighting to maintain its lead in a fragmenting world. **AI is the ultimate accelerant in this contest.** Because the US currently controls the talent, the algorithms, and the hardware design, AI is currently *extending* American hegemony. However, because AI lowers the barrier for asymmetric warfare and cyber disruption, it will make the world much harder for the US to police and control.

Meta: Muse Wonderreleased 2026-10-12 · captured 2026-11-14 · 1,772 tokens (55 in / 1,717 out) · $0.0346 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
Yes — with an important qualification. The US is still a hegemonic power, but it's no longer the *uncontested* hegemon it was in the 1990s. And AI is likely to both strengthen that position in the short term, and undermine it in the long term. ### 1. What "hegemon" actually means Hegemony isn't just being the strongest country. It's the ability to set the rules of the system that others operate within. By that definition, the US still qualifies in four areas: **1. Military:** It's the only country with a truly global military — 11 carrier groups, ~750 overseas bases, and alliance networks in Europe (NATO) and Asia (Japan, South Korea, Australia, Philippines) that no rival can match. **2. Economic / Financial:** The dollar is ~58% of global foreign reserves and used in ~88% of foreign exchange transactions. That gives the US unique power through sanctions, SWIFT, and the Federal Reserve. Wall Street and US capital markets are still where the world raises money. **3. Technological:** US firms dominate cloud computing (Amazon, Microsoft, Google), operating systems, chip design (Nvidia, AMD, Apple), and until recently, almost all frontier research. **4. Institutional / Cultural:** The post-1945 order — UN, IMF, World Bank, WTO — was built by the US, in English, around liberal market norms. US universities, media, and tech platforms still export values and talent pipelines. Where the qualification comes in: This is *relative* decline, not absolute collapse. In 2000 the US was ~30% of global GDP and China was ~3.6%. Today it's roughly 26% vs 17% (nominal, much closer on PPP). China is now the largest trading partner for over 120 countries, leads in manufacturing output, EVs, batteries, and 5G infrastructure, and has built parallel institutions like BRICS and the Belt and Road. So we’ve moved from a unipolar moment (1991-~2010) to something like a contested hegemony — US-led, but with a real peer competitor for the first time since the USSR. ### 2. Will AI change that? AI is a general-purpose technology like electricity or the internet, but with a crucial difference: it directly amplifies economic productivity, military capability, scientific discovery, and information control all at once. Who leads in AI will have leverage in almost every other domain. Right now, AI is *reinforcing* US hegemony for three reasons: **Compute:** Frontier AI runs on Nvidia GPUs, designed in the US, and on US hyperscale cloud. The US export controls on advanced chips to China (started 2022) are a classic hegemonic tool — using a chokepoint to slow a rival. **Capital and Talent:** The US private sector is investing hundreds of billions in data centers and models. OpenAI, Anthropic, Google DeepMind, Meta — plus top universities — still attract a disproportionate share of global AI researchers, many of them immigrants. **Ecosystem:** AI doesn't exist alone. It needs advanced chips (US design + Taiwan fabrication + Dutch ASML machines), energy, data, and software stacks. The US and its allies control most of that chain. But there are three forces pushing the other way: **1. Diffusion is built into AI.** Unlike nuclear weapons, AI models can be copied. Open-weight models from Meta (Llama) and Chinese firms like DeepSeek and Alibaba (Qwen) mean a capability that costs billions to train can be downloaded for free months later. That empowers middle powers — India, UAE, Saudi Arabia, France — and even non-state actors, weakening any monopoly. **2. China is not far behind, and plays a different game.** China has less access to top chips, but has massive state coordination, huge amounts of industrial and surveillance data, cheaper energy for data centers, and a strategy of embedding AI into manufacturing, logistics, and the military very fast. DeepSeek showed in 2025 that algorithmic efficiency can partly offset a hardware disadvantage. **3. AI creates new dependencies that hurt the US.** The US depends on Taiwan for >90% of the most advanced chip fabrication. It faces domestic bottlenecks in electricity, permitting for data centers, and political fights over regulation and AI safety. Hegemony requires not just inventing tech, but deploying it across society — China and the Gulf states may deploy faster in some sectors because they face fewer legal/political constraints. ### Three plausible scenarios **A) AI extends US hegemony (next 5-10 years most likely):** US firms maintain a 1-2 year lead in frontier models, chip controls work, allies adopt the US AI stack, and AI boosts US productivity and military intelligence/cyber/autonomy enough to widen the gap. **B) AI accelerates bipolarity:** The world splits into a US AI sphere and a China AI sphere — different chips, models, data rules, and standards — much like the internet is already splitting. Neither is a global hegemon, both are regional/technological hegemons. **C) AI erodes state hegemony itself:** If open-source AGI-level systems become widely available, power diffuses downward to companies, small states, and individuals. In that world, Microsoft or a future lab might matter more than many governments, and the very idea of a single state hegemon becomes outdated. My take: We're heading for A in the short term, drifting toward B in the medium term. The US will likely remain the *first among equals* because of its alliances, dollar, and AI/cloud/chip ecosystem — that combination is incredibly hard to replicate. But AI makes hegemony more fragile than industrial or nuclear power did. It moves too fast, copies too easily, and depends on supply chains no one country fully controls. The US may stay on top, but it will have to actively compete to set AI rules — on safety, chips, data, and military use — rather than having others automatically follow, which is what true hegemony used to look like.

Whether the United States is currently a “hegemonic” power depends on how you define hegemony and which metrics you prioritize. Most scholars would say the U.S. remains the world’s preeminent power, but that its dominance is more contested and less comprehensive than it was in the 1990s. **The case for continued U.S. hegemony** * **Military:** The U.S. maintains a global network of alliances and bases, and its defense budget exceeds that of the next several countries combined. No other state can project conventional military force on a comparable scale. * **Economic and financial:** The dollar remains the dominant global reserve currency, U.S. capital markets are the deepest and most liquid, and American firms lead in high-value sectors from semiconductors to enterprise software. * **Institutional and technological:** The U.S. sits at the center of the post-1945 institutional order (IMF, World Bank, NATO) and hosts the world’s leading research universities and venture-capital ecosystem. **The case for relative decline** * **Economic diffusion:** China is now the world’s largest manufacturing economy and a peer competitor in many technologies. The U.S. share of global GDP has fallen from its post-WWII peak. * **Institutional fragmentation:** Many states in the Global South are increasingly unwilling to align automatically with Washington, and alternative institutions (BRICS+, AIIB) offer competing venues. * **Domestic constraints:** Political polarization, infrastructure decay, and fiscal pressures raise questions about long-term capacity to sustain global commitments. **Will AI change this?** AI is unlikely to *by itself* overturn the current order, but it will almost certainly **intensify the competition** and reshape the *basis* of power. * **It could reinforce U.S. leadership.** The U.S. currently dominates the AI stack: frontier model development, advanced chip design (NVIDIA, AMD), cloud infrastructure, and elite AI talent. If AI becomes the general-purpose technology of the 21st century—as electricity or the internet did—American advantages in compute, data, and capital could compound. * **It could also diffuse power.** AI lowers the cost of sophisticated capabilities. Smaller states, non-state actors, and private firms can leverage powerful models without building massive indigenous research ecosystems. In security terms, AI-enabled swarms, autonomous systems, and cyber tools could erode the conventional military advantages that underpin U.S. hegemony. * **It creates new vulnerabilities.** Heavy reliance on complex, fragile AI supply chains (rare earths, advanced lithography, energy-intensive data centers) introduces new choke points. A state that leads in AI research but depends on foreign hardware or energy could find its leverage limited. **Bottom line** The U.S. is best described today as a **hegemonic power in a contested, multipolarizing system**—still first among unequals, but with a narrower margin than before. AI will not automatically preserve or destroy that position. Instead, it will become a central arena of geopolitical rivalry. If the U.S. can maintain its edge in AI infrastructure, talent, and governance while addressing domestic vulnerabilities, AI may extend its primacy. If it cannot, AI could accelerate the diffusion of power to rivals and non-state actors, making the international order more fragmented and unpredictable.

The answer depends on how you define hegemony, and reasonable scholars disagree. **The case that the US is hegemonic:** - **Military reach:** It spends more than the next several countries combined, maintains bases worldwide, and has no peer in power projection. - **Financial power:** The dollar dominates trade invoicing, reserves, and global finance, and US sanctions can cut countries off from the system. - **Institutions and alliances:** NATO, the Japan and Korea alliances, and the post-1945 economic architecture still anchor much of the international order. - **Technology and culture:** Most leading tech platforms, frontier AI labs, and top universities are American, and English and US culture have wide reach. **The case that it's declining or no longer fully hegemonic:** - China's economy is larger than the US in purchasing power terms and leads in manufacturing output and many green and industrial technologies. - Iraq, Afghanistan, and the difficulty of deterring or defeating regional powers suggest limits on what US force can achieve. - Many countries pursue hedging strategies and multi-alignment rather than choosing sides, and BRICS and other groupings are growing. - Domestic polarization and fiscal strain weaken the consent-based side of hegemony that scholars like Gramsci and Keohane emphasized. This is a form of soft power that could be contested. Whether the US keeps its position will depend less on AI itself than on domestic politics, the ability to build energy and industrial capacity, and whether it keeps allies and talent.

Whether the US remains a "hegemon" depends on how you define the term, but most international relations scholars would say it currently holds **contested or partial hegemony** rather than the uncontested dominance it enjoyed in the 1990s. And yes, AI is likely to be one of the most decisive variables in whether that position is reinforced, transformed, or eroded over the next decade. Here is how to think about both questions: ### 1. Is the US still hegemonic today? In the realist sense—military preponderance, control of sea lanes, the global reserve currency, and the ability to set the rules of the international system—the US remains the strongest single power. No other state can project force globally at scale, and the dollar still anchors roughly 60% of global reserves. The US also leads in the key technologies of the last era (semiconductors, aerospace, biotech) and hosts the deepest capital markets. However, hegemony is also about **legitimacy and institutional leadership**. Here the picture is more mixed. The US faces a rising China that challenges it economically and technologically, a more assertive Global South that refuses to align automatically with Washington, and internal polarization that undermines its ability to offer a coherent alternative model. Many analysts now describe the current order as **"multipolar"** or **"polycentric"** rather than unipolar, with the US as the "first among equals" rather than a true hegemon. ### 2. Will AI change that? AI will not simply "end" or "preserve" US hegemony; it will likely **reshape its form**. The direction depends on several contested factors: **Factors that could reinforce US dominance:** * **Compute and chips:** The US currently controls the advanced semiconductor supply chain (via TSMC, NVIDIA, and export controls on China). If AI progress remains gated by access to cutting-edge GPUs and training clusters, the US and its allies (Taiwan, South Korea, Japan, the Netherlands) hold a structural chokepoint. * **Talent and research:** US universities and companies still attract the world’s top AI researchers, and American firms (OpenAI, Anthropic, Google DeepMind, Meta) lead in frontier model development. * **Alliances and standards:** Washington is building "techno-democratic" coalitions (the Chip 4 alliance, AUKUS, the EU-US Trade and Technology Council) to set global AI governance standards, potentially locking in a US-led bloc. **Factors that could erode or diffuse it:** * **Diffusion of capabilities:** AI is a general-purpose technology. Open-weight models (like Meta’s Llama or Chinese models such as DeepSeek) lower the barrier to entry, allowing mid-sized powers and even non-state actors to acquire sophisticated capabilities without massive compute budgets. * **China’s parallel ecosystem:** Beijing is investing heavily in indigenous AI, and its vast data sets, manufacturing base, and state-directed coordination could allow it to leapfrog in applied AI (robotics, surveillance, military logistics) even if it lags in frontier models. * **Economic disruption:** AI could accelerate deindustrialization in the US or disrupt its service-sector dominance, while boosting manufacturing-heavy economies. It may also destabilize the dollar’s role if AI-driven productivity gains are captured by other economies first. * **New vulnerabilities:** AI enables autonomous weapons, hyper-targeted disinformation, and cyber-offense at scale. This could empower challengers (Russia, Iran, North Korea) to punch above their weight, eroding the security umbrella that underpins US hegemony. ### The likely outcome Rather than a simple yes/no, AI is probably pushing the world toward a **"bifurcated" or "fragmented" hegemony**: * A **US-led AI bloc** (North America, Europe, Japan, South Korea, Australia, India) controlling frontier research, advanced chips, and military AI. * A **China-led AI sphere** (Russia, parts of the Global South, some Gulf states) using open-source and indigenous models for surveillance, industrial automation, and military applications. * **Swing states** (Brazil, Indonesia, Saudi Arabia, Turkey) that leverage competition between the two to extract technology and investment. In this scenario, the US may remain the hegemon *within its bloc*—setting the rules for AI safety, data governance, and military use—but it will no longer be the sole arbiter of the global system. Alternatively, if the US maintains a decisive lead in artificial general intelligence (AGI) or autonomous systems, it could use that to reassert a more traditional form of dominance, much as nuclear weapons did in 1945. **Bottom line:** The US is still the hegemonic power, but increasingly a *contested* one. AI will likely determine whether that contest resolves into a durable US-led order, a bipolar standoff with China, or a more anarchic multipolar system where no single power can set the rules. The next 5–10 years—particularly who controls advanced compute and who deploys AI militarily first—will be decisive.

Yes—with an important qualification: **the US is still the leading global power, but its hegemony is uneven and contested, not absolute.** AI could reinforce that position or erode it; it is more likely to reshape the sources of power than automatically produce a new hegemon. ## Is the US a hegemon? A hegemon is not just the largest economy or strongest military. It is a state with an exceptional ability to **set rules, organize coalitions, and influence the choices available to others**. By that definition, the US has a strong claim: - **Military reach:** global force projection and an extensive alliance network. - **Financial power:** the dollar’s central role in reserves, trade, and borrowing, alongside deep capital markets. - **Technological influence:** leading firms, research institutions, and important positions in computing and software. - **Institutional and cultural influence:** substantial power over international arrangements and a worldwide cultural footprint. But “hegemon” can mislead if it implies control. China is a major industrial, technological, and regional military competitor. Other states can resist US preferences, and Washington cannot reliably dictate political outcomes abroad. Europe has significant regulatory power, while countries such as India pursue substantial strategic autonomy. So I would describe the present as **US primacy within an increasingly contested international order**—rather than either uncontested American dominance or an already fully multipolar world. ## Will AI change that? Probably, but the direction is not predetermined. There are three main mechanisms. ### 1. AI could strengthen US primacy The US has major advantages in frontier AI companies, capital, cloud infrastructure, and talent attraction. Critical parts of the semiconductor ecosystem also sit in allied or partner countries. If powerful AI remains expensive and dependent on scarce chips, enormous computing facilities, and concentrated expertise, those advantages could compound. AI-driven productivity, scientific discovery, and military capabilities could increase US power. But that depends on preserving alliances, attracting talent, building energy infrastructure, and translating technical progress into broad economic gains. ### 2. AI could diffuse power If capable AI becomes cheap, widely available, and easy to adapt, more countries and smaller organizations will gain access to capabilities previously reserved for large institutions. That could lower barriers to research, software development, intelligence analysis, and some military applications. It could also make cyberattacks, surveillance, and influence operations more accessible. **Diffusion of capability, however, does not necessarily mean equality of power.** A widely available AI system may still be much more valuable to a country with strong manufacturing, abundant electricity, good institutions, and a skilled workforce. ### 3. AI could change what hegemony consists of Control over computing infrastructure, chip supply chains, cloud platforms, and technical standards may become more important. Private firms could gain geopolitical leverage, too. Yet AI will not make existing advantages disappear. Geography, industrial capacity, energy, military logistics, financial credibility, and alliances will still matter. Owning the best model is not the same as being able to organize the international system. ## My assessment **In the near term, AI is more likely to reinforce existing concentrations of power than overturn them.** The US is well positioned, but China is a serious competitor, and neither country is technologically self-sufficient. Over the longer term, the decisive question is not simply *who develops the smartest AI?* It is **who can deploy AI effectively across their economy and state institutions—and convert that capability into durable influence and willing cooperation from others.**

Yes—with an important qualification: **the US is still the world’s leading power, but it is not an uncontested hegemon.** AI could strengthen that position or erode it; it is more likely to reshape the sources of power than automatically hand leadership to another country. ## Is the US a hegemon? A hegemon does more than possess the largest military or economy. It also helps set the rules, provides international public goods, and can persuade—or pressure—other states to operate within an order it favors. The US still has an exceptional combination of advantages: - **Military reach:** global power projection and an extensive network of alliances and bases. - **Financial influence:** the dollar’s central international role, deep capital markets, and leverage over parts of the global financial system. - **Technological strength:** leading firms, research institutions, investment networks, and the ability to attract talent. - **Institutional and cultural influence:** substantial influence over international institutions, standards, education, and media. Other countries may gain access to powerful models without needing to reproduce the full cost of developing them. If capable AI becomes cheap and widely available, the benefits may diffuse rather than remain concentrated in one country. The decisive question is not simply **“Who builds the best model?”** It is **“Who converts AI into sustained economic and strategic capacity?”** That requires reliable energy, chips, infrastructure, skilled workers, effective institutions, and organizations able to adopt the technology. Leadership in benchmarks alone does not establish geopolitical leadership. AI could also give private companies and infrastructure providers greater bargaining power, while making smaller states more capable in areas such as cyber operations and intelligence. That would complicate hegemony without necessarily producing a new hegemon. **My bottom line:** AI is more likely initially to intensify US–China competition than to overturn the international order outright. Over the longer term, it could entrench American leadership, support a more bipolar system, or diffuse power more broadly. Which outcome emerges will depend as much on governance, alliances, and economic adoption as on technical breakthroughs.

**Yes—with qualifications.** The US is still the strongest candidate for a global hegemon, but “hegemony” should not be confused with the ability to dictate outcomes everywhere. ### Is the US a hegemonic power? A hegemon has disproportionate power not just to win conflicts, but to **shape the rules, institutions, and incentives within which other countries operate**. The US fits that description in several important ways: - **Security:** Its military reach and alliance network remain exceptionally broad. - **Finance:** The dollar’s central role in reserves, trade, and borrowing gives Washington unusual influence. - **Technology:** US firms and research institutions occupy leading positions in many strategically important technologies. - **Institutions and culture:** American influence extends through international organizations, universities, media, and business practices. But its dominance is uneven. China is a formidable industrial and technological competitor. Other powers can resist US preferences, and even close allies pursue independent interests. American military superiority also does not reliably translate into lasting political success. So I would describe the system as **US-led, but increasingly contested**, rather than either unipolar in an absolute sense or fully multipolar. Whether “hegemony” implies legitimate leadership or coercive domination is a separate question; different countries experience American power differently. ### Will AI change that? Almost certainly—but **AI does not inherently favor either continued US dominance or its decline**. The US has major advantages across several of these, reinforced by relationships with countries central to semiconductor supply chains. If AI produces large productivity gains, those advantages could compound. 2. **Helping challengers catch up.** As capable systems become cheaper and more widely available, countries and firms may gain access to expertise that previously required enormous institutions. China also has substantial strengths in manufacturing, engineering, and deployment. Leadership in developing models need not translate into leadership in using them. 3. **Diffusing disruptive power.** AI could give smaller states and nonstate actors greater capabilities in cyber operations, intelligence, and persuasion. That might make international order harder for *any* hegemon to manage, without producing a clear successor. The decisive issue is therefore not just **who builds the best model**, but who converts AI into sustained productivity, effective institutions, military capability, and attractive partnerships. Domestic governance and the distribution of AI’s benefits will matter too. **My bottom line:** AI could initially strengthen US leadership because it rewards existing technological and financial advantages. Over time, however, it could also spread capabilities and increase instability. It is more likely to reshape the foundations of hegemony than automatically transfer it from the US to another country.

"Hegemon" is a contested label, and whether it fits depends on what you mean by it. **The case for yes** - Unmatched military reach: roughly 750 overseas bases, blue-water naval dominance, and alliance networks (NATO, Japan, South Korea, Australia) that no rival matches. - The dollar's central role in trade, reserves, and finance, which gives the US sanctions power few others have. - Leadership in technology, elite universities, capital markets, and cultural influence. **The case for no, or "not anymore"** - Hegemony in the strict sense means the ability to set the rules of the system. The US now faces constraints: China is a peer competitor in manufacturing and increasingly in technology, Russia and others openly defy US preferences, and many middle powers hedge rather than align. - The post-1945 "liberal order" was always partial. The USSR, then China, operated outside it, and the US itself has violated its norms when convenient. - Domestic polarization, debt, and policy volatility reduce the credibility of long-term US commitments. A fair summary: the US is the most powerful state and still the system's central pillar, but it is a *declining-share* hegemon, or a "primus inter pares" with less ability to dictate outcomes than in the 1990s. Scholars genuinely disagree. Some, like Stephen Brooks and William Wohlforth, argue US primacy remains durable. Others, like Mearsheimer, say we're in a multipolar or bipolar world. **Will AI change that?** Possibly, but the direction is uncertain. That's the "winner-take-most" scenario, though many doubt it will be that clean. - If AI mainly raises productivity broadly, it could reinforce existing advantages in wealthy, innovative economies, or it could reward fast adopters regardless of who invented the tech. - Disruption of labor markets could strain domestic politics in any country, including the US. - Non-state actors (large tech firms) may hold power that doesn't map neatly onto state hegemony. My overall view: AI is more likely to intensify great-power competition than to cement a single hegemon, because capabilities spread quickly and the main rival is capable. The US will probably remain a leading power, but whether it keeps its dominant position depends less on AI itself than on how well it maintains alliances, energy and chip supply, immigration of talent, and political stability. The United States is widely regarded as a **hegemonic power** due to its dominant economic, military, cultural, and institutional influence globally. This status emerged after World War II and was solidified during the Cold War, with the U.S. shaping international norms, alliances (e.g., NATO), and institutions like the IMF and World Bank. However, the 21st century has seen challenges to this unipolar order, particularly from China’s rapid economic and military rise, as well as regional powers like Russia. Many analysts now describe the world as transitioning toward **multipolarity**, where multiple states compete for influence. ### Will AI Change This? AI’s impact on global power dynamics is multifaceted and depends on several factors: 1. **Economic Competitiveness**: - AI could amplify the advantages of technologically advanced economies. The U.S. currently leads in AI research, venture capital, and tech innovation (e.g., Silicon Valley). If it maintains this edge, AI may reinforce its economic hegemony. - However, China is aggressively investing in AI, aiming to become a global leader by 2030. If China closes the gap or surpasses the U.S., it could erode American economic dominance. 2. **Military and Security**: - AI-driven advancements in autonomous weapons, cyberwarfare, and intelligence gathering could enhance the military prowess of leading nations. The U.S. already invests heavily in AI for defense, but rivals like China and Russia are doing the same. A breakthrough by another power might disrupt the balance. 3. **Global Governance and Norms**: - The U.S. has historically set standards for technology and data governance. If it fails to adapt to AI ethics, privacy, or regulation, other nations (e.g., the EU with GDPR) might shape global norms instead, diluting U.S. influence. 4. **Inequality and Power Distribution**: - AI could widen gaps between "haves" (advanced economies) and "have-nots," potentially consolidating power among early adopters. Alternatively, open-source AI tools might democratize access, empowering smaller states or non-state actors. 5. **Geopolitical Risks**: - Misuse of AI (e.g., disinformation campaigns, cyberattacks) could destabilize international relations, creating opportunities for rivals to exploit vulnerabilities. ### Conclusion: The U.S. remains a hegemonic power, but its dominance is no longer uncontested. AI could either **reinforce U.S. leadership** if it sustains its technological edge or **accelerate a shift toward multipolarity** if competitors like China outpace it. The outcome hinges on strategic investments, ethical governance, and the ability to navigate geopolitical tensions. While AI alone won’t dismantle U.S. hegemony, it will be a critical factor in reshaping global power structures in the coming decades.

Qwen: Qwen3.8 Max Primereleased 2026-09-23 · captured 2026-11-14 · 913 tokens (70 in / 843 out) · $0.0104 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
# US Hegemony and AI ## Is the US a hegemonic power? The label is defensible but contested, depending on what dimension you emphasize: **Arguments for:** - Military spending exceeds the next ~10 countries combined, with a global network of bases - The dollar remains the dominant reserve currency, giving the US unique financial leverage (sanctions, seigniorage) - Institutional architecture (IMF, World Bank, NATO) still largely reflects US preferences - Cultural and technological reach (platforms, universities, entertainment) remains unmatched **Arguments against or for qualification:** - The US cannot unilaterally impose outcomes the way a true hegemon might (failed to shape outcomes in Iraq, Afghanistan, Syria, Ukraine) - China's economic rise, the BRICS expansion, and de-dollarization efforts signal erosion - Domestic polarization undermines the credibility and consistency needed to sustain hegemony - Some scholars (e.g., Mearsheimer, Acharya) argue we're already in a multipolar or "multiplex" world A fair summary: the US is the *most powerful single state* but exercises less uncontested dominance than the term "hegemon" implies. It's a **declining or contested hegemon**, depending on your framework. ## Will AI change this? AI is unlikely to simply reinforce or simply undermine US primacy. It will likely do both simultaneously: **Ways AI could reinforce US power:** - The US currently leads in frontier AI research, talent concentration, and private investment - Military applications (autonomous systems, intelligence analysis, cyber) could widen gaps - Productivity gains could reinvigorate economic growth **Ways AI could erode or redistribute power:** - China is a serious competitor in AI and has advantages in data scale and state-directed deployment - AI lowers barriers for smaller actors (cyber capabilities, disinformation, drone warfare), enabling asymmetric challenges - If AI commoditizes certain advantages (e.g., code, analysis, logistics), it may reduce the premium on scale - Dependence on AI could create new vulnerabilities (adversarial attacks, supply chain risks) **The deeper question:** AI may change *what power is* more than it changes *who has it*. If economic and military advantage increasingly flow from computational capacity, data access, and algorithmic sophistication, the traditional pillars of hegemony (territory, population, industrial base) become less decisive. That could favor the US and China while marginalizing middle powers—or it could create a more fragmented landscape. ## Bottom line AI is more likely to **reshape the terms of competition** than to produce a clean transfer of hegemony. The US will probably remain a leading power, but the gap between it and rivals may narrow, and the nature of dominance itself will look different.
AionLabs: Aion 3.5 Minireleased 2026-09-23 · captured 2026-11-14 · 1,295 tokens (34 in / 1,261 out) · $0.001789 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
Yes, I'd broadly agree that the US is currently the world's dominant power, though "hegemonic" deserves some unpacking, because American dominance is strong in some domains and eroding in others. **Where US hegemony is real:** - **Military**: The US has unmatched global power projection — carrier strike groups, hundreds of overseas bases, and alliances (NATO, bilateral pacts in Asia) no rival comes close to replicating. - **Financial**: The dollar dominates reserves, trade invoicing, and debt markets. This gives the US extraordinary leverage — the ability to sanction almost anyone effectively — because so much of the world's finance runs through institutions it can reach. - **Institutional and ideological**: The postwar order (IMF, World Bank, WTO, SWIFT) was largely built on American terms, and English remains the global lingua franca of business, science, and the internet. - **Technology**: US firms lead in software, cloud computing, biotech, and — critically for your question — frontier AI. **Where it's contested:** - **Manufacturing**: China has overtaken the US as the world's industrial core; the US depends on Asian supply chains for everything from semiconductors to pharmaceuticals. - **Relative economic weight**: The US share of global GDP has fallen from ~40% in 1960 to roughly 25% today. - **Legitimacy**: The Iraq War, the 2008 financial crisis, and inconsistent application of its own rules have weakened the "soft" side of hegemony. De-dollarization efforts (BRICS, bilateral swap arrangements) are marginal but symbolically significant. So: hegemonic, yes — but a *contested* hegemony, more like early relative decline than unipolar dominance. **Will AI change that?** It's one of the biggest variables, and it cuts both ways: **How AI could reinforce US hegemony:** - The frontier labs (OpenAI, Anthropic, Google DeepMind, Meta) are American, funded by American capital, drawing on American research institutions. - The US controls the chokepoints: chip *design* (NVIDIA, AMD), the software stack, and — via export controls — the ability to deny rivals access to advanced compute. - If AI delivers large productivity gains, the US economy could re-accelerate relative to rivals, widening the gap rather than narrowing it. - Military applications (autonomous systems, ISR, decision-support) could extend the US qualitative military edge. **How AI could erode it:** - **Fabrication dependency**: The most advanced chips are made in Taiwan by TSMC. A Chinese move on Taiwan, or even its mere plausibility, exposes the fragility of the entire US AI enterprise. - **Diffusion**: Open-source models and the general diffusiveness of software technology mean frontier advantages may be short-lived. China's DeepSeek demonstrated that export controls slow but don't stop progress, and that efficiency can substitute for brute-force compute. - **China's structural advantages**: State-directed capital, massive data pools, a huge STEM workforce, and integration of AI with the world's largest manufacturing base. The US leads in *frontier models*; China may lead in *deployment*. - **Internal effects**: AI-driven labor disruption, concentration of wealth in a handful of firms, and AI-amplified disinformation could deepen the internal polarization that is arguably the biggest threat to US power — hegemony historically collapses from internal decay more often than external challenge. - **Cheap AI as an equalizer**: If AI lets middle powers automate capabilities that once required US-scale economies (intelligence analysis, cyber operations, propaganda), the barriers that sustained unipolarity weaken. **My overall take:** AI is unlikely to *end* US hegemony on its own, but it will likely determine *what kind* of hegemony survives. The most plausible scenarios are: 1. **AI entrenches US primacy** — if frontier advantages compound and diffusion stays slow. 2. **A bipolar AI order** — US and Chinese spheres with incompatible stacks, standards, and ideologies, with the rest of the world forced to align. 3. **Hollowing out** — the US retains nominal leadership while internal dysfunction and the diffusion of AI capabilities make that leadership increasingly nominal. The honest answer is that the race is genuinely open, and the outcome depends less on the technology itself than on choices about governance, alliance management, industrial policy, and whether the US can get out of its own way domestically. Hegemony is rarely lost to a single rival; it's usually lost to a combination of overextension, internal decay, and a rival that plays the long game better. AI raises the stakes of all three.
AionLabs: Aion 3.5released 2026-09-23 · captured 2026-11-14 · 1,645 tokens (34 in / 1,611 out) · $0.009768 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
Short answer: yes, mostly, though the label fits less comfortably than it did in the 1990s. And AI is likely to reinforce US primacy in the near term while introducing genuine long-term uncertainties. **Is the US a hegemon?** By most measurable criteria, yes: - **Military**: The US spends more on defense than the next several countries combined, has a global basing network (~750 bases in 80+ countries), and can project force anywhere on earth. No rival comes close. - **Economic/financial**: The dollar remains the dominant reserve currency (~60% of global reserves), SWIFT and US banking infrastructure give Washington unprecedented sanctions power (see: Russia 2022), and US capital markets are the deepest in the world. - **Institutional**: The US still anchors the IMF, World Bank, NATO, and many other postwar institutions, even as their legitimacy erodes. - **Technological**: US firms dominate the internet stack, and now AI. But the picture is messier than "hegemon" implies. The US lost wars in Iraq and Afghanistan despite overwhelming power. China is now a genuine peer competitor in trade, manufacturing, and increasingly technology. Domestic polarization makes US commitments unreliable — allies now hedge. And the Global South increasingly refuses to pick sides. So: a hegemon in decline? Or a primus inter pares in an emerging multipolar order? Reasonable people disagree. **Will AI change that?** AI cuts both ways, but the near-term effect is probably *reinforcing*: 1. **The US leads, decisively.** The frontier models (OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, Meta) are American. The critical hardware (Nvidia chips, TSMC fabrication, ASML lithography) sits in a US-aligned chokepoint chain. Washington has already weaponized this — the 2022 export controls on advanced chips to China were arguably the first shot in an AI cold war. 2. **AI compounds existing advantages.** Hegemony has always been about network effects — alliances, standards, platforms. AI supercharges the returns to being the incumbent platform power. 3. **But diffusion is real.** Open-weight models, algorithmic efficiency gains, and China's own capable models (DeepSeek being the obvious example) mean the frontier advantage may not be durable. If AI becomes a commodity like electricity, US leverage shrinks. 4. **The wildcard: AI as a disruptor of the hegemon's own foundations.** If AI drives massive productivity gains, the winners are whoever deploys it fastest at scale — which could favor manufacturing-heavy states or authoritarian systems that can restructure labor markets without political friction. And if AI destabilizes labor markets globally, the political backlash could hit the US's already fragile domestic consensus hardest. **My honest take**: AI is more likely to extend US hegemony by a decade or two than to end it — the compute, talent, and capital moats are deep. But hegemony rarely ends because a rival overtakes the hegemon in a straight line. It ends through overextension, internal decay, and unexpected shocks. AI could plausibly supply all three. The US is simultaneously the country best positioned to win the AI race and the country with the most to lose if AI destabilizes the global order it built. **Short answer:** Many scholars describe the United States as a *hegemonic* power today, but the term is contested and the future of that status could be reshaped by a range of factors—including artificial intelligence (AI). Whether AI will *change* that status depends on how the technology evolves, how it is governed, and how other actors respond. --- ## 1. What “hegemonic power” usually means | Dimension | Typical indicators | How the U.S. scores (according to many analyses) | |-----------|-------------------|-------------------------------------------------| | **Military** | Global force projection, alliances, defense spending | Largest defense budget, extensive network of bases, NATO leadership | | **Economic** | GDP, trade, financial influence, technology leadership | World’s largest economy; dollar is the primary reserve currency; dominant in high‑tech sectors | | **Political/Soft power** | Cultural exports, diplomatic reach, institutional influence | Global cultural influence (Hollywood, internet), UN Security Council seat, many multilateral institutions | | **Ideological** | Norms, values, governance model | Liberal democratic model promoted worldwide; “American way of life” as a cultural export | A *hegemonic* power is often defined as a state that can shape the international system’s rules, norms, and institutions in ways that benefit its interests, while also being able to enforce compliance through a mix of coercion (military), inducement (economic), and attraction (soft power). By that definition, many scholars argue the U.S. currently holds a hegemonic position, though some debate the extent and durability of that dominance. --- ## 2. Counter‑vailing perspectives - **Multipolarity arguments:** Some analysts contend that the world is already moving toward a more multipolar distribution of power, with China, the European Union, India, and others gaining economic and military clout. - **Relative decline:** Others point to U.S. domestic challenges (political polarization, fiscal deficits, infrastructure gaps) and argue that these could erode its ability to sustain hegemonic influence over the long term. - **Non‑state actors:** Transnational corporations, NGOs, and even criminal networks increasingly shape global outcomes, complicating the traditional state‑centric view of hegemony. --- ## 3. How AI could affect the U.S. hegemonic position ### 3.1 Potential reinforcing effects | Area | How AI could reinforce U.S. advantages | |------|----------------------------------------| | **Military** | AI‑enabled autonomous systems, cyber‑warfare capabilities, and decision‑support tools could widen the technological gap between the U.S. and many rivals. | | **Economic** | AI could boost productivity in high‑value sectors (finance, biotech, advanced manufacturing), potentially widening the U.S. lead in GDP per capita. | | **Soft power** | AI‑driven media, language models, and cultural platforms could amplify U.S. narratives and values globally. | | **Governance** | AI tools for public services, health, and education could improve quality of life, reinforcing the attractiveness of the U.S. model. | ### 3.2 Potential destabilizing effects | Area | How AI could challenge or dilute U.S. hegemony | |------|-----------------------------------------------| | **Technology diffusion** | Open‑source AI models and rapid adoption by other nations could erode the U.S. monopoly on cutting‑edge tech, spreading capabilities to China, Russia, the EU, and others. | | **Economic competition** | Countries that invest heavily in AI (e.g., China’s “AI Superpower” strategy) could overtake the U.S. in key industries, reducing its trade surplus and financial leverage. | | **Geopolitical fragmentation** | AI could enable new forms of economic and informational sovereignty (e.g., localized AI services, digital currencies), weakening the centrality of U.S.‑led institutions. | | **Domestic disruption** | If AI exacerbates inequality, job displacement, or political polarization in the U.S., it could undermine the legitimacy of the American model abroad. | | **Governance challenges** | The U.S. may struggle to regulate AI in ways that balance innovation, privacy, and security, potentially creating policy vacuums that other actors fill. | ### 3.3 The role of governance and international cooperation - **Regulation:** If the U.S. leads in setting global AI standards (e.g., safety, data privacy, export controls), it could reinforce its influence. Conversely, fragmented or competing regulatory regimes could dilute it. - **Alliances:** AI research and development are increasingly collaborative. The U.S. ability to maintain strong alliances (e.g., NATO, Quad) could amplify its AI edge, while rival blocs could develop competing ecosystems. - **Arms race dynamics:** Autonomous weapons and AI‑driven cyber capabilities could spark a new arms race, potentially destabilizing existing power balances. --- ## 4. What scholars and policymakers are saying - **Realist IR scholars** (e.g., Kenneth Waltz, John Mearsheimer) tend to view power as material (military, economic) and argue that AI will be just another tool that amplifies existing capabilities. In that view, the U.S. will likely retain its hegemonic status unless its material power erodes. - **Liberal scholars** (e.g., Joseph Nye, Anne-Marie Slaughter) emphasize soft power and institutional influence. They argue that AI could either enhance U.S. soft power (if used responsibly) or undermine it (if perceived as surveillance or manipulation). - **Technology‑focused analysts** (e.g., those at the Center for a New American Security, Brookings Institution) warn that the *pace* of AI adoption and governance will be decisive. If the U.S. fails to regulate AI effectively or if other states develop competing AI ecosystems, the U.S. could lose its “first‑mover” advantage. --- ## 5. Bottom line: Uncertainty, not inevitability - **Current consensus:** Most observers agree the U.S. is a hegemonic power *today*, but the degree of that hegemony is debated. - **AI’s impact:** AI could *reinforce* U.S. dominance if the country leads in technology, governance, and alliance coordination. Conversely, AI could *accelerate* a shift toward a more multipolar or even non‑state‑centric world if the technology spreads rapidly, if other states develop superior AI capabilities, or if U.S. domestic challenges intensify. - **Key variables to watch:** 1. **Speed and openness of AI diffusion** (open‑source vs. closed‑source). 2. **U.S. policy on AI regulation, export controls, and investment.** 3. **China’s and the EU’s AI strategies and their ability to create alternative ecosystems.** 4. **U.S. domestic political and economic stability.** 5. **International cooperation on AI safety and standards.** Because these variables are still evolving, any definitive prediction about AI *changing* U.S. hegemony would be speculative. The most prudent view is that AI will be a **significant factor** in the future distribution of global power, but its exact direction will depend on the interplay of technology, policy, and geopolitics.

It depends on what "hegemony" means, and that's where most of the disagreement lies. ## Is the US a hegemon? **The case for yes:** - **Military reach:** The US spends roughly as much on defense as the next nine or ten countries combined, operates around 750 overseas bases, and is the only country that can project power globally on a sustained basis. - **Financial centrality:** The dollar dominates reserves (~58%), trade invoicing, and debt markets. Control over dollar clearing lets the US impose sanctions that others struggle to match. - **Alliance network:** NATO, Japan, South Korea, Australia, and others give the US an alliance system no rival comes close to. China has essentially one treaty ally (North Korea). - **Institutional and cultural influence:** Much of the postwar order (IMF, World Bank, many trade norms) was US-designed, and American tech platforms, universities, and media remain globally dominant. **The case for "not anymore" or "only partially":** - **China's rise:** China leads in manufacturing output (about 30% of the global total), shipbuilding, and many supply chains, and is competitive in parts of AI, batteries, EVs, and solar. On purchasing-power-parity GDP, it is already larger than the US. - **Limits of power:** Iraq, Afghanistan, and the inability to prevent Russia's invasion of Ukraine or stop sanctions evasion suggest dominance doesn't translate into control. - **Fragmentation:** BRICS expansion, hedging by "middle powers" like India, Turkey, Saudi Arabia, and Brazil, and regional powers acting independently point toward multipolarity. - **Domestic constraints:** Polarization and shifting commitments to alliances and free trade raise questions about US willingness, not just capacity, to lead. **A reasonable synthesis:** Many scholars would say the US is still the single most powerful state across the most dimensions, but the "unipolar moment" of the 1990s–2000s is over. "Hegemony" in the sense of setting and enforcing global rules largely unchallenged is weaker than it was. "Primacy," meaning being first among others, is a more defensible description. Some call the current system "unbalanced multipolarity" or a US–China bipolarity with other significant players. ## Will AI change it? Plausible paths point in different directions: **AI could reinforce US primacy:** - The US currently leads in frontier models, top AI talent, private investment, and especially advanced chips through Nvidia and allied chokepoints like TSMC, ASML, and Samsung. - Export controls aim to lock in that advantage. - If AI produces large productivity or military gains and the leader compounds its lead, a durable US advantage is possible. **AI could erode it:** - China has shown it can produce competitive models (e.g., DeepSeek) at lower cost despite chip restrictions, and has advantages in deployment scale, energy buildout, and industrial integration. - Open-weight models and the falling cost of capability could diffuse power to many states and even non-state actors. - AI might reward manufacturing and robotics capacity, where China is strong, as much as frontier research. - Cheap autonomous systems (drones, cyber tools) could weaken expensive conventional advantages like carriers, as Ukraine has partly shown. **Key uncertainties:** - **How transformative AI turns out to be.** If gains are incremental, AI is one factor among many. If it drives explosive growth or decisive military advantages, whoever leads could gain outsized power. - **Whether leads are durable.** Historically, tech advantages diffuse, but compute and energy bottlenecks might make this time different. - **Energy and infrastructure.** AI is power-hungry, and China is adding electricity capacity far faster than the US. - **Governance and risk.** Accidents, misuse, or arms-race dynamics could reshape the picture in unpredictable ways. ## Bottom line Calling the US the world's leading power is well supported. Calling it an unchallenged hegemon is increasingly contested. AI is probably the biggest wildcard: it could entrench US leadership if American advantages in chips and frontier research compound, or accelerate multipolarity if capabilities diffuse and industrial scale matters most. Serious analysts disagree on which is more likely. The answer will likely turn on policy choices (export controls, immigration, energy, alliances) as much as on the technology itself. # US Hegemony and the AI Question ## Is the US a Hegemon? In many conventional measures, yes — and this is widely acknowledged across the political science spectrum: - **Military**: Global basing network, unmatched power projection, larger defense budget than the next several nations combined - **Financial**: Dollar as the world's reserve currency, control over SWIFT, dominance of capital markets - **Institutional**: Vetoes in the UN Security Council, disproportionate influence in the IMF, World Bank, and other Bretton Woods institutions - **Technological & Cultural**: Home to most of the world's leading tech firms, soft power through media and education, English as the global lingua franca That said, the *degree* and *durability* of that hegemony is genuinely debated. China's rise, multipolar trends, debatable outcomes of military interventions (Iraq, Afghanistan), and relative US GDP decline have all led some scholars to question whether we've already moved past peak hegemony. ## How AI Could Change the Picture This is where it gets complex, because AI cuts in **both directions**: **AI as a hegemony *reinforcer*:** - US firms currently lead in frontier model development - The US leverages export controls on advanced chips (NVIDIA etc.) as a strategic tool - AI could supercharge military superiority, intelligence capabilities, and economic productivity - Network effects could amplify winner-take-most dynamics favoring US tech ecosystems **AI as a hegemony *challenger*:** - **China** is investing enormously in AI and holds advantages in data volume and state-directed deployment - **Open-source models** could democratize access and erode technological moats - AI-driven **economic disruption** could destabilize US social cohesion and political institutions - AI-powered **information warfare, cyber capabilities, and autonomous weapons** could level military playingfields for smaller powers - The **compute/data/talent** landscape is global and harder to control than, say, uranium enrichment - **Governance failures** (deepfakes, AI-driven inequality, misuse) could erode US soft power and the legitimacy of the liberal international order ## The Deeper Structural Question What AI likely changes most fundamentally is **what "power" even means**. Hegemony has traditionally rested on industrial capacity, military hardware, and financial institutions. AI shifts leverage toward: - **Compute and data infrastructure** - **Algorithmic sophistication** - **Talent ecosystems** - **Speed of decision-making and adaptation** A country that leads in AI doesn't just get a better weapon — it gets a potential **compounding advantage** across virtually every domain (economics, military, diplomacy, science). But this also makes the system more **volatile and unpredictable**. ## My Assessment The US is currently the most influential power in the international system — describing it as a hegemon is defensible, though "declining hegemon" or "contested hegemon" may be more accurate over time. AI is a **pivotal variable** that could either extend American primacy by a generation or accelerate its erosion — depending heavily on: 1. Whether US lead in frontier AI is sustained 2. How effectively export controls and alliances work 3. Whether AI-driven disruption destabilizes domestic politics 4. Whether global AI governance regimes emerge that either entrench or redistribute power The honest answer is that **AI introduces profound uncertainty** into hegemonic dynamics in ways we can't fully forecast yet. It's one of the most consequential strategic questions of our time.

# US Hegemony and the AI Question ## Is the US a hegemonic power? This is a genuinely debated question, and the answer depends partly on how strictly you define "hegemony." **The case for yes:** - **Military**: The US spends more on defense than the next ~10 countries combined, maintains ~750 overseas bases, and has unmatched power-projection capability. - **Economic**: The dollar remains the dominant reserve currency (~58% of global reserves), and US financial institutions are central to global capital flows. - **Institutional**: The US holds a UN Security Council veto and has outsized influence in the IMF, World Bank, and NATO. - **Cultural/soft power**: American media, universities, and values have extraordinary global reach. - **Technological**: The US leads in key frontier technologies, including AI. **The case for nuance or "no":** - The unipolar moment of the 1990s–2000s has clearly passed. China's rise, Russia's assertiveness, and the growing agency of middle powers (India, Turkey, Saudi Arabia, Brazil) point toward a more multipolar order. - Some IR scholars distinguish between **hegemony** (dominance sustained through legitimacy and consent) and **primacy** (dominance through raw capability). The US may have primacy without full hegemony — other states often comply with US preferences not because they consent to American leadership, but because the costs of resistance are high. - The dollar's reserve status, while still dominant, faces incremental erosion through de-dollarization efforts, though no credible alternative has emerged yet. - US domestic dysfunction — political polarization, infrastructure decay, fiscal challenges — may erode the foundations of its power over time. **My assessment**: The US is best described as the **leading power in a system that is becoming increasingly multipolar**. It retains hegemonic characteristics in many domains, but its margin of dominance has narrowed. Whether you call it "hegemonic" depends on whether you require unchallenged dominance or merely clear primacy. --- ## Will AI change this? This is one of the most consequential geopolitical questions of the coming decades. A smaller state or even a non-state actor with access to powerful AI could develop capabilities that previously required a great power's resources. - **China's AI push**: China is investing enormously in AI, has massive data advantages, strong state coordination, and a large STEM workforce. If China closes the gap or leads in applied AI, it could accelerate its challenge to US primacy. - **Economic disruption**: AI could disrupt the industries and financial systems that underpin US economic dominance, potentially in unpredictable ways. - **Military leveling**: If AI-enabled cyber warfare, drone swarms, or autonomous systems become cheap and widely available, the US's conventional military superiority becomes less decisive. - **Institutional disruption**: AI could enable new forms of coordination and resistance among states seeking to counterbalance US influence. ### Scenario 3: AI creates a new, starker form of hierarchy - Perhaps the most important possibility: AI doesn't simply reinforce or erode existing hegemony — it **reshapes the nature of power itself**. - Whoever controls the most advanced AI systems may gain advantages so profound (in science, military, economic planning, social control) that the gap between AI leaders and everyone else becomes qualitatively different from any previous technological gap. - **Compute infrastructure** becomes a critical strategic resource — and it's concentrated in a few countries and companies. - **Data** becomes a strategic asset — and the US and China have the most. - This could mean a **bipolar AI order** (US vs. China) with everyone else increasingly dependent on one or the other, rather than a multipolar world. ### Key uncertainties 1. **Does AI development follow a "winner-take-most" dynamic?** If yes, the US (or US-China duopoly) may consolidate power. If AI capabilities diffuse rapidly, it could empower many actors. 2. **How quickly do AI capabilities translate into real-world power?** There's a gap between impressive demos and transformative applications. The timeline matters enormously. 3. **Can the US maintain its institutional and talent advantages?** The US's greatest AI advantage may not be any single technology but its ability to attract global talent and its ecosystem of universities, companies, and capital markets. If immigration restrictions or political instability erode this, the advantage could shrink. 4. **Will AI development be centralized or decentralized?** Open-source AI models (like Meta's Llama) are already spreading capabilities widely. The tension between open and closed development will shape the geopolitical implications. 5. **How do nuclear dynamics interact with AI?** If AI makes first-strike capabilities more reliable or destabilizing, it could reshape deterrence in dangerous ways. --- ## My overall view I think AI is **more likely to reinforce US primacy in the short-to-medium term** (next 5–15 years) because the US currently has the strongest position, but **it introduces genuinely destabilizing dynamics** that could reshape the international order in less predictable ways over the longer term. The most likely near-term outcome is a **US-China AI competition** that becomes the central axis of great-power rivalry, with other states increasingly forced to choose sides or hedge. This is somewhat analogous to the US-Soviet nuclear competition, but with important differences — AI is more dual-use, more commercially driven, and potentially more diffusible than nuclear weapons.
