The question touches on two of the most discussed themes in contemporary international relations and technology policy. I'll break each part down carefully, keeping the analysis grounded in how scholars, policymakers, and technologists generally frame these dynamics. ### 1. Is the U.S. commonly described as a hegemonic power? In international relations theory, **"hegemony"** refers to a state that provides the public goods, rules, and stability that underpin the global system, often leveraging a combination of military, economic, financial, and cultural leverage. By that framework, the United States is frequently cited as the post-WWII hegemon, and many IR scholars continue to describe it that way today for several reasons: - **Economic scale and reach**: The U.S. has the world's largest nominal GDP, dominates global tech and services supply chains, and the U.S. dollar remains the primary reserve currency, giving Washington significant indirect influence over global trade and finance. - **Military footprint**: The U.S. maintains the most powerful military, a worldwide network of bases, and capabilities in power projection, nuclear deterrence, and increasingly, AI-enabled and cyber domains. - **Institutional architecture**: The U.S. is a founding and leading member of the UN, WTO, IMF, World Bank, NATO, and numerous other regimes that shape global economic and security rules. - **Soft power**: U.S. universities, media, entertainment, technology platforms, and cultural exports remain globally pervasive, shaping norms and preferences far beyond its borders. However, the label is not uncontested. Many analysts argue that we are moving toward a **multipolar** system where China's rapid growth, the EU's regulatory weight, India's demographic and economic trajectory, and the rise of the Global South collectively constrain and diffuse U.S. dominance. Others emphasize "relative decline" in some metrics while noting the U.S. still holds an absolute lead in many critical areas. The consensus in the literature is not that hegemony is monolithic or permanent, but that the U.S. remains a central, structuring power whose position is being actively negotiated rather than simply ceded. ### 2. Will AI change that? Artificial intelligence is widely regarded as a **general-purpose technology** with the potential to reshape economic productivity, military capability, and geopolitical influence. Its impact on hegemonic structures will depend on how it develops, who controls its key inputs (compute, data, talent), and how states integrate it into their institutions. Several pathways are commonly discussed: | Area | Potential Effect on U.S. Hegemony | |------|-----------------------------------| | **Economic productivity** | AI could accelerate U.S. growth if it maintains leadership in AI research, semiconductor design, cloud infrastructure, and venture capital. Conversely, widespread AI adoption could lower barriers to entry for other economies, enabling faster catch-up and reducing the U.S.'s exclusive competitive edge. | | **Military and security** | The U.S. Department of Defense and defense industry are heavily investing in AI for autonomy, ISR (intelligence, surveillance, reconnaissance), and decision support. However, China, Russia, and others are pursuing parallel military AI strategies. Shifts in combat effectiveness, deterrence calculus, or cyber capabilities could alter the military balance, but technology alone rarely rewrites decades-old alliance structures and doctrine overnight. | | **Supply chains and critical inputs** | AI chips, large-scale data centers, and specialized talent are concentrated in a few hands. If the U.S. and its allies retain dominance in these inputs, they can reinforce existing hierarchies. If other regions build sovereign compute capacity, alternative AI ecosystems could emerge, gradually diluting U.S. leverage. | | **Governance and standards** | The U.S. currently plays a leading role in shaping AI ethics, safety frameworks, and industry standards (e.g., NIST AI Risk Management Framework, industry consortia). The EU's AI Act and China's state-directed approach show that regulatory competition is already underway. Who sets the "rules of the road" for AI could become a new arena of soft-power competition. | | **Soft power and information** | AI-generated content, language models, and synthetic media can both amplify and challenge existing cultural narratives. The U.S. tech sector's global reach means American-developed AI tools are widely deployed, but local actors are rapidly building their own models, potentially diversifying the information ecosystem rather than cementing a single narrative. | **Key takeaways on the AI-hegemony relationship:** - AI is unlikely by itself to "flip" the U.S. from hegemon to non-hegemon, or the reverse. Geopolitical power is distributed across military, economic, demographic, institutional, and normative dimensions. - AI more probably acts as an **accelerator or amplifier** of existing trends: it can deepen advantages for those already leading in talent, capital, and infrastructure, while also enabling broader diffusion if access and knowledge spread. - The geopolitical impact will hinge on policy choices: export controls, investment in domestic R&D, international cooperation/standards-setting, and education/talent pipelines. A country that successfully couples AI advancement with strong institutions and inclusive growth is more likely to strengthen its

This is a thoughtful question that touches on both established international relations theory and emerging technological dynamics. I’ll break it into the two parts you raised. ### On whether the U.S. is widely regarded as a hegemonic power In international relations scholarship and contemporary geopolitical discourse, the United States is frequently described as the world’s primary **hegemon** since the end of the Cold War. This characterization rests on several pillars: - **Military presence**: A global network of bases, unmatched power-projection capabilities, and a dominant role in security alliances (e.g., NATO, Indo-Pacific partnerships). - **Economic and financial dominance**: The U.S. dollar’s status as the primary global reserve currency, deep integration of U.S. financial markets, and the country’s outsized role in global trade and investment regimes. - **Soft power**: Cultural exports (film, music, technology), educational institutions, and normative influence around liberal democracy, human rights, and market economics. - **Technological leadership**: Historically in semiconductors, cloud computing, AI research, and biotechnology. That said, the label “hegemonic power” is not without debate. Some scholars argue the U.S. faces relative decline in share of global GDP, while others point to the rise of multipolarity or the emergence of peer competitors (most notably China). The term itself is also contested—some prefer “hyperpower,” “leader,” or “first among equals,” depending on the metric. The consensus, however, is that the U.S. remains the single most influential actor in the global system, even if its unipolar dominance is increasingly qualified by structural shifts. ### On whether AI will change that status Artificial intelligence is a transformative general-purpose technology, and its impact on great-power hierarchy will depend on how it’s developed, deployed, and governed. AI could affect U.S. hegemony through several overlapping channels: **Ways AI could reinforce U.S. leadership** - The U.S. currently leads in core AI inputs: top-tier research universities, venture capital, semiconductor design (e.g., NVIDIA, AMD), cloud infrastructure, and talent attraction. - AI-driven productivity gains could strengthen U.S. economic output, military readiness (autonomous systems, ISR, logistics), and innovation ecosystems—all of which underpin hard and soft power. - Integration of AI into defense and intelligence could widen existing gaps in capabilities relative to rivals. **Ways AI could challenge or redistribute U.S. influence** - **Diffusion**: AI tools are becoming increasingly accessible. Lower barriers to entry mean non-state actors, middle powers, and rival states can acquire capabilities that once required massive industrial complexes. - **Competition**: China has made AI a national priority, with state-directed investment, massive data ecosystems, and rapid deployment in surveillance, military tech, and commercial sectors. If China achieves parity or superiority in critical AI subfields (e.g., large-scale training, chip manufacturing, standards-setting), it could erode a key pillar of U.S. hegemony. - **Economic restructuring**: AI-driven automation and efficiency gains could shift economic gravity, affecting the dollar’s reserve-currency role or the competitiveness of traditional U.S. export sectors. - **Governance and norms**: How AI is regulated globally—on privacy, military use, misinformation, and intellectual property—will shape which models of governance gain traction, potentially diminishing the export of U.S.-centric frameworks. **The broader picture** Hegemony is rarely determined by a single technology. Historical shifts (nuclear weapons, the internet, container shipping, fossil fuel transitions) have all reshaped power distributions, but established powers often adapt and maintain centrality through policy, alliance-building, and innovation cycles. AI is likely to be another such inflection point rather than a deterministic reset. Most analysts expect AI to **reshape** the distribution of power rather than simply end or cement U.S. hegemony. If the U.S. sustains its lead in R&D, standards, and responsible integration, it could cement a new form of AI-augmented leadership. If rival states close gaps or if AI enables more diffuse, networked power structures, the unipolar character of the post-Cold War era may give way to a more multipolar or polycentric order—with the U.S. still a central, but perhaps less exclusive, pole. In short: the U.S. is widely recognized as a current hegemon, and AI is a high-stakes variable that could reinforce, redistribute, or modestly challenge that status—but the outcome will hinge less on the technology alone and more on how states, markets, and institutions manage its development and governance.

The US is still the closest thing to a hegemon in the international system, but it is a hegemon in a very different form than it was in 1991-2008. **Yes, in a qualified sense.** Hegemony is not just being the strongest, it is being the dominant power in multiple domains simultaneously and having the ability to set the rules of the system. The US retains structural hegemony in: * **Military:** The US spends more on defense than the next 9 countries combined, ~$916B in FY2024. It has 11 carrier strike groups, global basing in ~750 overseas installations, and unmatched power projection and nuclear triad. No peer competitor can contest it globally. * **Financial and monetary:** The dollar is ~58% of global foreign exchange reserves and ~80% of global trade invoicing. US Treasuries are the world's safe asset. The Fed's decisions move global capital markets. * **Institutional:** The US created and still anchors the post-WWII order: IMF, World Bank, WTO, NATO, G7, SWIFT. It can veto at the UN Security Council. * **Technological and cultural:** Silicon Valley still sets global standards for AI, cloud, semiconductors design, and software. English, Hollywood, universities, and US tech platforms remain the default global public sphere. That is hegemonic. The US can still act unilaterally in ways others cannot, and other states still orient their strategies around US preferences. The qualification is that it is no longer unipolar hegemony. The US is now a *reluctant, contested hegemon*. * **Economic weight has shifted.** The US is ~26% of global GDP nominally, ~15% PPP. China is ~18% nominal, ~30% PPP. The US share of global manufacturing is ~17%, China is ~30%. * **Relative decline in hard power projection.** The US is overstretched and war-weary after Afghanistan and Iraq. It is increasingly focused on the Indo-Pacific and less willing to police the Middle East or Europe unilaterally. * **Institutional legitimacy erosion.** Allies increasingly hedge. Europe builds strategic autonomy, Japan and South Korea build independent deterrence, and the Global South is less willing to accept US-led rules on trade, climate, and tech. * **Technology diffusion.** The US no longer monopolizes the full stack. China leads in manufacturing, rare earth processing, solar, batteries, and has a large AI talent pool. The EU regulates standards. So the US is hegemonic in the core security and financial architecture, but not hegemonic in the sense of being able to dictate outcomes everywhere. **Will AI change that?** AI will likely accelerate both the concentration and diffusion of power, which cuts both ways for US hegemony. Factors that could *reinforce* US hegemony: * **Compute and model leadership.** The US still leads in frontier model development, GPU supply via NVIDIA, and capital markets that can fund trillion-dollar AI labs. Hegemony in AI is currently hegemony in compute, data, and talent. * **Standard-setting.** Whoever defines safety standards, export controls, and interoperability norms for AI will shape the next tech order. The US is trying to do this via the CHIPS Act, AI Diffusion rules, and alliances like the US-Japan-South Korea semiconductor pact. * **Military applications.** AI is a force multiplier for ISR, autonomous weapons, cyber, and C2. The US military's advantage in data and integration could widen the gap with near-peers. Factors that could *erode* US hegemony: * **Diffusion and commoditization.** Once models are open-sourced and distilled, the advantage moves from model creation to application. China, India, and the EU are already strong in AI applications for manufacturing, logistics, and governance. * **Infrastructure dependence.** AI requires massive energy and data infrastructure. The US is vulnerable on energy costs and grid reliability, while China is building state-coordinated data centers and grid integration at scale. * **Fragmentation.** AI is becoming a geopolitical wedge. The US is pushing an AI alliance bloc, China is building its own stack with Huawei Ascend chips and domestic models, and the EU is pursuing regulatory hegemony. That is a move from US-led unipolarity to a bipolar or tripolar tech order. * **Asymmetric use.** A smaller state can use cheap AI for disinformation, biothreat design, or cyber sabotage in ways that erode US advantages in conventional deterrence. Most likely outcome is not a sudden loss of US hegemony, but a *domain-specific shift*. The US will likely retain financial and military primacy for the next decade, while AI leadership becomes more contested and multipolar. AI will make hegemony less about controlling territory and more about controlling data flows, compute access, and model governance. In short: The US is still hegemonic, but it's a declining and contested hegemony. AI will not end it, but it will make it narrower and more technological than geopolitical.

To answer your first question: **Yes, I agree that the US is currently often referred to as a hegemonic power.** However, it is important to understand that the nature of this hegemony is evolving, and the term "hegemony" itself is no longer the static, unchallenged reality it was during the Cold War. To understand the current landscape, we must look at the pillars of US hegemony: * **Military:** The US maintains the world's strongest navy, the largest nuclear arsenal, and the most extensive global military network (bases on every continent). * **Economic:** The US is the world's largest economy, the issuer of the global reserve currency (the dollar), and the dominant force in global financial markets. * **Political/Diplomatic:** The US is a permanent member of the UN Security Council, a founding member of NATO, and the leader of global institutions. * **Soft Power:** The US exports its culture, values, and legal systems, and its universities and tech companies are global institutions. AI is changing this. Autonomous drones, AI-driven cyber warfare, and predictive analytics are making military power more accessible. A smaller nation with a strong AI strategy could potentially challenge US dominance in the cyber or space domains. #### 2. The US currently holds the advantage in data, capital, and top-tier talent, which are the raw materials for AI. If China can achieve a technological breakthrough that the US fails to prevent, it could erode the US economic hegemony. #### 3. Deepfakes, automated propaganda, and algorithmic manipulation could undermine the US's soft power. Probably not in the near term. The US currently has a massive structural advantage in data, capital, and talent. It is highly unlikely that AI will suddenly make China or another power the undisputed hegemon. The US is widely described as a hegemonic power in International Relations, and by most academic definitions, that description is still accurate - though it is increasingly contested. Whether that hegemony will continue depends in large part on AI, which is likely to be a major determinant of future power. Here is how scholars usually break it down: ### 1. What Does "Hegemonic Power" Mean? In international relations theory, hegemony does not just mean "the strongest country." It means a state that has clear primacy in multiple domains at once and uses that primacy to shape the rules of the international system. A hegemon typically has: **a) Military primacy:** Ability to project power globally **b) Economic primacy:** Largest economy, reserve currency, control over key financial institutions **c) Institutional primacy:** Central role in creating and maintaining the rules, alliances, and organizations that govern the system **d) Cultural/Soft Power primacy:** Influence over global culture, technology, education, and norms Hegemonic Stability Theory argues that such a power provides public goods - like security guarantees, freedom of navigation, and a stable financial system - that other states benefit from, even as they may resent the dominance. ### 2. Is the US Still Hegemonic? There are two common views: **Argument that the US IS still hegemonic:** * **Military:** The US spends more on defense than the next 10 countries combined, has ~750 bases in 80 countries, 11 aircraft carriers, and leads NATO, the world's most powerful alliance. * **Economic:** The US is ~26% of global GDP at nominal rates, the dollar makes up ~58% of global foreign exchange reserves and is dominant in oil and international transactions, and US institutions like the IMF and World Bank are headquartered in Washington. * **Technology and Finance:** Wall Street, Silicon Valley, and US control over key technologies like advanced semiconductors and software platforms remain central to the global economy. * **Alliances:** No other power has a comparable network of formal treaty allies in Europe, East Asia, and the Middle East. In this view, we are still in the US-led order established after 1945 and consolidated after the Cold War, even if that order is under strain. **Argument that US hegemony is DECLINING or already multipolar:** * **Economic share is falling:** The US was ~40% of global GDP in 1960. China is now the largest economy by purchasing power parity and the largest trading partner for most countries. * **Challengers:** China, Russia, and regional powers are more willing and able to challenge US-led rules, as seen in Ukraine, the South China Sea, and the expansion of BRICS. * **Internal constraints:** Political polarization, debt, and domestic debates over interventionism have made allies question the long-term reliability of US commitments. * **De-dollarization and alternative institutions:** China has created the Asian Infrastructure Investment Bank, Belt and Road Initiative, and CIPS payment system as alternatives to US-led institutions. Most scholars today describe the system as **"unipolarity in decline" or "competitive multipolarity"** - the US is still the single most powerful state by a significant margin, but it can no longer dictate outcomes alone as it could in the 1990s. ### 3. Will AI Change That? AI is widely seen as a general-purpose technology like electricity or the internet, and leadership in AI could reinforce or erode hegemony. There are three plausible scenarios: **1. AI Reinforces US Hegemony** The US currently leads in the full AI stack: advanced chip design [Nvidia, AMD], frontier models [OpenAI, Google DeepMind, Anthropic, Meta], cloud infrastructure [Amazon, Microsoft, Google], and top research talent and universities. If the US maintains its lead in compute, especially advanced semiconductors and the energy infrastructure to power them, and turns AI into sustained productivity growth, military advantage, and control over global AI platforms, it could extend its economic and military lead for another generation. Export controls on advanced chips to China are an explicit attempt to preserve this advantage. **2. AI Accelerates Multipolarity** AI is highly diffuse. Models can be replicated, open-sourced, and run relatively cheaply once developed. China is already highly competitive in AI applications, surveillance, drones, and manufacturing integration, and leads in AI-related patents and data scale. If AI capabilities proliferate quickly, it could lower the barrier for middle powers to develop advanced military and economic capabilities, reducing the gap between the US and its competitors. **3. AI Creates a New Kind of Power Struggle** Some analysts argue AI will not just shift power *between* states, but create new power centers *beyond* states - large tech companies that control frontier models, compute, and data. Hegemony may depend less on territory and armies and more on who controls compute, energy, data, and AI governance standards. The country or bloc that sets the global rules for AI safety, regulation, and deployment could gain significant institutional power. Key variables to watch: * **Compute and Chips:** Who controls fabrication of cutting-edge chips, primarily TSMC in Taiwan but designed in the US * **Energy:** AI requires enormous electricity; power generation may become a strategic bottleneck * **Talent and Research:** Ability to attract and retain top scientists * **Adoption:** Whether AI translates into broad economic growth or remains concentrated in a few firms * **Military Integration:** Who most effectively integrates AI into command, cyber, and autonomous systems ### Conclusion By the traditional definition, the US still qualifies as a hegemonic power - it is the only state with global primacy across military, economic, financial, and technological domains. However, that hegemony is less absolute than it was 20-30 years ago and is now contested. AI is unlikely to single-handedly end or guarantee US hegemony, but it will be a central factor. If the US sustains its current lead in AI innovation and deployment, it is likely to prolong its primacy. If that lead narrows or AI diffuses rapidly to competitors, it is likely to accelerate the shift toward a more multipolar system. The label “hegemonic power” is often applied to the United States, and for good reason—but whether it fully applies today depends on how you define hegemony. ### In what sense is the U.S. still hegemonic? - **Military:** The U.S. has the world’s largest defense budget, a global network of bases, and unmatched power-projection capabilities. No other country comes close in conventional military terms. - **Economic and financial:** The U.S. dollar remains the world’s primary reserve currency, and U.S. financial markets, institutions, and supply chains are deeply embedded in the global economy. - **Technological and innovation:** The U.S. still leads in many cutting-edge fields—AI, semiconductors, biotech, space, and software—though the lead is narrowing in some areas. - **Institutional and normative:** The U.S. helped build and still heavily influences the post-WWII international order: the UN, IMF, World Bank, NATO, and various alliances. It also projects cultural influence through media, education, and technology platforms. So in a traditional sense, the U.S. is still the most powerful single state. But “hegemony” implies not just power, but the ability to set the rules and have others follow them—often willingly. That is more contested today. ### Why the “hegemony” label is contested - **Rise of China:** China has become a near-peer competitor economically and militarily, especially in Asia. It offers an alternative model of development and increasingly challenges U.S. influence in trade, technology, and global governance. - **Multipolarity:** The world is no longer bipolar (U.S. vs. USSR) or unipolar (U.S. after 1991). It is more multipolar, with the EU, India, Brazil, and others carving out independent roles. - **Declining legitimacy:** U.S. actions in Iraq, Afghanistan, and elsewhere have damaged its moral authority. Many countries no longer see the U.S. as a neutral or benevolent leader. - **Domestic strain:** Political polarization, debt, inequality, and social unrest weaken the U.S. model’s appeal and its capacity to act consistently abroad. So a fair answer might be: **The U.S. is still the most powerful country, but it is no longer a hegemon in the classic sense—it cannot simply dictate outcomes, and its leadership is increasingly challenged.** --- ### Will AI change that? AI is a double-edged sword for U.S. hegemony. It could reinforce it, or it could accelerate its decline. #### Ways AI could strengthen U.S. hegemony - **Military advantage:** AI-enabled drones, cyber capabilities, autonomous systems, and intelligence analysis could give the U.S. a decisive edge over rivals, especially if it maintains leadership in AI research and deployment. - **Economic dominance:** The U.S. is home to the world’s leading AI companies—OpenAI, Google, Microsoft, Nvidia, etc. If AI drives productivity growth, the U.S. could extend its economic lead. - **Technological standard-setting:** Whoever sets AI safety standards, data governance norms, and export controls can shape the global rules. The U.S. currently has significant leverage through export controls on advanced chips and AI models. - **Alliance networks:** The U.S. can use AI to deepen cooperation with allies (e.g., intelligence sharing, joint AI development) and create a “democratic AI bloc” that excludes rivals. #### Ways AI could weaken U.S. hegemony - **Diffusion of power:** AI is not a zero-sum technology. It can be developed by many countries and non-state actors. China, in particular, is investing heavily in AI and may match or surpass the U.S. in certain areas, especially in applied AI, surveillance, and manufacturing. - **Disruption of the dollar and financial system:** AI-driven automation could reshape global supply chains, reducing reliance on U.S.-centric systems. Cryptocurrencies and digital currencies, accelerated by AI, could challenge dollar dominance. - **Loss of control:** AI could make the world more unpredictable. Autonomous weapons, cyberattacks, and AI-generated disinformation could erode the U.S.’s ability to maintain order, even if it remains militarily strong. - **Domestic disruption:** AI will likely cause massive job displacement and economic inequality. If the U.S. handles this poorly, it could face internal instability that undermines its global standing. - **Legitimacy crisis:** If the U.S. uses AI for mass surveillance or autonomous warfare, it could further erode its moral authority, making it harder to lead by example. --- ### The most likely outcome AI probably won’t single-handedly decide U.S. hegemony. It will amplify existing trends. If the U.S. maintains its lead in AI research, talent, and deployment—and manages the social and ethical consequences—AI could help it remain the dominant power for decades. But if AI accelerates the rise of China, fragments the global economy, or destabilizes U.S. society, it could hasten the transition to a more genuinely multipolar world. In short: **The U.S. is a declining hegemon, not a fallen one. AI is a wildcard that could either extend its dominance or accelerate its replacement—depending on how it is developed, governed, and used.**

If China matches or surpasses the US in AI, it could erode the US's technological monopoly. - **Capability diffusion**: AI is a "democratizing" technology. Smaller states or non-state actors can use AI to leapfrog traditional barriers (e.g., cyberattacks, economic espionage). This makes the world harder for any single power to control. - **Internal instability**: AI could exacerbate inequality in the US (job displacement, concentration of wealth in tech elites), leading to political polarization and social unrest. A weakened, internally divided US would struggle to project hegemony abroad. - **New global governance**: If AI leads to a "multipolar AI" landscape—where no single country controls the entire stack (data, compute, algorithms)—the US will lose its ability to set global tech standards. **The Verdict:** AI is a **force multiplier**, not a magic wand. It will not *end* US hegemony, but it will likely **accelerate the shift from unipolarity to a more complex bipolar or multipolar competition**, particularly between the US and China. The US has a head start, but the race is tight. The outcome will depend on: 1. **Policy choices** (investment in AI safety, education, and infrastructure). 2. **Geopolitical alliances** (whether the US can keep allies like Europe and Japan aligned). 3. **Domestic stability** (managing AI's economic disruption). In short, AI won't automatically dethrone the US, but it will determine whether the US remains the *sole* hegemon or becomes merely the *first among equals* in a fragmented, AI-driven world order.

The characterization of the United States as a hegemonic power is widely supported in international relations scholarship, though it requires important nuance. Whether AI will alter that status depends less on the technology itself and more on how it interacts with broader economic, institutional, and geopolitical dynamics. ### 1. Is the U.S. currently a hegemonic power? **Yes, but with qualifications.** In IR theory, hegemony refers to a state that wields disproportionate influence across multiple domains (military, economic, institutional, cultural, technological) and plays a central role in shaping international rules and norms. By that standard, the U.S. remains a hegemon today, but its position is more **relative, contested, and adaptive** than during the unipolar period of the 1990s–2000s. - **Strengths:** The U.S. retains the world's most capable military with global reach, the dollar's entrenched role as the primary reserve currency, leadership in high-value sectors (semiconductors, software, biotech, finance), deep alliance networks (NATO, Indo-Pacific partnerships), and outsized influence in international institutions. - **Constraints:** Economic interdependence, the rise of China, regional assertiveness (e.g., India, EU, Gulf states), domestic political polarization, and the costs of global engagement have eroded the U.S.'s ability to act unilaterally. ## The US as a Hegemonic Power The US is frequently described as a hegemonic or even "hyperpower," and there is substantial basis for this characterization. Since the end of the Cold War, the US has held an extraordinary concentration of power across multiple dimensions: - **Military**: The US defense budget exceeds that of the next several nations combined, with global force projection capabilities unmatched by any other country. - **Economic**: The US dollar serves as the world's primary reserve currency, and the US has historically been the largest or one of the largest economies globally. - **Technological & Scientific**: The US leads in innovation, higher education, and R&D investment. - **Cultural**: American soft power — through media, entertainment, tech brands, and values — exerts enormous global influence. - **Institutional**: The US plays a central role in international institutions and maintains a vast network of alliances. However, this picture is nuanced. The rise of China as a comprehensive strategic competitor, the emergence of a more multipolar world, and domestic challenges within the US (political polarization, fiscal pressures) all suggest that unipolar hegemony is already eroding to some degree. Some scholars argue the US is better described as the world's most powerful *single* state rather than a true hegemon in the classical sense. **In short: Yes, the US is often and reasonably called a hegemonic power, but that status is contested and evolving.** --- ## Will AI Change That? AI is arguably the most consequential technology since nuclear weapons, and it will almost certainly reshape the global balance of power — though the direction of that change is not predetermined. ### Ways AI could **reinforce** US hegemony: - The US currently leads in frontier AI research, home to labs like OpenAI, Google DeepMind, Anthropic, and Meta, along with access to the world's most powerful compute infrastructure. - AI amplifies existing advantages in intelligence, cybersecurity, logistics, and military effectiveness. - AI-driven economic productivity gains could widen the gap between the US and competitors. - Control over the AI supply chain (advanced chips, data centers, talent pipelines) gives the US significant leverage. ### Ways AI could **challenge or erode** US dominance: - China is investing massively in AI and could close or even close the gap in certain domains, particularly in military AI and surveillance technology. - AI could **democratize** certain capabilities — smaller nations or non-state actors could gain disproportionate influence through AI tools. - AI-driven automation could reshape global economics in ways that don't necessarily favor the current hegemon. - AI governance and regulation are becoming new battlegrounds, and leadership in setting global norms could shift influence. - AI could reduce the importance of traditional military power and increase the importance of technological and informational dominance, where the playing field is more fluid. ### The most likely outcome: AI will probably **intensify power asymmetries** in the short to medium term, benefiting those who already lead. This could entrench US dominance — or, if other nations (especially China) close the gap, it could accelerate a **multipolar** or even **bipolar** world order. AI is unlikely to eliminate hegemonic dynamics, but it will redefine what "power" means and who holds it. **In conclusion:** The US's hegemonic status is real but not guaranteed to last. AI will be one of the most decisive variables in determining whether that status is maintained, amplified, or fundamentally transformed — and the outcome will depend on decisions made not just by governments, but by corporations, researchers, and societies around the world.

Here is an analysis of both parts of your question. --- ### Part 1: Is the US currently a hegemonic power? **Yes, but it is a contested, evolving hegemony.** Historically, a "hegemon" is a nation with enough military, economic, technological, and cultural power to dictate or heavily influence global rules and institutions. Following the fall of the Soviet Union in 1991, the US entered a period of "unipolarity" where it was the undisputed global hegemon. Today, the nature of that dominance has changed: 1. **Where US Hegemony Persists:** * **Military Power:** The US defense budget remains larger than the next nine countries combined. It maintains a unique global power-projection capability through hundreds of military bases worldwide, unequaled alliance networks (like NATO), and advanced force projection. * **Financial Infrastructure:** The US dollar remains the dominant global reserve currency, giving the US immense geopolitical leverage (e.g., via financial sanctions) and economic privileges. * **Soft and Technological Power:** American technological platforms, universities, global culture (media, entertainment), and financial markets continue to exert unmatched influence globally. 2. **Where US Hegemony is Weakening:** * **The Rise of China:** China is now a peer competitor economically, militarily in the Indo-Pacific, and technologically. This has effectively ended absolute US unipolarity. * **Multipolarity:** The growth of coalition blocs like BRICS, combined with a more assertive Russia and regional powers (e.g., India, Saudi Arabia, UAE) pursuing strategic autonomy, means the US can no longer dictate global outcomes unilaterally. * **Domestic Fragmentation:** Internal political polarization in the US has raised questions globally about American reliability, long-term strategy, and leadership continuity. **Conclusion on current status:** The US is no longer an absolute *unipolar hegemon*, but it remains the most powerful single actor in a rapidly shifting **multipolar system**. --- ### Part 2: Will AI change that? **Yes, AI will heavily influence global power dynamics, but whether it extends or ends US hegemony depends on several factors.** AI is a "general-purpose technology" (like electricity or the steam engine) that acts as a force multiplier across national defense, economic productivity, scientific discovery, and intelligence. How AI Could Erode US Hegemony * **China’s Strategic Advantages:** China is uniquely positioned to challenge US leadership in AI. It possesses massive datasets (due to population and state surveillance), a vast pool of STEM graduates, strong state-directed capital, and rapid adoption of technology in manufacturing and infrastructure. If China achieves a breakthrough in Artificial General Intelligence (AGI) first, it could leapfrog US military and economic dominance. * **Supply Chain Vulnerabilities:** Advanced AI relies on hardware. The concentration of advanced semiconductor manufacturing in Taiwan (TSMC) creates a critical strategic vulnerability for the US. A disruption there could severely cripple Western AI development. * **Asymmetric Warfare:** AI lowers the cost of asymmetric tactics. Smaller nations or non-state actors could use AI for sophisticated cyberattacks, automated dis-information campaigns, or low-cost autonomous drone swarms

Whether the United States is considered a hegemonic power depends largely on how one defines hegemony in the 21st century. Most political scientists agree that while the U.S. remains the world’s foremost global power, traditional "hegemony"—understood as undisputed, unipolar dominance—has eroded. Here is a breakdown of whether the U.S. is currently a hegemon and how artificial intelligence (AI) is poised to reshape that status. --- ### Part 1: Is the U.S. currently a hegemonic power? **Yes, in terms of capabilities:** * **Military:** The U.S. maintains global military reach with unmatched power projection, a network of international alliances (like NATO), and defense spending that exceeds the next several countries combined. * **Economic & Financial:** The U.S. dollar remains the world’s primary reserve currency, giving Washington extraordinary leverage over global finance. Wall Street and American multinational corporations remain central to the global economy. * **Soft Power:** American culture, media, language, and higher education continue to exert global influence. **No, in terms of unipolar control:** * **The Rise of Multipolarity:** We no longer live in the "unipolar moment" of the 1990s. The rise of China as an economic and technological peer, alongside the assertive foreign policies of middle powers like India, Brazil, and regional players like Russia, means the U.S. can no longer dictate global outcomes unilaterally. * **Internal Vulnerabilities:** Deep political polarization, social divisions, and questions about the long-term sustainability of U.S. public debt have diminished perceptions of American governance as a model to emulate. **Verdict:** The U.S. is best described as a **"primus inter pares" (first among equals)** or a declining hegemon navigating a transition back to a multipolar world order. --- ### Part 2: Will AI change that? **In short: Yes, AI is the ultimate variable in the future of global power.** AI is not just another consumer technology; it is a foundational, "dual-use" technology (having both civilian and military applications) that will redefine national power. How AI impacts U.S. hegemony depends on three major factors: #### 1. Economic Productivity and Growth * **The Stakes:** The countries that lead in AI development will capture the lion's share of future global economic growth, automation efficiencies, and scientific breakthroughs (e.g., in pharmaceuticals, materials science, and energy). * **The U.S. Position:** The U.S. currently leads in foundational AI research, venture capital, and cutting-edge hardware (like advanced semiconductors designed by NVIDIA). However, maintaining this lead requires continuous influxes of global talent and massive energy infrastructure. #### 2. Military Superiority ("Algorithmic Warfare") * **The Stakes:** Future conflicts will be decided by speed, data processing, autonomous systems, and cyber capabilities. AI will dictate command-and-control efficiency. * **The U.S. Position:** The Pentagon is aggressively integrating AI (e.g., Project Maven, Replicator initiative). However, China is pursuing a strategy of "military-civil fusion" to rapidly integrate commercial AI breakthroughs into its military, posing a direct challenge to U.S. dominance. #### 3. The Bipolar Tech Split * Rather than a single global standard, we may see a **bifurcated technological world**—a "Splinternet"—divided between a U.S.-led ecosystem and a China-led ecosystem. * Countries in the Global South will likely choose sides or adopt tools based on cost, governance models, and geopolitical alignment. If China successfully exports its AI surveillance and governance infrastructure to developing nations, American ideological hegemony will further decline. ### Conclusion AI is unlikely to preserve traditional American hegemony on its own, nor will it instantly destroy it. Instead, **AI is an accelerant.** If the U.S. maintains its lead in AI innovation, talent attraction, and ethical standard-setting, it can cement a new kind of technological hegemony, adapting to a multipolar world by remaining the indispensable center of gravity. Conversely, if the U.S. falls behind China in AI deployment and governance, it will mark a definitive turning point in the end of American global hegemony.

The US is still a hegemonic power, but it's a different kind of hegemony than it was in 1991 or 1945. It's more accurate to call it a contested hegemony. If we use the classic definition from hegemonic stability theory, a hegemon needs to lead in 4 areas: **1. Military:** The US spends more than the next 10 countries combined, has 11 carrier strike groups, ~750 overseas bases, and is the only country that can project force globally at scale. No other power is close. **2. Economic:** The US is ~26% of global GDP at market exchange rates, with the deepest capital markets in the world. About 58% of global foreign exchange reserves are in dollars, and ~50% of global trade is invoiced in dollars. That gives it structural power through sanctions, monetary policy, and the IMF/World Bank system it designed. **3. Institutional / Diplomatic:** The post-WWII architecture - UN, NATO, IMF, World Bank, SWIFT - was largely built by and around the US. It still sets the agenda in those institutions. **4. Cultural / Technological:** English as the lingua franca, US universities, Hollywood, Silicon Valley. The US still dominates the frontier tech stack. On all four, the US is still #1. No other country leads in all four. What has changed is that hegemony is no longer unipolar dominance. It's relative decline, not absolute decline. China has near-parity in manufacturing, purchasing-power GDP, and is a peer competitor in some tech areas. Russia can contest regional security. Middle powers like India, Turkey, Brazil, Indonesia, Saudi Arabia now hedge and don't automatically align with Washington. And the US itself has been less willing to pay the costs of hegemony - endless wars, open markets, acting as global security guarantor. So: Yes, the US is still the hegemon, but we are moving from unipolar hegemony to a multipolar system where the US is *primus inter pares* - first among equals - rather than the sole superpower. ### Will AI change that? AI is a General Purpose Technology like electricity or the internet. It won't automatically end US hegemony, but it will be the main arena where hegemony is decided over the next 20 years. It can cut both ways: **Forces that could reinforce US hegemony:** 1. **The full stack advantage:** The US currently leads the AI stack - chip design [Nvidia, AMD], fabrication equipment, cloud infrastructure [AWS, Azure, Google Cloud], frontier models [OpenAI, Anthropic, Google DeepMind, Meta], and AI talent concentration. 2. **Energy and capital:** Frontier AI requires massive amounts of capital and electricity. The US has both the venture capital ecosystem and, unlike Europe, abundant energy and land for data centers. 3. **Military integration:** The US military is better positioned to integrate AI into C4ISR, drones, and decision-making than any other military. If the US maintains its lead in compute and frontier models, AI dramatically increases US productivity, military effectiveness, and economic leverage. **Forces that could erode US hegemony:** 1. **Diffusion is fast and cheap:** Unlike nuclear weapons or aircraft carriers, AI models can be copied, open-sourced, and run on commodity hardware. DeepSeek, Qwen, and Llama show that frontier-level capability is not staying exclusive for long. 2. **Asymmetric equalizer:** AI allows smaller states and non-state actors to do things that used to require a superpower - sophisticated cyberattacks, mass disinformation, autonomous drone swarms. That erodes the US military advantage. 3. **China's different strategy:** China is behind on cutting-edge chips due to export controls, but ahead on deployment - AI in manufacturing, surveillance, logistics, and with massive amounts of real-world data. If AI becomes more about application than inventing the biggest model, China could benefit more. 4. **De-dollarization of intelligence:** If AI becomes the most valuable commodity, power shifts from who controls the dollar or oil to who controls compute, data, and electricity. That is a more contested field. The most likely outcome is not that AI replaces the US with China as the new hegemon, but that AI makes hegemony itself harder to sustain for anyone. In the past, hegemony was about controlling physical chokepoints - the Strait of Hormuz, the dollar clearing system. AI creates a world where power is more distributed, more software-defined, and where the barrier to entry for disruptive capability is lower. So AI probably won't end US hegemony in the next decade, but it will determine whether US hegemony is extended for another 30 years or whether we accelerate into a truly multipolar, or even fragmented, system where no one is hegemonic. Yes—with qualifications. Yes—with qualifications. The US retains major advantages: - **Military reach:** It has the world’s most capable global military, extensive overseas bases, powerful alliances, and leading naval and air forces. - **Financial influence:** The dollar remains the dominant reserve and transaction currency, while US-controlled financial institutions and sanctions give Washington unusual leverage. - **Alliance networks:** NATO and US partnerships in Asia provide a coalition structure that no rival currently matches. - **Technological and cultural power:** The US remains central in advanced computing, software, aerospace, biotechnology, entertainment, higher education, and venture capital. - **Institutional influence:** Many post-1945 international institutions and rules were built around American leadership. “American hegemony” is a useful description, but only with qualifications. The United States remains unusually dominant across several dimensions of power: - **Military:** unmatched global force projection, alliance networks, naval reach, and defense spending. - **Economic and financial:** the world’s largest economy in nominal terms, the dollar’s central role in trade, reserves, and finance, and deep capital markets. - **Technological:** leading firms and research institutions in semiconductors, cloud computing, advanced AI, biotech, and software. - **Institutional and cultural:** major influence through universities, media, standards-setting bodies, international institutions, and alliances. But it is not an uncontested hegemon in the sense the US often was after the Cold War. China is a genuine peer competitor in manufacturing, trade, state capacity, and several technologies; the EU, India, Japan, Gulf states, and others have more room to maneuver; and many countries resist choosing fully between Washington and Beijing. It is therefore more accurate to call the system **US-led but increasingly multipolar**, rather than purely unipolar. AI could reinforce American power—but it is unlikely to settle the question by itself. ### Ways AI could strengthen US primacy 1. **Concentration in the AI stack** Frontier AI requires advanced chips, cloud-scale computing, capital, elite research talent, energy, and sophisticated firms. The US and close partners currently possess major advantages in much of that stack—especially leading chip design, cloud platforms, frontier-model labs, and access to cutting-edge semiconductor equipment through allied supply chains. 2. **Military and intelligence advantages** AI can improve intelligence analysis, logistics, cyber operations, autonomous systems, targeting, and decision support. A country with existing global military infrastructure may gain more from these capabilities than one starting from a weaker base. 3. **Economic productivity and innovation** If AI substantially raises productivity, the states that deploy it effectively across science, industry, health, services, and defense could widen their economic lead. The US is well positioned because it combines research, entrepreneurship, financing, and a large technology sector. 4. **Standards and governance power** Countries that build the leading systems may influence global rules around safety, data, export controls, intellectual property, and technical standards. ### Ways AI could weaken or complicate US dominance 1. **Technology diffuses** Software spreads more easily than aircraft carriers or nuclear weapons. Open models, cheaper computing, and widespread access to talent could allow many states—and non-state groups—to acquire meaningful capabilities. 2. **China and others can compete selectively** Even if the US retains an edge in frontier chips or models, China has enormous industrial capacity, a large technical workforce, major domestic markets, and the ability to deploy AI at scale. Other powers may become leaders in particular niches. 3. **AI empowers smaller actors** Cyber tools, propaganda, surveillance, drones, automated research, and financial manipulation can lower the cost of exercising influence or causing disruption. That may make the world less orderly even if the US remains strongest overall. 4. **Dependence on physical infrastructure** AI is not just code. It relies on energy, chips, supply chains, data centers, rare materials, and skilled labor. These dependencies create vulnerabilities and distribute leverage among allies and competitors. 5. **Domestic political capacity matters** A country’s AI advantage will depend less on impressive demonstrations than on whether it can educate workers, build infrastructure, regulate intelligently, maintain social legitimacy, and translate innovation into broad productivity. Internal dysfunction could squander technological leadership. So: I would agree that the US is still the leading power and retains important hegemonic features, especially through its alliance system and financial-technological position. But I would not describe its position as absolute or permanent. AI is more likely to make power **more contested and more concentrated at the same time**: concentrated among states and firms able to supply compute, chips, energy, and models, but contested because AI-enabled capabilities will spread widely. The decisive question is not whether AI “belongs” to the US; it is whether the US and its allies can turn their current advantages into durable economic, institutional, and political strength.

Yes—with qualifications. The United States is still best described as the world’s leading power, and in several domains it remains hegemonic, but it is not an uncontested global hegemon in the way it was in the immediate post–Cold War period. Its position rests on an unusual combination of advantages: - **Military reach:** The US has unmatched global force-projection capacity, overseas bases, naval power, intelligence networks, and defense alliances. - **Financial power:** The dollar remains central to global trade, reserves, borrowing, and sanctions policy. US capital markets are exceptionally deep. - **Alliance networks:** NATO, alliances in East Asia, and partnerships elsewhere give the US influence that China or Russia cannot easily replicate. - **Technology and firms:** Many of the leading AI, software, semiconductor-design, cloud, biotech, and platform companies are American. - **Institutional and cultural influence:** US universities, media, legal structures, research institutions, and immigration appeal remain major sources of soft power. But “hegemon” can mislead if it implies control. The US faces meaningful constraints: China is a peer competitor in manufacturing and a major technological and military challenger; Russia can disrupt regional security; India, the EU, Gulf states, Brazil, and others exercise more independent influence; and US allies do not always follow Washington’s preferences. In that sense, the system is increasingly **multipolar**, though not evenly so. The US is first among powers, rather than all-powerful. ## Will AI change that? AI is likely to reinforce great-power competition rather than simply determine a new hegemon. It could strengthen the US—but it could also make power more dispersed and fragile. ### Why AI could reinforce US primacy Advanced AI depends heavily on resources in which the US is strong: - Frontier semiconductor design and much of the AI software ecosystem - Leading cloud-computing firms and research labs - Venture capital and access to global capital markets - Top universities and the ability to attract skilled immigrants - Deep integration with allied technology producers, especially in Europe, Japan, South Korea, Taiwan, and others If AI substantially raises productivity, scientific discovery, military logistics, intelligence analysis, and innovation, countries that can develop and deploy it effectively may gain a major economic and strategic advantage. The US is well placed in that competition. ### Why AI may not create a durable US monopoly AI also has features that limit monopoly: - Models and techniques spread quickly, especially once they become cheaper to train and run. - Many capabilities can be built on open research, widely available data, and commodity hardware. - China has enormous engineering capacity, a vast domestic market, strong state support, and significant AI talent. - Other countries may specialize: chips, energy, robotics, data services, cybersecurity, regulation, or AI applications. - The physical foundations of AI—advanced chips, manufacturing equipment, electricity, data centers, undersea cables, and critical minerals—are geographically distributed and vulnerable. The US can restrict access to the most advanced chips and tooling, but restrictions may encourage rivals to build alternative supply chains. They can buy time; they do not guarantee permanent technological dominance. ### The military and political effects may matter more than the models AI could shift power through: - **Cyber operations and disinformation:** Lowering the cost of influence campaigns, espionage, fraud, and disruption. - **Surveillance and repression:** Giving authoritarian states more tools to monitor populations—though this can also create brittleness and mistrust. - **Autonomous and semi-autonomous weapons:** Making drones, targeting, logistics, and battlefield sensing cheaper and more widespread. - **Economic productivity:** Potentially widening the gap between states that can integrate AI into firms, government, education, and infrastructure and those that cannot. - **Governance standards:** Countries that set rules for chips, safety, data, intellectual property, and international AI use may gain structural influence. So AI may make the world less stable before it makes any one country clearly dominant. It can empower large states, but also smaller states, companies, criminal groups, and non-state actors. ## Bottom line I would call the US the leading power in an increasingly competitive, partially multipolar order—not an uncontested hegemon. AI is more likely to be an amplifier of existing strengths and weaknesses than a single “hegemon-maker.” A favorable outcome for US influence would require more than leading AI labs. It would require maintaining alliances, attracting talent, investing in energy and infrastructure, protecting democratic legitimacy, managing chip supply-chain vulnerabilities, and helping partners benefit from AI rather than treating them merely as markets or security dependencies. If it does those things, AI could extend US leadership. If it does not, AI could accelerate diffusion of power and deepen geopolitical fragmentation.

**Yes, the US is reasonably described as a hegemonic (or at least primus inter pares / leading) power today, though the label is imperfect and contested; AI is more likely to reinforce than overturn that position in the near-to-medium term, while introducing new risks and diffusion effects.** ### Is the US a hegemonic power? In international-relations terms, hegemony usually means a state with preponderant material capabilities (economic, military, technological) plus the ability to shape rules, institutions, and often preferences in the system. By that standard: - **Material dominance remains real**: The US has the world’s largest nominal GDP, deepest capital markets, reserve-currency status (dollar), unmatched power-projection military (including alliances and basing), and leading positions in high-end semiconductors, software, biotech, and aerospace. Soft power (universities, culture, standards-setting) and network effects (finance, internet governance legacies, English) amplify this. - **It is not absolute or unchallenged**: China’s scale in manufacturing, trade, infrastructure lending, and certain tech applications; Russia’s spoiler capacity; and the agency of middle powers/EU/India create a more multipolar *distribution of influence* than classic mid-20th-century US hegemony. Domestic polarization, fiscal trajectories, and alliance free-riding debates also constrain it. Many scholars therefore prefer “primacy,” “liberal international order leadership,” or “partial/incomplete hegemony.” - **Empirical pattern**: The US still disproportionately sets agendas in security (NATO+, Indo-Pacific frameworks), finance (IMF/World Bank influence, sanctions reach), and tech standards, even as others contest them. Calling it “a hegemonic power” is fair shorthand; calling it an uncontested global hegemon overstates. ### Will AI change that? AI is a general-purpose technology with military, economic, and governance spillovers. It will affect power balances, but current trajectories point more toward **amplifying existing US advantages** than neatly dethroning them—while creating diffusion, dual-use, and instability risks. - **US starting position is strong**: Leading frontier labs, talent concentration, chip design (and allied fabrication via TSMC etc.), cloud infrastructure, venture capital, and data/compute ecosystems. Export controls, alliance coordination (e.g., on advanced nodes), and research universities reinforce this. Military applications (ISR, autonomy, cyber, decision support) build on existing C4ISR and alliance advantages. - **China and others are serious competitors**: Scale in data, applied AI deployment, manufacturing of supporting hardware, and state-directed investment mean China can close gaps in many commercial and some military domains. Open-source models, talent flows, and chip smuggling/leakage erode moats. Europe, India, and others will matter in regulation, niches, and markets. Pure “US monopoly” is unlikely. - **Mechanisms that could sustain or grow US edge**: Faster iteration cycles tied to compute + capital; alliance tech sharing vs. more closed competitors; dollar/finance recycling into AI investment; standards and safety regimes that favor incumbents with resources. AI-enabled productivity could widen economic leads if adoption and complementary institutions (rule of law, immigration for talent) hold. - **Mechanisms that could erode it or create multipolarity**: Rapid capability diffusion (open models, cheaper inference); AI-driven manufacturing/robotics reducing labor-cost edges; new dependencies (rare earths, energy for data centers); cyber/AI sabotage risks; governance failures or safety incidents that tax legitimacy; or adversaries using AI for asymmetric strategies (disinfo, swarming, financial stress). A true discontinuous jump (e.g., transformative AGI controlled by one actor) is speculative and cuts both ways. - **Time horizon matters**: Over 5–15 years, expect contested US leadership in frontier AI rather than clean displacement. Longer-term outcomes depend on compute governance, energy, talent, alignment/safety progress, and geopolitics far more than on any single model release. AI will also reshape *what hegemony means* (e.g., more emphasis on compute sovereignty, algorithmic influence, bio+AI, space). **Bottom line**: The hegemonic-power description fits the US better than any alternative today. AI is a power-concentrating *and* power-diffusing technology; the US is well-placed to capture disproportionate benefits if it maintains alliances, compute access, talent inflows, and institutional adaptability, but complacency, decoupling frictions, or safety failures could narrow the lead. It will change *how* primacy is exercised more reliably than *whether* the US remains central. Outcomes are path-dependent on policy and discovery, not predetermined.

Yes, with important qualifications. The United States is still the closest thing the international system has to a hegemon, but that position is relative, contested, and no longer the unipolar dominance of the 1990s. AI is likely to matter a great deal to whether that primacy holds, erodes, or is reconfigured—but it will not decide the outcome by itself. **What “hegemonic” usually means here** In international-relations usage, a hegemon is not just the strongest state. It is a power that sets many of the rules, supplies key public goods (security guarantees, a reserve currency, open sea lanes, technology standards), and shapes others’ choices more than they shape its own. By that standard, the post-1945 and especially post-1991 United States has often been described as hegemonic or as enjoying primacy. **Why the label still fits, imperfectly** Several foundations remain unusually strong: - Military power projection and alliance networks (NATO, bilateral treaties in Asia) have no peer. - The dollar, deep capital markets, and the ability to impose financial sanctions still give Washington leverage others lack. - Leading firms, universities, and research ecosystems—especially in software, semiconductors design, and now frontier AI—remain concentrated in the US and its close partners. - Cultural and institutional influence is broad, even when resented. At the same time, hegemony is weaker than the casual label suggests. China’s economic and military rise, the diffusion of technology, fatigue with open-ended US commitments, and internal polarization all constrain what Washington can do. Many analysts now speak of contested primacy or an emerging multipolar or bipolar structure rather than stable hegemony. “Often referred to as” is fair; “unchallenged hegemon” is not. **Will AI change that?** It can, in more than one direction. AI is a general-purpose technology with military, economic, and intelligence applications. Leadership in frontier models, compute, chips, energy, talent, and data could widen the US advantage in productivity, autonomous systems, cyber operations, and decision speed. Export controls, alliance coordination on semiconductors, and private capital concentration currently tilt that race toward the US and partners. If that lead compounds, AI could reinforce primacy rather than end it. The same technology can also narrow gaps. Capabilities diffuse through open models, espionage, and commercial adoption. States that are not frontier leaders can still gain military and economic tools that were previously scarce. China is investing heavily in AI, power generation, and industrial application; if it closes the gap in deployed systems even without matching every frontier lab, relative US advantage shrinks. AI may also empower smaller actors and create new vulnerabilities—cyber, information, and infrastructure—that are hard for any hegemon to control. What AI will not do is automatically crown a successor or freeze the status quo. Power shifts depend on who turns models into reliable military and economic capacity, who secures energy and supply chains, how allies pool talent and compute, and whether domestic politics sustain long-term investment. Previous technological shifts (industrial production, nuclear weapons, computing, the internet) rearranged power but did not make leadership inevitable for the first mover. So: the hegemonic description is still the best short label for US primacy, provided one treats it as contested rather than absolute. AI is one of the main variables that could entrench, dilute, or redistribute that position over the next few decades. It is not a destiny.

The term **“hegemonic power”** is widely used in international relations scholarship and policy analysis to describe a state that possesses disproportionate influence over the global system—through economic weight, military reach, technological leadership, cultural appeal, and the setting of international rules. In that descriptive sense, it is accurate to say the United States is frequently characterized as a hegemonic power, particularly in the period following the Cold War. However, whether the U.S. remains a *unipolar* hegemon is debated. Many analysts argue the world is moving toward a **multipolar** or **competitive** order, where the U.S. retains unique strengths (the dollar’s reserve status, global alliances, top-tier universities, and Silicon Valley) but faces rising challengers, most notably China. ### Will AI change that? AI is a **general-purpose technology** that is likely to reshape the foundations of state power rather than simply preserve the status quo. Sakana: Fugu Ultrareleased 2026-06-24 · captured 2026-11-14 · 17,642 tokens (11,900 in / 5,742 out) · $0.2318 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
# Is the US a hegemonic power? And will AI change that? **Short answer:** Yes — but with an important qualification. The US is best described as a *contested, relatively declining* hegemon rather than the unchallenged hegemon of the 1990s. And AI is likely to reinforce US dominance in the near term while accelerating the drift toward US–China competition over the longer term. It won't, by itself, crown or dethrone a hegemon. --- ## Part 1: Is the US a hegemon? "Hegemony" means more than being strong. A hegemon has preponderant power across multiple dimensions that lets it *set the rules, norms, and institutions* others operate within. Judged against the usual dimensions, the picture is mixed. **Where the label clearly holds:** - **Military:** The US is the only state with genuinely global power-projection — a worldwide base and alliance network (NATO, East Asian partnerships), carrier strike groups, and defense spending exceeding the next several countries combined. - **Financial/structural power:** The dollar remains the dominant reserve and trade-invoicing currency (~55–60% of reserves), underpinning global finance and the sanctions architecture. This is arguably the deepest and most durable pillar of US power. - **Technology and institutions:** The US hosts most leading tech firms and top universities and sits at the center of institutions it helped design (IMF, World Bank). - **Soft power:** American culture, media, universities, and English as a lingua franca retain strong global pull. **Where the label is strained:** - **A shrinking economic share:** The US was ~40% of global GDP around 1960 and is roughly 25% today; China is at or near parity on a purchasing-power basis. - **A genuine peer competitor:** A true hegemon faces no peer. China contests US rule-setting directly (Belt and Road, BRICS expansion, the AIIB, de-dollarization efforts) and leads in manufacturing, trade, and critical minerals. - **Eroding legitimacy and a more multipolar world:** The 2003 Iraq war, the 2008 financial crisis, and domestic polarization have dented the "benign leader" image, while India, the EU, Gulf states, Turkey, and Brazil increasingly hedge rather than simply align. **Verdict:** I partly agree. The US is still the single most powerful state and the only one with truly global reach, so "hegemon" is defensible — but it is a *contested and relatively declining* hegemon in a system drifting toward multipolarity, not the uncontested hegemon of the "unipolar moment." --- ## Part 2: Will AI change that? AI is best understood as a **force multiplier that accelerates existing trends in either direction** — not a deterministic kingmaker. Its effect depends on *who controls the critical inputs* and *who captures the diffusion*. **How AI could reinforce US dominance:** - **The chip chokepoint:** Frontier AI depends on advanced semiconductors, and the US dominates or influences the key nodes — chip design (Nvidia, AMD), EDA software (Cadence, Synopsys), and, via allies, manufacturing (TSMC) and lithography (ASML). Export controls weaponize this — itself an exercise of hegemonic structural power. - **Capital and talent:** Leading frontier labs are US-based or US-aligned, backed by uniquely deep capital markets. - **Military edge:** AI-enabled intelligence, cyber, autonomy, and decision-support could widen an already-large qualitative advantage. - **Productivity:** If the US adopts AI faster than rivals, large productivity gains could compound its lead. **How AI could erode US dominance:** - **Diffusion:** Capability spreads. Open-weight models, cheaper inference, and efficient small models lower barriers — and diffusing general-purpose technologies historically *de-concentrate* power rather than entrench a single leader. - **China's scale:** Huge engineering talent, industrial capacity, state coordination, and a strong incentive to indigenize chip production could neutralize the US chokepoint over time. - **Asymmetric uses:** Cyber, disinformation, and cheap autonomous systems let weaker actors offset conventional US advantages. - **Adoption over invention:** Whoever best integrates AI across the economy, government, and defense may gain more than whoever merely builds the best models. - **Domestic disruption:** If AI worsens inequality or polarization inside the US, it could weaken the home foundations of American power. **Net assessment:** - **Short-to-medium term:** AI likely *reinforces* US advantages, because the lead in chips, frontier models, cloud, and capital is real and actively defended. - **Longer term:** The deeper logic of general-purpose technology is diffusion, which tends to *erode* monopolies. So AI more plausibly pushes the system toward **US–China bipolar competition** (or broader multipolarity) than toward restored US unipolarity. **The decisive variables:** 1. Whether the chip chokepoint holds or China achieves self-sufficiency. 2. The stability of Taiwan. 3. Which society diffuses AI most productively. 4. Whether AI mostly *concentrates* power (favoring the leader) or *diffuses* it (favoring challengers). --- ## Bottom line - **Is the US a hegemon?** Yes — the most powerful and only globally capable state — but a *contested and relatively declining* one, not the unchallenged hegemon of the post–Cold War era. - **Will AI change that?** Not by itself. AI will likely entrench US advantages in the near term but accelerate the drift toward US–China competition over time. The outcome hinges less on who builds the smartest models and more on who controls AI's critical inputs and who diffuses it most effectively
