**Short answer:** Many analysts and policymakers do describe the United States as a hegemonic power today, but the term is contested and its durability is increasingly tied to how emerging technologies—especially artificial intelligence (AI)—reshape economic, military, and diplomatic leverage. AI could both reinforce U.S. primacy and erode it, depending on how the technology diffuses, how other states adapt, and how the United States manages its own innovation, governance, and alliances. --- ## 1. What “hegemony” means in this context | Dimension | Typical indicators of U.S. hegemony (21st‑century) | |-----------|---------------------------------------------------| | **Military** | Largest defense budget (~$800 bn), global network of bases, advanced platforms (carrier groups, nuclear triad, precision‑guided weapons), dominant in defense‑technology standards. | | **Economic** | World’s largest economy (nominal GDP), issuer of the primary reserve currency (USD), headquarters of most “tech giants,” leading in financial markets, trade rules shaped by U.S.-led institutions (WTO, IMF, World Bank). | | **Technological** | Dominance in semiconductors, AI research, internet protocols, and many “platform” companies; leads in patents and R&D spending. | | **Normative/institutional** | Architect of the liberal international order (UN, NATO, G7/G20), promoter of democratic governance, human‑rights discourse, and rule‑based trade. | | **Cultural** | Global reach of Hollywood, music, academia, and social media platforms; soft‑power appeal. | These pillars are not static. They can be reinforced or weakened by internal choices (e.g., fiscal policy, political cohesion) and external pressures (e.g., rising powers, transnational challenges). --- ## 2. Why many scholars call the U.S. hegemonic 1. **Unmatched military reach** – Even with budget constraints, the U.S. can project power globally in ways no other nation can. 2. **Financial architecture** – The dollar’s role as the primary reserve and invoicing currency gives the U.S. “exorbitant privilege” (low borrowing costs, seigniorage). 3. **Tech leadership** – Companies like OpenAI, Google, and Amazon produce AI models and platforms that set global standards. 4. **Institutional embeddedness** – Many international regimes (e.g., NATO, WTO) are U.S.-led or heavily U.S.-influenced. 5. **Alliances and network effects** – The U.S. maintains a web of security and economic partnerships that amplify its influence. These factors create a feedback loop: other countries align with U.S. standards to gain access to markets, technology, and security guarantees. --- ## 3. Counter‑arguments and limits to U.S. hegemony | Issue | Why it challenges a hegemonic label | |-------|--------------------------------------| | **Domestic political fragmentation** | Partisan gridlock can hamper coherent long‑term strategy, affect credibility, and reduce policy continuity. | | **Rise of China** | China’s growing military capabilities, Belt‑and‑Road Initiative, and emerging tech sector (e.g., Huawei 5G, AI chips) create a rival pole. | | **Multipolar economic ties** | Many countries now trade heavily with the EU, China, and regional blocs; the U.S. share of global GDP has slowly declined. | | **Normative fatigue** | Overextension (e.g., prolonged wars, “war on terror”) and perceived overreach can erode soft power. | | **Technology diffusion** | AI, quantum computing, and biotech are becoming more accessible, potentially democratizing power. | | **Global challenges** | Climate change, pandemics, and cyber threats require cooperation rather than dominance. | Thus, while the U.S. remains a leading power, “hegemonic” may be an overstatement in a truly multipolar world. --- ## 4. How AI could reshape the balance ### 4.1 AI as a force multiplier for existing hegemony | Mechanism | Potential U.S. advantage | |-----------|--------------------------| | **Military AI** (autonomous weapons, decision‑support, cyber) | Could widen the gap between U.S. forces and rivals, especially if integrated with existing platforms. | | **Economic productivity** | AI‑driven automation could boost U.S. GDP growth, reinforce tech leadership, and create new exportable services. | | **Data dominance** | The U.S. hosts most global data centers and leads in data‑rich AI training sets. | | **Standard‑setting** | Early standards in AI safety, ethics, and interoperability can become global norms, extending U.S. influence. | | **Talent and ecosystem** | Silicon Valley, research universities, and venture‑capital networks attract global talent, reinforcing a “brain‑drain” advantage. | If the United States maintains its lead in AI research, chip manufacturing (e.g., through the CHIPS Act), and regulatory frameworks that encourage innovation while addressing risks, AI could deepen its hegemonic position. ### 4.2 AI as a democratizing or diffusing force | Diffusion pathway | How it could erode U.S. hegemony | |------------------|-----------------------------------| | **Global AI capability spread** | Countries like China, the EU, India, and emerging tech hubs develop indigenous AI models and hardware, reducing dependence on U.S. technology. | | **Open‑source AI** | Models such as Llama, Falcon, and others become widely available, enabling non‑U.S. actors to build competitive capabilities at lower cost. | | **AI‑enabled economic restructuring** | Automation may shift comparative advantage away from traditional high‑skill sectors, potentially weakening the U.S. edge in knowledge‑intensive industries. | | **Cyber and autonomous threats** | AI‑powered cyber attacks, drones, or autonomous systems could level the playing field for adversaries, challenging U.S. military superiority. | | **Regulatory divergence** | Different AI governance approaches (e.g., EU’s risk‑based regulation vs. more permissive regimes) could fragment the global market, diluting U.S. standards. | ### 4.3 Geopolitical scenarios | Scenario | AI’s impact on U.S. hegemony | |----------|------------------------------| | **U.S. retains AI leadership** (strong R&D, talent retention, favorable policy) | AI reinforces existing advantages → deeper hegemony, but still contested by other powers. | | **U.S. falls behind in core AI hardware** (e.g., China dominates advanced chips) | AI becomes a strategic equalizer → U.S. loses some military and economic edge; other powers gain leverage. | | **Global AI commons** (open‑source, shared standards, multinational labs) | Power becomes more distributed; hegemony weakens, but the U.S. still influences norms. | | **AI‑driven fragmentation** (different regional AI ecosystems, trade blocs) | The world may become a “technological bloc” system; the U.S. could remain a pole but not a sole hegemon. | --- ## 5. Key variables that will determine the outcome | Variable | Why it matters | |----------|----------------| | **Innovation policy** (R&D funding, tax incentives, export controls) | Determines who creates the next breakthrough models and chips. | | **Education & talent flow** | The U.S. has historically attracted global talent; changes in visa policy or prestige could shift flows. | | **Strategic competition vs. cooperation** | Whether AI is weaponized in a new arms race or used for collaborative problem‑solving (climate, health) shapes power dynamics. | | **Regulatory approach** | Over‑regulation could stifle innovation; under‑regulation could create safety and trust issues that undermine soft power. | | **Alliances and technology sharing** | Programs like the “Five Eyes” intelligence sharing, NATO AI initiatives, and joint research projects can amplify U.S. influence. | | **Domestic political stability** | Consistent, long‑term policy signals are crucial for investor and ally confidence. | --- ## 6. Bottom line - **The United States is widely regarded as a hegemonic power** because of its unmatched military reach, financial architecture, technological leadership, and institutional embeddedness. - **Hegemony is not absolute**; it is constantly tested by domestic challenges, the rise of China, and the diffusion of capabilities. - **AI will be a pivotal factor** in whether that hegemony deepens or erodes. The technology’s dual nature—enhancing existing advantages while also being widely accessible—means the outcome will depend on a combination of: 1. **U.S. policy choices** (innovation support, talent attraction, regulation). 2. **Global diffusion patterns** (open‑source releases, state‑led programs, cross‑border collaborations). 3. **Strategic decisions** of other major powers (how they integrate AI into military and economic strategy). If the United States can sustain its lead in AI hardware, model development, and the ecosystems that support them—while also shaping global norms and standards—AI will likely reinforce its hegemonic position, albeit in a more contested, multipolar environment. Conversely, if AI capabilities become more evenly distributed, or if the U.S. loses ground in critical AI components, the technology could act as a catalyst for a more balanced (or even fragmented) international order, diminishing the United States’ relative dominance.

This is a rich question, so let me take it in two parts. ## Is the US a hegemon? I'd say "hegemonic" is still broadly accurate, but it's a more contested and conditional hegemony than in the 1990s. Consider the dimensions: **Where US dominance remains strong:** - **Military**: No rival comes close to global power projection (11 carrier groups, ~750 overseas bases, unmatched air and naval capabilities). - **Financial**: The dollar still accounts for ~58% of global reserves and the vast majority of trade invoicing and SWIFT transactions. No serious alternative exists yet. - **Institutional**: The US still anchors the IMF, World Bank, NATO, and much of the global trading system. - **Innovation**: It leads in software, biotech, aerospace, and—crucially—frontier AI. - **Cultural/ideational**: English-language media, universities, and consumer brands retain enormous reach. **Where the picture is murkier:** - **Economic weight**: The US share of global GDP has declined from ~40% in 1960 to ~25% today (in nominal terms). China is the larger economy by PPP and the dominant trading partner for most countries. - **Manufacturing**: The US depends heavily on East Asian supply chains, especially semiconductors. - **Political cohesion**: Domestic polarization arguably weakens America's ability to sustain long-term strategy, and its willingness to underwrite global public goods has visibly eroded (trade wars, alliance skepticism, institutional withdrawal in various forms). - **Competitors**: China offers a genuine alternative model in a way the USSR never did economically. So my view: the US is a hegemon in *capabilities* but increasingly behaves as one of several great powers in *conduct*. "Hegemonic" describes the residue of unipolarity more than the current operating reality. ## Will AI change that? AI is interesting because it cuts both ways: **Factors favoring continued US primacy:** - The frontier of AI development is heavily American (OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, Meta) or US-aligned (DeepMind in the UK). - The chokepoints are largely American or controllable by the US: Nvidia's chips, ASML's EUV machines (Dutch, but geopolitically aligned), cloud infrastructure, and the design software layer. - US export controls on advanced chips represent a deliberate attempt to weaponize this lead. - If AI delivers major productivity gains, early adopters could compound their advantage. **Factors pointing the other way:** - **Diffusion**: AI knowledge spreads fast. DeepSeek's efficiency gains showed that export controls slow but don't stop competitors, and open-weight models erode moats. - **China's strengths**: It leads in AI application deployment, has massive data and engineering talent, and dominates the physical supply chain (rare earths, battery tech, and increasingly mature-node chips). - **Energy and compute**: AI demands enormous electricity. Whether the US can build power generation fast enough is a genuine open question; its grid and permitting processes are slow. - **Hegemony isn't just tech**: Even if the US "wins" AI, that doesn't automatically restore dollar dominance, fix domestic politics, or rebuild manufacturing. **My tentative take**: AI is more likely to *entrench* US advantages for the next decade or so—because compute, capital, and talent are currently so concentrated—than to overturn them. But it could also accelerate the shift from a unipolar to a more bipolar or networked world if China closes the gap, since AI is the one technology where China has the scale, talent, and state commitment to compete seriously. The deeper risk to US hegemony probably isn't losing the AI race—it's that AI amplifies internal instability (labor disruption, misinformation, concentration of wealth) faster than it amplifies external power. What draws you to the question—the economic side, the military dimension, or something else?
OpenRouter: Fusionreleased 2026-06-13 · captured 2026-11-14 · 2,159 tokens (775 in / 1,384 out) · $-2159 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
**Is the US hegemonic?** Mostly yes, though less than it was, and unevenly across domains. **Where US dominance is still clear:** - **Military:** The US spends more on defense than roughly the next nine countries combined. It has about 750 overseas bases, an unmatched alliance network (NATO, Japan, South Korea, Australia), and it's the only power that can project force anywhere in the world. - **Finance:** The dollar is used in nearly 90% of foreign-exchange transactions and makes up about 58% of global reserves. US control over dollar clearing makes its sanctions powerful in a way no other country's are. - **Institutions and culture:** The US had an outsized hand in designing the IMF, World Bank and WTO. American universities, tech platforms and entertainment still dominate globally. **Where the picture is weaker:** - **Economic weight:** China is larger in purchasing-power terms. It's the world's dominant manufacturer and the top trading partner of most countries. - **Limits of power:** The US struggled in Iraq and Afghanistan and can't dictate outcomes in Ukraine, Gaza or the South China Sea. - **Rival blocs:** BRICS, Chinese-led lending and the "Global South" increasingly hedge between powers. - **Self-inflicted wear:** Political polarization, tariffs and doubts about the reliability of US commitments push allies to diversify. So I'd call the US the leading power in a world that's becoming multipolar. It's still the hegemon on military and financial measures, but not the near-total unipolar power of the 1990s. Whether "hegemony" is the right word depends on whether you mean being the strongest or being able to set the rules. The US is clearly the first. Its ability to do the second is shrinking. **Will AI change it?** It could cut either way. I think the most likely result is that AI strengthens the US position in the short term but makes the long term less certain. *Reasons AI could reinforce US power:* - The leading frontier labs (OpenAI, Anthropic, Google DeepMind, Meta) are American, and so is most of the AI investment money. - The US and its allies control the chip chokepoints: Nvidia, plus TSMC in Taiwan, ASML in the Netherlands and US design software. Export controls on advanced chips to China use exactly this advantage. - If AI significantly boosts productivity, military capability or scientific discovery, whoever leads gets compounding returns. *Reasons it might erode US power:* - **Diffusion:** Models leak, get copied or are released openly. China's DeepSeek showed that capable models can be built more cheaply and with restricted chips. AI advantages may be short-lived, more like the internet than nuclear weapons. - **China's other strengths:** China has more electricity generation capacity, more manufacturing scale for robotics and AI hardware, lots of data, and a state able to push adoption quickly. - **Who uses it best:** Historically, power has often gone to the country that applies a technology best, not the one that invented it. Britain invented much of industrial technology, but the US and Germany used it at scale. - **Weakening old foundations:** AI-enabled cyberattacks, drones and disinformation are cheap and asymmetric. They could undercut expensive traditional military dominance and the stability of democracies. - **Domestic disruption:** If AI causes job upheaval and inequality without good policy, it could weaken US cohesion from the inside. *Wildcards:* If transformative or "general" AI arrives fast and one actor gets a decisive lead, it could lock in dominance more firmly than anything in history. That might be the US, China, or even companies rather than states. If progress is gradual and spreads widely, AI is more likely to speed up multipolarity. **My bottom line:** The US is still the world's leading power, but its hegemony is being contested. AI is probably the most important variable for the next 20 years. The US starts ahead, but chips, energy, adoption speed and domestic stability will matter more than who builds the best model first. Are you more interested in the economic, military or governance side of this?

It depends on what you mean by “hegemonic.” If you mean a state with disproportionate influence over the international order, the United States clearly fits that description today. If you mean a single power that can unilaterally dictate outcomes anywhere in the world, then the label is much weaker. ### Why the US is often called hegemonic Most scholars who call the US hegemonic point to structural dominance in several areas: * **Military:** The US spends more on defense than the next several countries combined, maintains a global network of bases and alliances, and possesses unique power-projection capabilities. * **Finance and economy:** The dollar remains the world’s primary reserve currency, giving the US outsized influence over trade, sanctions, and monetary conditions far beyond its borders. * **Technology and innovation:** American firms lead in cloud computing, advanced semiconductors, AI research, biotechnology, and space, and US universities attract much of the world’s top scientific talent. * **Institutions and norms:** The US played the central role in building the post-1945 international order and still exerts enormous influence in institutions like the IMF, World Bank, NATO, and the UN. * **Culture and media:** American media, entertainment, and consumer brands shape global culture and narratives to an extent unmatched by any other country. So, yes, in the sense of being the most powerful actor across the widest range of domains, the US is still the closest thing to a hegemon in the current system. ### But the label is contested Many analysts argue we are no longer in a unipolar world. The reasons include: * **China’s rise:** China is the world’s largest economy by purchasing power parity, the largest trading partner for many countries, and a growing military and technological competitor. * **Regional powers:** India, the EU, Russia, Turkey, Saudi Arabia, and others have growing autonomy and often resist US preferences. * **Limits of US power:** Costly wars in Iraq and Afghanistan, political polarization at home, and the inability to prevent crises in Ukraine, the Middle East, or the Indo-Pacific show that American dominance is not limitless. * **Economic interdependence:** Unlike a classic empire, the US cannot simply command other major economies; it has to bargain, coerce, or build coalitions. So a more cautious formulation is that the US remains the **leading global power** and the principal architect of the existing international order, but it operates in an increasingly **contested, multipolar environment**. ### Will AI change that? AI could push the balance in either direction. The net effect is uncertain and depends heavily on policy choices, not just technology. **Arguments that AI could reinforce US dominance:** * **Existing advantages:** The US currently leads in frontier AI labs, cloud infrastructure, venture capital, top AI researchers, and advanced chip design. AI may amplify these advantages. * **Military applications:** AI-enhanced command, surveillance, autonomous systems, and cyber capabilities could widen the gap between the US military and most rivals. * **Economic productivity:** If US firms capture the productivity gains from AI first, they could reinforce American economic and financial centrality. * **Standard-setting power:** US and allied decisions on AI regulation, export controls, and safety norms may shape how the rest of the world adopts AI. **Arguments that AI could erode US dominance:** * **China’s AI ambitions:** China has vast data, a large domestic market, state-directed investment, and a strategy of military-civil fusion. It could become a co-leader or even surpass the US in certain AI domains. * **Diffusion of power:** AI can lower the cost of advanced cyberattacks, drone warfare, propaganda, and surveillance. Smaller states or non-state actors could challenge US interests asymmetrically. * **Domestic disruption:** If AI causes major job displacement, inequality, or political instability in the US without effective policy responses, it could weaken the country’s social and economic foundation. * **Erosion of soft power:** AI-generated disinformation and polarization could undermine democratic institutions and the credibility of the US model abroad. ### Bottom line It is reasonable to describe the US as a hegemonic or at least **preeminent** power today, provided you recognize that its dominance is partial and increasingly contested. AI is unlikely to automatically end US dominance, but it will be one of the most important arenas of great-power competition in the coming decades. Whether it reinforces American leadership, accelerates the rise of China, or diffuses power more broadly will depend less on the technology itself than on who controls the chips, data, talent, and rules that govern AI.
Anthropic: Claude Fable Latestreleased 2026-06-09 · captured 2026-11-14 · 930 tokens (52 in / 878 out) · $0.0355 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
**Is the US a hegemon?** I'd say yes, with qualifications. It depends on which definition you use. In the loose sense of being the single most powerful state, with unmatched ability to shape international rules and institutions, the US still qualifies. It has: - The only military with true global reach, plus an alliance network no rival can match - The dollar as the dominant reserve currency, which gives it extraordinary financial leverage, such as sanctions - Leading positions in technology, capital markets, and higher education In the strict sense of a power that can reliably set and enforce the rules of the international system, the picture is weaker. China is a near-peer economically and increasingly a military peer in its own region. Countries like India, Turkey, and the Gulf states hedge rather than align. The US has struggled to get its preferred outcomes in places like Afghanistan and Ukraine, and in trade disputes. Its own commitment to the order it built has also become less consistent, which matters because hegemony rests partly on others seeing the leadership as legitimate and predictable. So a common framing among scholars is that the unipolar moment of the 1990s has ended, but nothing has replaced the US at the center. It is something like a contested or partial hegemon. **Will AI change that?** It could, but I think the direction is uncertain. There are a few competing possibilities: - **AI reinforces US primacy.** American firms lead in frontier models, the US and its allies control key chokepoints in advanced chips, and the US has deep capital markets to fund the buildout. If AI delivers large productivity or military advantages, the leader benefits most. - **AI narrows the gap.** China is a close second, with strengths in engineering talent, energy infrastructure, manufacturing, and rapid deployment. AI capabilities also tend to diffuse quickly. Open models and efficiency gains mean a lead may be measured in months, not decades. - **Adoption matters more than invention.** Historically, the powers that benefited most from general-purpose technologies like electricity were those that diffused them through their economies, not necessarily those that invented them. On that measure, the race is open. - **AI shifts power away from states.** A handful of private companies control the frontier, which complicates the traditional state-centric picture of hegemony. My best guess is that AI is more likely to intensify US–China competition than to settle it decisively. But if AI progress turns out to be as transformative as some predict, that would be the kind of shock that could reshuffle the order in either direction.

# US Hegemony and the AI Question ## Is the US a hegemonic power? **Largely yes, but it's contested and eroding.** The case for: - **Military**: Unmatched global reach—hundreds of overseas bases, dominant navy, ~40% of world military spending - **Economic**: Dollar remains the global reserve currency (~60% of reserves), US controls key financial infrastructure (SWIFT access, sanctions power) - **Technological**: Dominance in software, semiconductor design, and leading AI labs - **Institutional/cultural**: Shaped the post-WWII order (UN, IMF, NATO); soft power through media, universities, English The case against (or for "declining hegemony"): - China's economy rivals the US (larger by purchasing power parity) - Multipolarity is growing: BRICS expansion, de-dollarization efforts, regional powers acting independently - Domestic polarization undermines consistent foreign policy - Failed interventions (Iraq, Afghanistan) damaged credibility **My read**: The US is still the *only* state with truly global power projection, but it's a weaker hegemon than in 1995—more "first among great powers" than unchallenged leader. ## Will AI change this? AI could cut either way: **Reinforcing US dominance:** - Leading AI labs (OpenAI, Anthropic, Google DeepMind) are American - US controls the semiconductor chokepoint via export controls (NVIDIA chips, allied pressure on ASML/TSMC) - Attracts global AI talent - If AI drives massive productivity gains, first-mover advantages compound **Undermining it:** - China is a serious competitor (strong research output, state investment, data advantages) and export controls may accelerate its self-sufficiency - AI may diffuse faster than past technologies—open-source models erode moats - AI-enabled asymmetric capabilities (cyber, disinformation, autonomous weapons) could let smaller actors punch above their weight - If AI disrupts US labor markets and politics badly, internal instability could matter more than external competition **A wildcard**: If transformative AI arrives, the key actors might be *companies and compute-holders* as much as states—potentially a different kind of power structure altogether. ## Bottom line AI is more likely to be the *decisive arena* of hegemonic competition than an automatic equalizer or entrencher. The US enters with real advantages, but hegemony built on a single technological edge is fragile—Britain's industrial lead didn't last forever either. **Step‑by‑step reasoning** 1. **Define “hegemonic power.”** - In international‑relations theory a *hegemon* is a state that possesses the material capability (military, economic, technological) and the willingness to shape the rules, norms, and institutions of the global order. - Classic examples: Britain in the 19th century, the United States after World II (the “Pax Americana”). 2. **Assess the United States today against that definition.** | Dimension | Current U.S. Position | Comments | |-----------|----------------------|----------| | **Military** | Largest defense budget (~$900 bn), global network of bases, power‑projection capability (carrier groups, nuclear triad). | Still unmatched in conventional power projection, though gaps appear in hypersonics, space, and cyber. | | **Economy** | Largest nominal GDP (~$27 tn), dominant reserve‑currency (USD ≈ 60 % of global reserves), deep capital markets. | Relative share of world GDP has fallen from ~30 % (1990) to ~24 % (2023); China’s PPP‑GDP now exceeds the U.S. | | **Technology & Innovation** | Leader in AI, semiconductors (design), biotech, quantum, aerospace. | China is catching fast in AI research output, 5G, and certain hardware supply chains. | | **Institutional Influence** | Core architect of UN, IMF, World Bank, WTO, NATO, G7/G20; sets standards (e.g., financial, internet governance). | Rising contestation: alternative institutions (AIIB, BRICS‑bank), regional trade blocs, “rules‑based order” debates. | | **Soft Power** | Cultural exports, higher‑education draw, democratic‑values appeal. | Declining trust in some regions; competition from Chinese cultural diplomacy and digital platforms. | | **Political Will** | Generally willing to lead alliances, enforce norms (e.g., sanctions, freedom‑of‑navigation ops). | Domestic polarization, “America First” rhetoric, and fatigue from prolonged wars create ambivalence. | **Bottom line:** The U.S. still *fits* the textbook definition of a hegemon, but its *margin* over the next‑closest competitor (China) has narrowed considerably across several dimensions. 3. **Identify the main challenges to U.S. hegemony.** - **Rise of China** – economic size, state‑directed tech investment, Belt‑and‑Road infrastructure, growing military reach. - **Multipolar diffusion** – EU, India, Japan, Brazil, and regional powers increasingly assert autonomy. - **Erosion of the liberal‑order consensus** – disputes over trade rules, human‑rights norms, climate commitments. - **Domestic constraints** – fiscal deficits, political polarization, debt‑ceiling brinkmanship, and public war‑weariness. - **Technological diffusion** – dual‑use technologies (AI, quantum, biotech) spread faster than any single state can control. 4. **How could AI reshape the balance?** | Potential AI Effect | Mechanism | Likely Impact on U.S. Hegemony | |----------------------|-----------|--------------------------------| | **Productivity & Economic Growth** | AI‑driven automation, drug discovery, logistics optimization. | If the U.S. captures a disproportionate share of AI‑generated value, its economic lead widens; if diffusion is rapid, the advantage shrinks. | | **Military Capability** | Autonomous systems, decision‑support, cyber offense/defense, swarm drones. | Early‑adopter advantage could reinforce power projection; however, AI lowers entry barriers for rivals (e.g., cheap drones). | | **Standard‑Setting & Governance** | Control of foundational models, data‑governance norms, export controls on chips. | The U.S. (with allies) currently leads standard‑setting bodies (IEEE, ISO, OECD AI Principles). Maintaining that lead sustains institutional hegemony. | | **Talent & Research Ecosystem** | Attraction of top researchers, university‑industry pipelines, open‑source community leadership. | U.S. universities and firms (Google, Microsoft, OpenAI, etc.) still dominate high‑impact publications and talent pools, but China’s state‑directed talent programs are closing the gap. | | **Strategic Vulnerabilities** | Dependence on semiconductor supply chains (TSMC, Samsung), data‑privacy regulations, AI‑enabled disinformation. | Vulnerabilities could be exploited by rivals, eroding confidence in U.S. leadership. | | **Diffusion & “Commoditization”** | Open‑source models, cloud‑based AI services, lower compute costs. | Faster diffusion reduces the *exclusivity* of any one state’s AI edge, pushing the system toward a more multipolar tech landscape. | 5. **Synthesize: Will AI *change* the hegemonic status?** - **Short‑to‑medium term (5‑10 yr):** AI is likely to *reinforce* U.S. advantages **if** the U.S. maintains lead in semiconductor manufacturing (via CHIPS Act, allied fab capacity), retains talent, and shapes global AI governance. - **Long term (10‑20 yr):** The *commoditization* of AI, combined with China’s massive state investment and the EU’s regulatory leadership, will *flatten* the technological playing field. Hegemony will depend less on a single breakthrough and more on the ability to integrate AI across the economy, military, and alliance architecture. - **Structural outcome:** AI alone will not *abolish* U.S. hegemony, but it will *accelerate* the transition toward a **multipolar** or **“heteropolar”** order where several centers (U.S., China, EU, possibly India) possess comparable high‑end AI capabilities and compete over norms and standards. --- ## Final Answer **Yes, the United States still qualifies as a hegemonic power** in the classic sense: it uniquely combines unrivaled global military reach, the world’s largest economy and reserve currency, a dominant position in cutting‑edge technology, and a central role in the institutions that set the rules of the international order. **However, the margin of that hegemony has narrowed.** China’s rapid economic growth, state‑directed technological push, and expanding military capabilities, together with a more assertive set of middle powers and domestic political constraints in the U.S., have turned the post‑Cold‑War unipolar moment into a *contested* hegemony. **Artificial intelligence will be a decisive factor in the next decade.** - In the **near term**, AI is likely to *strengthen* the U.S. position because the United States (with its allies) leads in foundational research, talent attraction, semiconductor design, and the setting of global AI standards and export controls. - In the **longer term**, AI’s rapid diffusion, open‑source ecosystems, and the massive state‑led investments of rivals—especially China—will *commoditize* many AI capabilities, lowering the barrier for other powers to field comparable military, economic, and surveillance tools. This erosion of technological exclusivity will push the international system toward a **multipolar/heteropolar** configuration where several states wield comparable AI power and vie for influence over the norms that govern its use. **Bottom line:** The U.S. remains a hegemon today, but AI will *reshape* the foundations of that hegemony. Whether the United States retains a *lead* or merely becomes *first among equals* will depend on how successfully it sustains its innovation ecosystem, secures critical hardware supply chains, and leads the creation of widely‑accepted AI governance frameworks. If it falters on any of those fronts, AI will accelerate the transition to a more balanced, multipolar world order.

Whether the United States is currently a "hegemonic power" and how AI will affect that status are subjects of intense debate in international relations, economics, and technology policy. There is no single consensus, but here is a structured analysis of the prevailing arguments. --- ### Part 1: Is the US Currently a Hegemonic Power? **The Short Answer:** Most scholars agree the US remains the **sole global superpower** and the **primary architect of the current international order**, but they increasingly describe its position as **"relative decline," "contested hegemony,"** or **"partial hegemony"** rather than the unipolar dominance seen in the 1990s. #### Arguments for "Yes" (Structural Dominance Persists) 1. **Military Reach:** The US is the only nation capable of projecting sustained military power globally (carrier strike groups, global basing network, nuclear triad, advanced logistics). It spends more on defense than the next ~10 countries combined. 2. **Financial Centrality:** The US Dollar (USD) dominates global reserves (~58–60%), trade invoicing, and cross-border banking. The US effectively controls the "plumbing" of global finance (SWIFT, correspondent banking), granting immense sanctions power. 3. **Alliance Architecture:** The US sits at the hub of a unique "hub-and-spokes" alliance system (NATO, AUKUS, Quad, bilateral treaties with Japan, Korea, Australia, Philippines, etc.). No rival has a comparable network of formal mutual defense commitments. 4. **Technological & Innovation Ecosystem:** The US leads in foundational sectors: semiconductor design (though not fabrication), AI foundation models, cloud computing, biotech, and aerospace. It attracts the lion's share of global venture capital and top-tier talent. 5. **Demographic & Energy Advantage:** Unlike China, Russia, Europe, or Japan, the US has a relatively favorable demographic trajectory (supplemented by immigration) and is a net energy exporter (oil & gas), providing strategic resilience. #### Arguments for "No" / "Contested" (Erosion of Hegemony) 1. **Rise of Peer Competitors (China):** China is a **peer competitor in manufacturing** (world’s factory), a **near-peer in specific military domains** (hypersonics, shipbuilding, Indo-Pacific A2/AD), and a **leader in specific tech** (5G, EV batteries, solar, high-speed rail). It offers an alternative development model (BRI) and payment systems (CIPS). 2. **Multipolar Economic Reality:** The G7 share of global GDP (PPP) has fallen below the BRICS+ share. The US cannot unilaterally dictate outcomes at the WTO, G20, or UN Security Council as it once could. 3. **Weaponization of Interdependence Backfiring:** Frequent use of financial sanctions (Russia, Iran, Venezuela) and export controls (chips to China) accelerates "de-dollarization" efforts and the building of parallel supply chains/financial rails by adversaries *and* partners (e.g., EU strategic autonomy, India’s multi-alignment). 4. **Domestic Polarization & Institutional Decay:** Political dysfunction, debt trajectory, and polarization undermine the credibility of US commitments and the attractiveness of its governance model (soft power erosion). 5. **Inability to Enforce Order:** The US failed to deter Russia’s invasion of Ukraine (initially), struggles to manage the Israel-Gaza conflict, and cannot prevent Houthi attacks on global shipping without massive coalition effort—signaling limits to coercive power. **Synthesis:** The US enjoys **"command of the commons"** (sea, space, cyberspace, finance) and **structural power** (setting rules), but it lacks the **compellence** to force outcomes against determined great powers in their own neighborhoods. It is a **"lonely superpower"**—unrivaled globally, but constrained regionally. --- ### Part 2: Will AI Change That? AI is widely considered a **General Purpose Technology (GPT)** akin to electricity or the steam engine. It will almost certainly **reshape the distribution of power**, but the *direction* of that shift for the US is contested. #### Scenarios Where AI **Strengthens** US Hegemony 1. **The "Compute Moat" & Export Controls:** The US currently controls the choke points of the AI hardware stack: **EUV lithography (ASML - Dutch but US-aligned), GPU design (Nvidia), and cloud infrastructure (AWS/Azure/GCP).** Current export controls (Oct 2022, 2023 updates) aim to freeze China out of advanced compute. If the US maintains this "compute governance" lead, it locks in a structural advantage for decades. 2. **Productivity & Economic Surge:** If AI drives a massive Total Factor Productivity (TFP) boom in the US (solving Baumol’s cost disease in healthcare, education, construction, R&D), the US economy could widen the GDP gap with rivals, funding military/tech dominance. 3. **Military-Technical Revolution:** The US DoD (Replicator Initiative, CCA - Collaborative Combat Aircraft) is integrating AI into autonomous swarms, decision-support, and ISR. If the US operationalizes "algorithmic warfare" first, it restores conventional deterrence credibility in the Indo-Pacific. 4. **Talent Magnet:** The US still attracts the vast majority of top-tier AI researchers (Stanford AI Index: ~60% of top researchers work in US institutions, many foreign-born). Immigration policy is the variable here. 5. **Standard Setting:** US firms (OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, Meta, Microsoft) define the frontier. US safety/governance frameworks (NIST, Executive Orders) risk becoming global *de facto* standards (the "Brussels Effect" but for AI). #### Scenarios Where AI **Erodes** US Hegemony 1. **Diffusion & Open Source:** Model weights leak (LLaMA, Mistral, DeepSeek, Yi). Inference costs plummet. The "moat" shrinks from years to months. Commoditized AI empowers middle powers and non-state actors, diluting the advantage of the frontier leader. 2. **China’s "New Whole-of-Nation System":** China mobilizes state capital, data advantages (surveillance/smart city), and application-layer speed (industrial AI, military-civil fusion). They may lose the *frontier model* race but win the *deployment/industrialization* race, boosting their manufacturing/military efficiency faster than the US. 3. **Energy & Infrastructure Bottlenecks:** Training GPT-5/6/7 requires Gigawatts of reliable power and massive data center build-out. The US grid is aging, permitting is slow (NEPA), and energy costs are rising. China builds coal/nuclear/renewables + grid infrastructure at state-directed speed. **Compute requires energy; energy sovereignty is now AI sovereignty.** 4. **Semiconductor Geography (TSMC Risk):** The US designs chips; Taiwan (TSMC) makes 90% of advanced logic. A Taiwan Strait crisis destroys the US AI hardware supply chain instantly. The CHIPS Act aims to fix this, but US fab yield/cost competitiveness vs. TSMC remains unproven at scale. 5. **Governance Paralysis & Trust Crisis:** Deepfakes, disinformation, algorithmic bias, and labor displacement could deepen US political polarization, trigger regulatory overreaction (stifling innovation), or erode democratic legitimacy—undermining the *domestic foundation* of hegemony. 6. **Financial Disruption:** AI-driven algorithmic trading, DeFi, or CBDC competition (Digital Yuan/mBRIDGE) could accelerate fragmentation of the dollar system if the US fails to innovate in fintech/payments. #### The "Wild Cards" * **AGI/ASI Timeline:** If Artificial General Intelligence arrives soon (3–5 years) and is **alignable**, the first mover gains a potentially decisive, perhaps insurmountable, strategic advantage (economic/military/scientific). If it is **unalignable**, it poses an existential risk to *all* state power. * **AI in Nuclear Command & Control:** Integration of AI into NC3 (Nuclear Command, Control, Communications) by any major power changes crisis stability and deterrence calculus fundamentally. --- ### Conclusion **Do I "agree" the US is hegemonic?** **Yes, but with the qualifier "contested."** The US holds a **structural lead** in the *foundations* of 21st-century power (finance, alliances, innovation ecosystem, energy, demographics), but it has lost the *unipolar margin* it enjoyed post-1991. It is a **hegemon under pressure**, managing a transition

# US Hegemony and the AI Question ## Is the US currently a hegemonic power? The label is largely accurate, though with caveats. The US still possesses the most comprehensive set of hegemonic attributes: - **Military**: Unmatched global force projection and alliance network - **Economic**: Largest nominal GDP and deepest capital markets - **Financial**: Dollar's reserve currency status and SWIFT leverage - **Institutional**: Leadership of IMF, World Bank, and the post-WWII order - **Technological**: Dominance in frontier sectors (until recently) - **Cultural/ideological**: Soft power through media, universities, and political model However, hegemony is *relative*, not absolute. The post-Cold War "unipolar moment" is clearly over. China is a peer competitor in several dimensions, and the system is trending toward multipolarity. The US can still set the global agenda in many domains, but its ability to do so unilaterally has eroded. ## Will AI change this? AI is genuinely novel in how it might reshape power distributions, and the answer is **both reinforcing and destabilizing**—depending on how the next decade unfolds. ### Ways AI could reinforce US hegemony - **Current lead in foundation models, chips (NVIDIA), and cloud infrastructure** gives the US a meaningful head start - **Capital markets and talent attraction** remain structural advantages - **AI-enhanced military capabilities** (intelligence, autonomous systems, cyber) could widen the conventional gap - **Export controls on advanced semiconductors** are an attempt to weaponize this lead - **Productivity gains** could boost economic growth and fiscal capacity ### Ways AI could undermine US hegemony - **Diffusability**: Unlike nuclear technology, AI is largely software-based and replicable. Open-weight models (e.g., DeepSeek, Llama) are rapidly closing capability gaps - **China's pace**: China is no longer a laggard—it's competitive in computer vision, NLP, and increasingly in frontier models, with state-directed coordination - **Empowerment of smaller actors**: AI lowers the barrier for cyber operations, autonomous weapons, and sophisticated disinformation, eroding the advantage of large state militaries - **Private sector power**: Concentration of AI capability in companies (OpenAI, Google, Anthropic) creates a new axis of power that doesn't map neatly onto state hegemony - **Energy and infrastructure constraints**: The compute race requires massive electricity and capital—potentially a vulnerability - **Regulatory fragmentation**: Different governance models (US laissez-faire, EU precautionary, China state-directed) could create competing AI ecosystems ## Net assessment AI is unlikely to be a single decisive variable. More likely, it will **accelerate trends already underway**: 1. The US-China rivalry will intensify around AI as the central theater of competition 2. The "diffusion" problem means the US cannot easily monopolize AI the way it monopolized, say, the dollar 3. AI may produce a *different* kind of power distribution—not traditional hegemony, but a fragmented landscape where states, corporations, and open-source communities all wield influence 4. The country that integrates AI most effectively into its economy and military will gain the most, and that race is genuinely close The most honest answer: **AI will not determine whether the US remains hegemonic, but it will be the primary arena in which that question is decided over the next 20 years.** The US enters this contest with real advantages, but fewer than it had in previous technology races—and the outcome is far from assured.

The United States is widely described as a hegemonic power, a characterization that rests on its overwhelming dominance across multiple dimensions of global affairs. This hegemony is evident in its military capabilities, economic weight, technological leadership, cultural influence, and institutional power. These pillars reinforce each other, creating a self‑sustaining system of hegemony. ### How AI Could Transform the Landscape AI is a general‑purpose technology with the potential to disrupt every sector, from defense to economics to society. Its impact on U.S. hegemony can be examined through three lenses: reinforcement, diffusion, and redistribution. #### 1. Reinforcement of U.S. Hegemony - **Leadership in AI Research**: The U.S. currently leads in AI talent, funding, and corporate activity (e.g., Google, Microsoft, OpenAI). If this continues, AI could amplify existing advantages—more efficient military systems, smarter economic planning, and greater productivity. - **Military Applications**: AI‑driven autonomous weapons, intelligence analysis, and logistics could cement U.S. military superiority, making it harder for rivals to catch up. - **Economic Growth**: AI could boost U.S. GDP, maintain the dollar’s dominance through fintech innovations, and create new markets controlled by American firms. #### 2. Diffusion of Power - **Democratization of AI**: Open‑source frameworks, cloud services, and global talent pools mean that other countries and non‑state actors can also develop advanced AI. This could erode the U.S. monopoly on cutting‑edge capabilities. - **Decentralized Threats**: AI lowers the barrier for malicious use (e.g., cyberattacks, disinformation), empowering smaller states, terrorist groups, or individuals to challenge U.S. interests asymmetrically. - **Economic Disruption**: Automation may displace workers worldwide, potentially destabilizing economies that rely on low‑cost labor, including those of U.S. allies and adversaries alike. #### 3. Redistribution Toward Other Powers - **China’s AI Ambitions**: China has made AI a national priority, investing heavily and already leads in areas like facial recognition and AI‑driven surveillance. If China matches or surpasses U.S. AI capabilities, the balance of power could shift, especially in the Indo‑Pacific region. - **Multipolar AI Ecosystem**: The EU, with its strong regulatory framework and research base, could carve out a distinct AI niche, promoting values like privacy and ethics that contrast with U.S. and Chinese models. This could lead to competing standards and spheres of influence. ### Scenarios for the Future - **Status‑Quo Extension**: The U.S. maintains its lead, using AI to deepen hegemony. This requires sustained investment, immigration policies that attract talent, and effective governance of AI risks. - **Balanced Multipolarity**: AI capabilities spread relatively evenly among major powers, leading to a more multipolar world where no single state can dominate. Hegemony gives way to great‑power competition and cooperation. - **Fragmented Order**: AI development fragments along geopolitical lines, with distinct “AI blocs” (U.S., China, EU) operating under different rules, potentially causing friction and reducing global integration. ### Conclusion The United States today undeniably holds hegemonic power. Whether AI will change that depends on how the technology evolves and how effectively the U.S. and other nations manage its development. AI could reinforce U.S. dominance if America sustains its edge, but it also holds the promise of diffusing power and enabling new challengers. The ultimate outcome will be shaped by policy choices, investment patterns, and the ability of societies to govern AI responsibly. In any case, AI is set to be a major factor in the future distribution of global power.

By that standard, the US qualifies more than any other country: - **Military**: The US accounts for roughly 40% of global defense spending. It maintains a global network of bases and alliances that no other power approaches. If one side achieves a decisive lead in transformative AI (especially systems that meaningfully accelerate scientific discovery or military capabilities), the power differential could widen or narrow rapidly. Historical analogies like nuclear weapons or the internet show that technological leaps can shift relative power within a decade or two. - Diffusion risk: Advanced AI capabilities may spread more easily than previous strategic technologies, potentially empowering middle powers or even non-state actors in ways that reduce the value of traditional hegemony. ### Likely Trajectory The most probable near-term outcome is that AI **extends** rather than ends US primacy, but only if the US maintains its lead in the highest-end capabilities and integrates them effectively. The United States is widely regarded as a hegemonic power due to its unparalleled military strength, economic influence, cultural reach, and leadership in global institutions. This status stems from decades of strategic positioning, technological innovation, and geopolitical maneuvering. However, the rise of artificial intelligence (AI) introduces both opportunities and challenges that could reshape this dynamic. ### **Will AI Reinforce U.S. Hegemony?** 1. **Technological Leadership**: The U.S. currently leads in AI research, venture capital investment, and talent concentration (e.g., Silicon Valley). Dominance in foundational technologies like machine learning and semiconductors could solidify its economic and military edge. 2. **Military Applications**: AI-driven advancements in autonomous systems, cybersecurity, and data analytics may enhance U.S. defense capabilities, maintaining deterrence and operational superiority. 3. **Economic Leverage**: AI’s role in boosting productivity and creating high-value industries could strengthen the U.S. dollar’s dominance and attract global talent, reinforcing soft power. ### **Could AI Undermine U.S. Hegemony?** 1. **Rise of Competitors**: China’s state-backed AI initiatives and investments in critical infrastructure (e.g., 5G, quantum computing) pose a direct challenge. Other nations, such as the EU or India, may leverage AI to reduce dependency on U.S.-led systems. 2. **Decentralization of Power**: Open-source AI tools and democratized access to technology could empower smaller states or non-state actors, diluting traditional hierarchies. 3. **Ethical and Governance Risks**: If the U.S. fails to address AI-related concerns (e.g., bias, privacy, job displacement), it risks losing trust in global governance frameworks, allowing rivals to set norms. ### **Other Factors at Play** - **Geopolitical Alliances**: U.S. hegemony depends on maintaining coalitions (e.g., NATO, Quad). AI collaboration or competition within these groups will shape outcomes. - **Global Challenges**: Climate change, pandemics, and inequality may require multilateral solutions, potentially shifting focus from unilateral dominance to cooperative models. - **Internal Stability**: Domestic polarization or economic inequality in the U.S. could weaken its ability to project power, regardless of AI advancements. ### **Conclusion** AI is likely to amplify existing trends rather than single-handedly redefine global power structures. While the U.S. remains well-positioned to leverage AI for continued influence, its hegemony will depend on how effectively it navigates competition, addresses societal risks, and adapts to a multipolar world. The future may see a more fragmented order where AI acts as both a tool of dominance and a catalyst for redistribution of power.

The question of whether the United States is a hegemonic power—and how artificial intelligence (AI) might alter that status—is a central topic in international relations and geopolitics. There is no single consensus, but rather a spectrum of expert opinion. Here is an analysis of the current state of US hegemony and the potential impact of AI. ### Part 1: Is the US currently a hegemonic power? To answer this, one must define "hegemony." In political science, hegemony generally refers to a state’s ability to exert influence over the international system through a combination of economic, military, cultural, and diplomatic power. **Arguments for US Hegemony:** * **Military Dominance:** The US maintains the world’s largest military budget, a global network of bases, and the ability to project power into any region of the world simultaneously. * **Economic Influence:** The US dollar remains the world’s primary reserve currency, giving the US unique leverage over global financial systems (e.g., through sanctions). * **Soft Power:** Despite challenges, American culture, technology, and educational institutions continue to exert significant global influence. * **Alliances:** The US leads the most powerful military alliance in history (NATO) and maintains critical security partnerships in the Indo-Pacific. **Arguments Against (or for "Declining" Hegemony):** * **The Rise of Multipolarity:** Many analysts argue we have moved from a "unipolar" moment (post-Cold War) to a "multipolar" world. The rise of China as a near-peer competitor, the resurgence of Russia, and the growing influence of regional powers like India, Brazil, and Saudi Arabia suggest the US can no longer dictate global outcomes unilaterally. * **Internal Polarization:** Domestic political instability and social polarization are often cited as factors that weaken the US’s ability to project consistent, long-term foreign policy. * **Strategic Overstretch:** Some argue that the US is overextended, trying to maintain influence in too many theaters simultaneously, which creates vulnerabilities. **Conclusion on current status:** Most scholars describe the US as a "declining hegemon" or a "primus inter pares" (first among equals). It remains the most powerful nation, but its ability to unilaterally shape the global order has diminished significantly compared to the 1990s. --- ### Part 2: Will AI change that? AI is widely viewed as a "general-purpose technology"—one that will fundamentally reshape economies, militaries, and societies. Whether it reinforces or undermines US hegemony depends on several factors. #### How AI could reinforce US Hegemony: * **The "First-Mover" Advantage:** The US currently leads in AI research, development, and commercialization. Companies like OpenAI, Google, Microsoft, and NVIDIA are American. If the US maintains this lead, it could secure a massive economic and military advantage, effectively "locking in" its dominance for decades. * **Military Superiority:** AI-driven systems (autonomous drones, advanced cyber-warfare, predictive logistics, and enhanced intelligence analysis) could create a "force multiplier" effect, allowing the US to maintain military superiority even if its raw manpower or industrial capacity is challenged. * **Talent Attraction:** The US remains the primary destination for global AI talent. If this "brain drain" continues, the US will continue to innovate faster than its rivals. #### How AI could undermine US Hegemony: * **The Democratization of Power:** AI is a dual-use technology. While it helps the US, it also lowers the barrier to entry for other nations. Smaller or less wealthy nations could use AI to punch above their weight in cyber-warfare, disinformation, or economic efficiency, potentially eroding the US's relative advantage. * **The "China Challenge":** China has made AI a national priority, investing heavily in state-directed AI development. If China succeeds in integrating AI into its economy and military faster or more effectively than the US, it could neutralize the US's current technological edge. * **Economic Disruption:** AI could cause massive domestic economic shifts. If the US fails to manage the social and economic fallout of AI-driven automation (e.g., job displacement), it could exacerbate the internal political instability that already threatens its global standing. ### The Verdict AI is likely to be a **catalyst for change** rather than a simple stabilizer. * **If the US maintains its lead:** AI will likely act as a "hegemony-extender," allowing the US to maintain its dominance through superior technological and military capabilities. * **If the gap closes:** AI will likely accelerate the transition to a multipolar world. Because AI is so transformative, a shift in AI leadership could lead to a rapid shift in the global balance of power. Ultimately, AI is not a magic wand. It will amplify the existing strengths and weaknesses of the nations that wield it. For the US, the question is not just about developing the best AI, but about whether it can maintain the social cohesion, economic dynamism, and strategic focus necessary to utilize that technology effectively on the world stage.

And I think artificial intelligence will play a major role in determining whether the US maintains that position over the coming decades. However, there is an important distinction between being the world's most powerful country and being a hegemon. AI could reinforce American dominance in some areas while simultaneously weakening its ability to exercise global leadership. ### 1. Is the United States still a hegemonic power? In international relations, a hegemon is a state that possesses sufficient economic, military, technological, and institutional influence to shape the international system. By that definition, the United States remains a hegemonic power, particularly in five areas. **Military power:** The US maintains an unmatched global military presence. It has an extensive network of military bases, alliances, and security partnerships. China is becoming a formidable regional competitor, particularly in East Asia, but it does not possess the same capacity to project military power globally. **Financial power:** The US dollar remains the world's principal reserve and transaction currency. This gives Washington extraordinary influence over global finance, including the ability to impose financial sanctions. American capital markets also remain central to the global economy. **Technological power:** The US dominates important parts of the global technology ecosystem, including advanced semiconductor design, cloud computing, software, and frontier AI development. **Institutional influence:** The US played a central role in establishing the post-1945 international order, including institutions such as the IMF, World Bank, and NATO. Although its influence over these institutions varies, it remains exceptionally important. **Cultural influence:** American universities, entertainment, digital platforms, and scientific institutions continue to shape global culture and intellectual life. Nevertheless, **American hegemony is not equivalent to American supremacy in every domain**. China is a major economic and technological competitor, particularly in manufacturing, industrial infrastructure, and increasingly AI. Other powers, including India and the European Union, are also seeking greater strategic autonomy. Furthermore, American influence increasingly faces resistance, even among some traditional partners. I would therefore characterize the contemporary international system as one of **contested American hegemony**, rather than either unquestioned US dominance or a fully multipolar world. ### 2. Will AI strengthen American hegemony? There are compelling reasons to believe it could. **First, the US has a substantial advantage in the AI ecosystem.** Many of the most influential AI companies are American, including OpenAI, Google, Anthropic, and Meta. The United States also has a particularly strong position in several essential components of AI development: - Advanced semiconductor design, particularly through NVIDIA and AMD. - Hyperscale cloud infrastructure, including Microsoft, Amazon, and Google. - Frontier AI research and development. - Venture capital and access to private investment. - Leading research universities and a strong capacity to attract international talent. These advantages reinforce one another. For example, American AI companies benefit from access to domestic capital, computing infrastructure, and specialized expertise. This creates a powerful ecosystem that competitors may find difficult to replicate. If AI becomes a general-purpose technology comparable in importance to electricity or the internet, leadership in AI could translate into substantial economic and geopolitical advantages. **Second, AI could increase American economic productivity.** Historically, technological leadership has been an important foundation of hegemonic power. British industrial leadership helped sustain Britain's nineteenth-century global influence. American leadership in mass production, computing, and information technology reinforced US dominance during the twentieth century. AI could produce a similar transformation. If American firms adopt AI more successfully than their international competitors, the US could experience faster productivity growth, greater corporate profitability, and increased economic influence. However, this outcome is not guaranteed. Developing frontier AI models and successfully deploying AI across an entire economy are different capabilities. **Third, AI could strengthen American military superiority.** AI has potentially significant applications in intelligence analysis, logistics, cyber operations, autonomous systems, and military decision support. A country capable of integrating AI effectively into its defense infrastructure could gain major strategic advantages. The United States possesses both a sophisticated defense industry and a leading AI sector, putting it in a strong position to benefit from these developments. Yet military AI also creates vulnerabilities. Relatively inexpensive autonomous systems could challenge expensive conventional military platforms, potentially reducing some traditional American advantages. ### 3. But AI could also undermine American hegemony This is where the question becomes especially interesting. **AI might simultaneously increase American technological power while reducing the broader importance of traditional hegemonic structures.** There are three particularly important mechanisms. #### A. AI could accelerate China's rise China possesses several advantages that should not be underestimated. These include an enormous industrial base, substantial engineering capabilities, major technology companies, and considerable state capacity to coordinate infrastructure investment. While the US has important advantages in frontier AI development, China is highly competitive in AI research, open-weight models, and practical applications. China may also benefit from its strength in manufacturing, robotics, and physical infrastructure. Consider two hypothetical scenarios: In the first, AI primarily creates value through software, digital services, and intellectual property. American companies could capture a substantial share of that value. In the second, AI creates enormous value by transforming factories, logistics networks, energy systems, and robotics. Under that scenario, China's industrial capabilities could become an especially important advantage. **The geopolitical winner of AI may therefore depend less on who develops the most sophisticated model and more on who integrates AI most effectively into the real economy.** This distinction is crucial. #### B. AI could make smaller countries more powerful Historically, economic and military power have depended heavily on population, industrial capacity, natural resources, and capital. AI could alter some of those relationships. For instance, smaller countries might use advanced AI systems to compensate for shortages of highly skilled labor. AI could improve their ability to conduct scientific research, develop sophisticated software, manage infrastructure, and provide public services. If highly capable AI becomes widely accessible, countries may be able to acquire capabilities that previously required much larger populations or economies. This could reduce certain advantages traditionally enjoyed by great powers. Only a handful of countries can maintain frontier capabilities. Power becomes concentrated. The US and China emerge as the dominant AI powers, while other countries become dependent on their technological ecosystems. This could reinforce a bipolar international order. **Scenario 2: AI resembles the internet.** In this scenario, powerful AI capabilities become increasingly inexpensive, accessible, and widely distributed. Countries and businesses across the world can use advanced AI without necessarily controlling its original development. The economic benefits spread internationally. While the US might retain technological leadership, its ability to translate that leadership into geopolitical control would be limited. This could contribute to a more multipolar international system. **Scenario 3: A hybrid system emerges.** This is the outcome I currently consider most plausible. Frontier AI development remains concentrated among a relatively small number of major companies and countries. At the same time, increasingly powerful AI capabilities become widely available through commercial services, open-weight models, and more efficient computing. Under these conditions, the US could retain considerable influence over the technological foundations of AI while other countries gain substantial capabilities through its deployment. That would produce a world characterized by both concentrated technological power and increasingly distributed economic capabilities. ### 5. In such a world, countries that control the underlying AI infrastructure could possess extraordinary influence. But this raises an important distinction. **Would that influence belong to the United States as a state, or to American technology companies?** These are not necessarily the same thing. **Yes, the US qualifies as a hegemonic power today, though with important qualifications.** A hegemon is a state that possesses clear preponderance across military, economic, technological, and institutional dimensions, allowing it to shape the international system more than any other actor. The United States meets this standard on most metrics: - **Military**: It accounts for roughly 40% of global defense spending, maintains a network of alliances and bases that no other power approaches, and leads in power projection (naval carriers, air superiority, precision strike, space, and cyber). - **Economic and financial**: The dollar remains the dominant reserve currency, US capital markets are the deepest, and American firms dominate high-value sectors (tech platforms, semiconductors design, aerospace, pharmaceuticals, energy). - **Institutional and normative**: The US still sets many of the rules in trade, finance, technology standards, and security arrangements, even if enforcement has become patchier. That said, hegemony is not absolute. China has narrowed the gap in manufacturing, infrastructure, and certain technologies, and the world is more multipolar than in the 1990s. US domestic dysfunction, debt trajectory, and alliance management problems also constrain its freedom of action. Still, no other state combines the same breadth and depth of advantages, so the label remains reasonable. **AI’s effect on US hegemony is likely to be reinforcing rather than erosive in the medium term, but the outcome is not predetermined.** Several dynamics point toward continued or even strengthened US advantage: - The US currently leads in frontier AI models, talent concentration, compute infrastructure, and private-sector investment. Leading labs and chip designers remain overwhelmingly American or closely allied. - Military applications (autonomous systems, intelligence fusion, logistics, cyber) favor the side with superior software and data ecosystems; the US military and its industrial base are positioned to integrate these faster than most competitors. - Economic productivity gains from AI would disproportionately benefit the economy that already hosts the most advanced firms and capital markets. Countervailing factors exist: - China is investing heavily and can mobilize state resources at scale; it may achieve parity or localized leads in specific applications (surveillance, manufacturing robotics, certain defense uses). - Diffusion of AI capabilities could empower middle powers or non-state actors, reducing the relative value of traditional US advantages in some domains. - If AI triggers major economic or social disruption inside the US itself, it could weaken the domestic foundations of hegemony. On balance, AI is more likely to extend the period of US primacy than to end it, because the technology amplifies existing strengths in innovation ecosystems, capital allocation, and alliance networks more than it creates a clean break. The decisive variable will be execution: whether the US sustains its lead in foundational models and compute while managing alliance coordination on export controls and standards. If it does, hegemony persists in a new technological form; if it stumbles badly on either front, relative decline accelerates.

The most likely outcome is a **more multipolar world**, where AI enables new centers of power to emerge, but the U.S. remains a key player—just not the *only* one. I'd say the US is still the most powerful state in the system, but whether "hegemon" fits depends on how you define the term. **Evidence for hegemony:** - **Military reach:** No other country projects power globally. It has about 750 overseas bases and treaty alliances across Europe and Asia. - **Finance:** The dollar dominates trade invoicing, reserves, and payments, and US sanctions have real bite because of it. - **Technology:** US firms lead in frontier AI, cloud computing, and chip design, and export controls can slow rivals. - **Institutions:** The US helped build and still anchors the IMF, World Bank, and much of the postwar order. **Evidence against:** - **Relative economic share** has declined from about half of world output after WWII to roughly a quarter today (measured in nominal terms), and China's economy is larger in purchasing-power terms. - **Hegemony requires legitimacy and consent,** not just capability. Wars in Iraq and Afghanistan, and perceptions of double standards, have weakened that. - **Other powers are rising:** China leads in manufacturing and many clean-tech supply chains, India is growing fast, and BRICS and other groupings are building alternatives, though slowly. - **Limited control:** The US often cannot get its preferred outcomes, as in Syria, Ukraine, or Gaza. So I think "leading power in a increasingly contested or multipolar system" describes it better than "hegemon" in the fuller Gramscian or Ikenberry sense, though it retains hegemonic features in finance and military reach. **Will AI change that?** It could go either way, and the near-term and long-term effects differ: - **Reinforcing:** AI runs on compute, capital, and talent, all concentrated in the US. Leading labs and hyperscalers are American, and chip export controls target China's access to advanced semiconductors. If frontier capabilities compound, an early lead could become decisive. - **Eroding:** AI diffuses. DeepSeek showed that a Chinese lab could approach frontier performance with less compute and cheaper methods, and open-weight models spread quickly. Controls on chips may slow rivals but not stop them. China also has strengths in robotics, manufacturing, energy, and deploying AI at scale. What matters more is whether the US keeps its alliances, attracts talent, builds energy and chip capacity, and sets norms others accept.
