Whether the United States currently qualifies as a hegemonic power—and how artificial intelligence (AI) might alter its global standing—is a central subject of debate among international relations scholars, economists, and geopolitical analysts. Rather than a single consensus, experts hold varying views depending on how they measure global power and technological trajectory. ### Is the United States Currently a Hegemonic Power? After the collapse of the Soviet Union, the United States entered what political commentators like Charles Krauthammer termed a "unipolar moment," characterized by unmatched military, economic, and cultural dominance. Today, experts are divided on whether that hegemony remains intact. **Arguments for Continued U.S. Hegemony** Analysts who argue the U.S. remains a hegemon point to structural advantages that no other single nation has matched. Political scientist Stephen Brooks and international relations scholar William Wohlforth have argued in *Foreign Affairs* that the world remains fundamentally unipolar because the gap between the U.S. and its nearest competitors is still vast when combining economic output, military reach, and technological innovation. Proponents of this view highlight: * **Financial Dominance:** Economist Carla Norrlof and others emphasize the enduring power of the U.S. dollar as the world's primary reserve currency, which grants Washington unique leverage over the global financial system, including the ability to enforce sweeping economic sanctions. * **Military and Alliance Networks:** The U.S. maintains a global network of military bases and formal alliances—such as NATO and partnerships in the Indo-Pacific—that no rival possesses. **Arguments that U.S. Hegemony is Over or Declining** Conversely, many scholars argue that the era of American hegemony has ended, giving way to a multipolar or bipolar world. * **The Rise of Peer Competitors:** Realist scholar John Mearsheimer argues that the unipolar era ended around the late 2010s due to the economic and military rise of China, alongside a resurgent Russia seeking to challenge the U.S.-led order. * **Multipolarity and "Non-Polarity":** Richard Haass, former president of the Council on Foreign Relations, has described the modern era as one of "non-polarity" or diffuse power, where regional powers (such as India, Brazil, Turkey, and Gulf states) increasingly act independently of Washington's preferences. Critics of the hegemony thesis also point to domestic political polarization in the U.S. and the growing influence of alternative diplomatic groupings like the expanded BRICS bloc as evidence of waning American influence. --- ### Will Artificial Intelligence Change the Global Balance? There is widespread agreement among technologists and policymakers that AI will be a defining factor in future geopolitics, much like nuclear weapons or the internet in the 20th century. However, analysts disagree on whether AI will cement U.S. leadership, enable a rival to overtake it, or decentralize global power altogether. **1. AI Could Reinforce U.S. Dominance** Some tech analysts and national security experts argue that AI will extend American primacy. Currently, U.S.-based companies (such as OpenAI, Google, Anthropic, and Meta) lead in developing frontier AI models, and U.S. firms like Nvidia dominate the design of the advanced semiconductors required to train them. * Analysts at institutions like the Center for Strategic and International Studies (CSIS) note that the U.S., alongside key allies in the Netherlands, Japan, and Taiwan, controls critical "chokepoints" in the semiconductor supply chain. Through export controls, Washington aims to maintain a durable technological lead that translates into superior economic productivity and military capabilities. **2. AI Could Accelerate a Shift Toward China** Other observers contend that AI provides a unique opportunity for rivals—specifically China—to close the power gap. In *AI Superpowers*, technologist and venture capitalist Kai-Fu Lee argued that while the U.S. historically led in foundational breakthroughs, China holds distinct advantages in rapid implementation, vast pools of data, and coordinated state support. * Proponents of this view argue that U.S. export controls may inadvertently spur China to achieve self-sufficiency in chip manufacturing and alternative AI architectures. Furthermore, military analysts warn that if China successfully integrates AI into autonomous weapons systems and cyber warfare faster than the U.S. Department of Defense, it could neutralize traditional American military advantages in regions like the Taiwan Strait. **3. AI Could Decentralize Power (Eroding Traditional Hegemony)** A third perspective suggests that AI will undermine the ability of *any* single nation to act as a traditional hegemon. * **The Rise of "Technopolarity":** Political scientist Ian Bremmer and technologist Mustafa Suleyman have written in *Foreign Affairs* about an emerging "technopolar" order. They argue that because the infrastructure, capital, and talent required to build frontier AI reside largely within private technology corporations rather than governments, tech companies themselves are becoming geopolitical actors that rival nation-states in influence. * **Asymmetric Proliferation:** Researchers focusing on open-source AI models point out that once advanced AI weights and algorithms are publicly released, they can be utilized by smaller nations and non-state actors at a fraction of the original development cost. Just as inexpensive drones changed modern battlefield dynamics, accessible AI could empower smaller states to conduct sophisticated cyber operations or boost their economies, further diffusing global power.

Whether the United States remains a hegemonic power—and how artificial intelligence might alter its standing—is a subject of ongoing debate among international relations scholars, economists, and technology analysts. ### Is the United States a Hegemonic Power? * **Arguments that US hegemony persists:** Many political scientists and defense analysts argue that the United States continues to hold a unique, dominant position in global affairs. They point to the US military's unmatched global reach and defense spending, its extensive network of alliances (such as NATO), and the enduring dominance of the US dollar as the world's primary reserve currency. Additionally, US leadership in higher education, scientific research, and cultural exports is often cited as evidence of sustained "soft power." * **Arguments that hegemony is declining:** Conversely, other scholars—particularly those who view the world as shifting toward multipolarity—contend that US hegemony has weakened since the end of the Cold War. They point to the rapid economic and military rise of China, the resurgence of Russia, and the growing geopolitical influence of regional powers such as India, Brazil, and the European Union. Proponents of this view also argue that domestic political polarization, rising national debt, and a reluctance among the US public to engage in foreign interventions limit Washington's ability to unilaterally shape global outcomes. ### Will AI Change the Balance of Power? * **AI as a reinforcement of US power:** Some technology and national security experts suggest that AI could solidify or even extend US influence. They note that the United States is home to many of the world's leading AI research labs and technology companies, benefits from deep venture capital markets, and maintains a significant lead in designing the advanced semiconductors required to train frontier AI models. From this perspective, AI-driven productivity gains and military applications could give the US a decisive edge. * **AI as a catalyst for multipolarity:** Other analysts argue that AI could erode US dominance. China, in particular, has made massive state-backed investments in AI, possesses vast amounts of data, and has demonstrated the ability to develop competitive models despite US export controls on advanced chips. Furthermore, the rapid proliferation of open-source AI models lowers the barrier to entry for smaller nations and non-state actors. Some experts contend that this diffusion of technology—spanning everything from automated cyber capabilities to economic productivity tools—could distribute power more broadly across the globe rather than concentrating it in a single country. Ultimately, most observers agree that the impact of AI on global power dynamics will depend not only on technological breakthroughs, but also on how nations regulate, deploy, and integrate these tools into their economies and militaries.

"Hegemon" is a contested label, and whether it fits depends on what you mean by it. **The case for yes (with caveats)** The US still holds a combination of advantages no other state matches: - Military reach: roughly 750 overseas installations, blue-water naval dominance, and alliance networks (NATO, Japan, South Korea, Australia). - Financial centrality: the dollar is the dominant reserve and invoicing currency, and US-controlled payment systems give it sanctions power. - Technological leadership in many frontier areas: semiconductors design, cloud, and especially AI. - Cultural and institutional influence, plus top universities that draw global talent. **The case for no, or "no longer"** - Relative decline: US share of global GDP has fallen from about 40% after WWII to roughly 25% nominally. China is the largest economy by purchasing power parity and the dominant manufacturer. - Limits on getting outcomes: the US couldn't decisively win in Iraq or Afghanistan, and it struggles to make other states follow its preferences on Russia sanctions, for example. - Contested regions: China is a near-peer in the Western Pacific, where distance and missile technology erode US advantages. - Domestic polarization and policy volatility make US commitments seem less reliable, which weakens the alliance system that was central to hegemony. A common reading is that the US is the **leading power in a more multipolar world**: still first by most measures, but no longer able to set the rules unilaterally. Some scholars prefer "primacy" to "hegemony" for this reason. **Will AI change it?** Honestly, nobody knows, but the plausible channels are: 1. **Economic growth:** If AI substantially raises productivity, the country that diffuses it best gains. The US has an edge in frontier labs, capital, and chips, but China is strong in deployment, data, manufacturing, and robotics, and the gap in model quality has been narrowing. 2. **Military:** AI-enabled targeting, drones, cyber, and logistics could shift balances. Cheap autonomous systems could also erode the advantage of expensive US platforms. 3. **Chokepoints:** US export controls on advanced chips are a deliberate attempt to preserve a lead. They may slow China, but also incentivize Chinese self-sufficiency. 4. **Winner-take-most dynamics:** If AI yields runaway advantages (a big if), whoever leads could entrench dominance. If capabilities diffuse quickly, as open models suggest they might, the effect is more leveling. 5. **Internal stress:** AI-driven job disruption, misinformation, and concentration of power in a few firms could strain US politics, and also those of rivals. My tentative view: AI is more likely to *amplify* existing competition than to settle it. The outcome depends less on who builds the best model than on who integrates AI into industry, military, and government most effectively, and on energy, chips, and talent. But if “hegemonic” means **the leading power that disproportionately shapes global rules, finance, security, technology, and institutions**, then yes, the US still fits. The US remains dominant or near-dominant in several key areas: - **Military reach:** unmatched global force projection, naval power, bases, alliances. - **Financial system:** the dollar, Treasury market, sanctions power, IMF/World Bank influence. - **Technology:** leading AI labs, cloud providers, chip design firms, software platforms. - **Alliances:** NATO, Japan, South Korea, Australia, parts of the Middle East. - **Culture and media:** universities, entertainment, English-language internet, global brands. - **Institutional agenda-setting:** ability to shape norms, standards, and coalitions. But the hegemony is now **contested**. China is the strongest systemic challenger; Russia can disrupt; middle powers hedge; and many countries resist US-led blocs. So I’d call the US a **contested hegemon** or the core power of a still-important but no longer uncontested US-led order. ## Will AI change that? Yes, but not in a simple “AI will end US hegemony” or “AI will preserve it” way. If AI dramatically boosts productivity, weapons systems, cyber capabilities, scientific research, and state capacity, the US could extend its lead. ### Ways AI could weaken US hegemony AI may also diffuse power: - Open-source models allow smaller states and non-state actors to access advanced capabilities. - China has enormous data, engineering talent, industrial capacity, and state coordination. - AI could reduce the advantage of expensive bureaucracies or legacy institutions. - Cyber, disinformation, autonomous weapons, and biosecurity risks could make power more chaotic. - If AI automates innovation, countries with abundant energy, compute infrastructure, and manufacturing capacity may gain quickly. - Dependence on Taiwan and semiconductor supply chains remains a strategic vulnerability. It will empower rivals, increase the value of compute and energy, intensify technological blocs, and make economic and military competition faster-moving. The term “hegemonic power” generally refers to a state that holds preponderant influence over others—militarily, economically, culturally, and institutionally—shaping global rules and norms in its own interest. Applying this to the United States today, there is a strong case that it remains the world’s foremost hegemon, but that dominance is increasingly qualified and contested. Let me break down the two parts of your question. ### Do I agree that the US is a hegemonic power? **Yes, with important caveats.** The US retains unmatched structural advantages, but the nature of its hegemony has shifted from the unipolar moment of the 1990s to a more competitive, multi-faceted landscape. **Evidence supporting continued US hegemony:** - **Military pre-eminence:** The US accounts for roughly 40% of global military spending, maintains over 750 overseas bases, and possesses unique power-projection capabilities (carrier strike groups, global logistics, space assets). No other country can militarily intervene globally at scale. - **Economic centrality:** The US dollar remains the world’s primary reserve currency (around 58% of allocated reserves), its financial markets are the deepest and most liquid, and it is home to tech giants that dominate the digital economy. US GDP is still the largest by a wide margin at market exchange rates. - **Institutional and normative influence:** The US created or co-created the core post-WWII institutions (UN, IMF, World Bank, NATO) and continues to wield outsized influence within them. Its universities, media, and pop culture give it immense soft power, even if that has been dented in recent years. - **Technological leadership:** From the internet to AI, the US has been the primary source of breakthrough innovations. It hosts the leading AI companies (OpenAI, Google DeepMind, Anthropic, etc.) and the most advanced semiconductor design firms (NVIDIA, AMD, Qualcomm, Broadcom). Its capacity to set technological standards is a key pillar of hegemony. **Caveats and counter-arguments:** - **Hegemony is not control.** The US cannot unilaterally dictate outcomes—witness the limits of its power in Iraq, Afghanistan, or in the face of China’s rise and Russia’s revisionism. - **Rising challengers:** China has narrowed the gap in economic weight (PPP GDP now larger), technological innovation, and global infrastructure investment (Belt and Road). It is building alternative institutional frameworks (BRICS+, AIIB, etc.) and offers a systemic rival to the liberal order. - **Diffusion of power:** Power is shifting not only to states but to non-state actors (multinational corporations, mega-tech firms, terrorist networks, global movements). Many problems—climate change, pandemics, cyber threats—require collective action where even the hegemon cannot act alone. - **Domestic constraint:** Political polarization, fiscal limits, and public fatigue with foreign entanglements have made US leadership less reliable, visible in the withdrawal from the TPP under Trump, the chaotic Afghanistan exit, and periodic threats to alliances. Thus, the US is still a hegemon, but a “constrained hegemon” or a “first among unequals” rather than an unchallenged superpower. The post-Cold War unipolar moment has given way to a complex multi-domain competition. ### Will AI change that? **Potentially yes — but the direction is not predetermined.** AI could reinforce US hegemony, erode it, or transform the very nature of hegemony. The outcome will depend on how the US and its rivals harness AI and adapt domestically and internationally. **Scenario 1: AI reinforces US hegemony** - **Economic and military superiority compound.** The US leads in frontier AI models (large language models, autonomous agents) and critical hardware (GPUs, fabrication tools). If it maintains or widens this lead, AI-driven productivity gains and military applications (autonomous weapons, intelligence analysis, cyber capabilities) could widen the gap with competitors. - **Standards and norms:** The US is actively shaping global AI governance (the AI Executive Order, the Bletchley Park process, export controls on chips). If it can set the rules for safe, ethical AI globally, it locks in an advantage that aligns with its values and interests. - **Network effects and data/AI scaling:** The US-led digital ecosystem (Facebook, Google, AWS) could benefit from AI integration, making its platforms even more indispensable. AI might also supercharge its surveillance and national security capabilities, enhancing its panopticon reach. **Scenario2: AI erodes or redistributes hegemony** - **Lowered barriers to entry for rivals:** AI could democratize disruptive technology. Hackers or small states could use AI to develop lethal autoomous drones, synthetic disinformation, or cyberweapons at a fraction of current costs. This could nullify traditional US advantages in expensive platforms like aircraft carriers. - **China’s focused push:** China has clearly identified AI as a core strategic domain, with massive state investment, huge data pools, weaker privacy constraints, and rapid advances in areas like facial recognition, swarm drones, and perhaps soon in generative AI (DeepSeek models show narrowing gaps despite sanctions). If Chinese AI becomes competitive or superior in key applications, it could shift the digital infrastructure and military balance in Asia and globally. - **Erosion of the dollar and financial hegemony:** AI could accelerate the use of digital currencies (including CBDCs) and alternative payment systems (e.g., based on Alipay or a digital euro), diminishing the dollar’s international role if the US does not lead in financial technology. - **Cognitive and information warfare:** AI-generated deepfakes and micro-targeted propaganda can thoroughly pollute the information environment. For an open society like the US, this poses asymmetric risks: adversaries could exploit AI to sow discord and erode trust in democratic institutions, weakening US soft power and domestic cohesion. **Scenario3: Transformation of hegemony itself** - **AI as a systemic shift:** Just as nuclear weapons changed the logic of great-power rivalry, AI might transform what it means to be a hegemon. Dominance may come to depend less on territory and conventional forces, and more on control of AI supply chains (advanced chips, rare minerals, talent), data infrastructure, and algorithmic governance. In this new domain, the US currently has a lead, but the race is far from over. - **The emergence of “AI sovereignity”:** Many states may pursue independent AI capabilities to avoid relying on either the US or China. This could fragment the global tech landscape and make the world less amenable to any single hegemon. - **Risks of entrenchment vs. instability:** If the US uses AI to consolidate its dominance (e.g., through total surveillance driven by AI), it might provoke severe blowback and accelerate alliance fracturing. Conversely, if it fails to adopt AI effectively in governance and defense, its relative decline could be hastened. ### Conclution The US remains the world’s leading hegemonic power, though it is far from omnipotent. AI is a wild card: it could deepen US advantages and extend its primacy, or it could empower challengers and decrease the returns from physical size and legacy military power. The key factors will be who leads in AI capability, who controls the underlying supply chains, who shapes global norms, and—most critically—which political system better adaptes to the domestic disruptions (job displacement, inequality, surveillance) that AI will bring. So, AI will likely change the nature and durability of US hegemony, but whether it preserves or ends it is still an open question.

The term **“hegemonic power”** in international relations typically describes a state that possesses disproportionate influence across economic, military, technological, and cultural domains, and which plays a central role in shaping the rules and institutions of the global order. ### 1. Is the US currently a hegemonic power? By most scholarly and geopolitical metrics, **yes**, the United States still functions as the global hegemon, though its position is often described as “contested” or “relative” rather than absolute: * **Economic:** The US dollar remains the world’s primary reserve currency, and the US hosts the deepest capital markets. * **Military:** It has the largest defense budget globally and a network of alliances (NATO, bilateral treaties) unmatched by any other power. * **Technological & Cultural:** US firms dominate semiconductors design, software, and foundational internet platforms; its universities lead in research; and its cultural exports shape global norms. * **Institutional:** It was central to creating the post-1945 institutions (IMF, World Bank, UN frameworks) that still govern international relations. AI Accelerates Power Diffusion or Shifts** * **China:** Through state-directed investment and massive data pools, China could achieve parity or superiority in applied AI (e.g., facial recognition, autonomous logistics), challenging US tech dominance. * **Open Source:** The proliferation of open-weight models (like Meta’s Llama or Mistral) may democratize capability, allowing smaller nations or non-state actors to bypass US-controlled infrastructure. * **Compute Geography:** If energy or chip manufacturing shifts (e.g., Middle East investments in data centers), the US leverage via export controls could weaken. **C. AI Disrupts the Hegemon Internally** If AI causes severe labor displacement or algorithmic misinformation that destabilizes US domestic cohesion, the state’s capacity to project power abroad could diminish—historically, hegemons often decline due to internal rot rather than external conquest. ### Conclusion AI will not immediately end US hegemony, but it is the **central arena** in which that hegemony is now being contested. The power will belong to whoever best integrates AI into their economy, military, and governance while managing its societal risks. The US position is strong but not guaranteed; the next decade of policy choices will determine whether AI acts as a pillar of continued dominance or a catalyst for a more multipolar world.

# US Hegemony and AI ## Is the US a hegemonic power? This is genuinely debated in international relations, and I think the honest answer is: **largely yes, but with important qualifications and the trend is worth watching.** **The case for:** - **Military**: The US maintains roughly a quarter of global defense spending, unmatched force projection (global basing network, 11 carrier strike groups), and the world's most capable nuclear arsenal alongside Russia's - **Financial**: The dollar serves as the dominant reserve currency, giving the US outsized influence through sanctions capability and control of key financial infrastructure - **Institutional**: The US founded and remains the leading power in most major post-1945 international institutions (UN Security Council veto, NATO, IMF, World Bank) - **Technological & cultural soft power**: Silicon Valley, American universities, English as lingua franca, entertainment industry influence - **Alliance network**: No other state has a comparable web of formal security alliances spanning every continent **The qualifications:** - The **"unipolar moment"** (Krauthammer's term) arguably peaked around 2001-2005. The US struggled to translate enormous power into desired outcomes in Iraq and Afghanistan, which exposed limits on converting material power into political results. - **China** is a genuine systemic peer competitor in ways no rival has been since the Soviet Union — but in economics rather than pure military terms. It's already the largest economy by PPP. - **Regional hegemons** (China in East Asia, Russia in parts of the post-Soviet space, Iran/Middle East dynamics) mean the US doesn't dominate everywhere equally. - Scholars like **Mearsheimer** would argue the US is the most powerful *state* in the system but that anarchy persists — there's no world government, and regional balances constrain American power. - The **"rules-based order"** framing sometimes obscures that the US itself selectively ignores rules when convenient (Iraq invasion, various unilateral sanctions), which undermines the institutional dimension of hegemony. **My assessment**: The US is best described as a **primus inter pares** with genuine hegemonic characteristics — dominant in most (but not all) domains of power. Strict hegemonic stability theory (Gilpin, Kindleberger) envisions a state that both leads and effectively manages the global system. The US does this imperfectly, and its ability to do so appears to be **gradually eroding** even before we consider AI. --- ## Will AI change that? This is inherently speculative, but I think the answer is **AI will both reinforce and erode US hegemony simultaneously — with the balance depending on several contingent factors.** ### Factors reinforcing US hegemony through AI: **1. Current AI leadership is concentrated in the US** - The frontier models (GPT-4, Gemini, Claude, Llama) are overwhelmingly developed by American companies - US dominance in research output, talent attraction, and AI-related publications remains significant - The compute infrastructure (NVIDIA, cloud providers) and capital markets that fund AI are primarily American **2. AI as a power amplifier** - If AI strengthens the specific mechanisms of hegemony — intelligence gathering, military effectiveness, economic optimization, sanctions enforcement — then the current leader benefits disproportionately - AI could make American military capability even more asymmetric (autonomous systems, better logistics, decision-support) - Financial surveillance and sanctions enforcement powered by AI could strengthen dollar hegemony **3. Talent and ecosystem lock-in** - The concentration of top AI researchers, venture capital, and institutional knowledge in Silicon Valley and major US universities creates self-reinforcing advantages - This mirrors historical patterns where technological leadership creates network effects **4. The "nuclear weapon" analogy** - Like nuclear technology in the 1940s-50s, advanced AI might initially require enormous resources, concentrating advantages in the state that gets there first ### Factors eroding US hegemony through AI: **1. AI as an equalizer** - AI could function more like the **internet or printing press** — diffusing capabilities rather than concentrating them - Smaller states, non-state actors, or even individuals could gain disproportionate capabilities (cyber operations, intelligence analysis, propaganda, economic optimization) - A mid-sized power with good AI could potentially match capabilities that previously required vast human capital and infrastructure **2. The China competition** - If China reaches parity in AI (or surpasses in specific domains), it could close critical capability gaps — particularly in military technology, economic optimization, and surveillance - China's advantages: massive data pools, strong state direction of AI research, semiconductor manufacturing ambitions, demographic scale - An AI race could be **the** decisive arena where US hegemony is either consolidated or lost **3. Domestic disruption** - AI-driven economic displacement could weaken US domestic cohesion, political stability, and fiscal capacity - If AI increases inequality, undermines employment, or triggers social instability, the domestic foundations of hegemony erode - Hegemony requires domestic legitimacy and capacity — AI could strain both **4. Open-source diffusion** - While frontier models are concentrated, open-source models (Llama, Mistral, etc.) can be fine-tuned and deployed by any state or actor - This creates a floor of AI capability that spreads globally regardless of who leads at the frontier **5. Decision-making and escalation risks** - AI could introduce new instability into great power relations (AI-driven arms races, autonomous weapons concerns, AI-enabled cyber conflicts) - Paradoxically, if AI makes everyone more capable of defense and offense simultaneously, it might destabilize the existing hierarchy **6. Loss of informational advantages** - US intelligence hegemony partly depends on information superiority. AI-powered intelligence by rivals could erode this specific advantage. ### The deeper structural question: The key uncertainty is whether AI is more like: - **A power-concentrating technology** (like nuclear weapons or aircraft carriers — requiring massive investment and benefiting incumbents), or - **A power-diffusing technology** (like the internet, printing press, or gunpowder — lowering barriers and empowering challengers) I suspect the answer is **both, at different levels**: - At the **front

## Is the US a hegemon? It depends on how strictly you define the term, but the case is strong, with important caveats. **The case for "yes":** - **Military:** The US spends roughly as much on defense as the next nine or ten countries combined, has about 750 overseas bases, and is the only country that can project power globally on a sustained basis (carrier groups, airlift, a global alliance network). - **Financial:** The dollar is about 58% of global reserves and is used in roughly 88% of FX transactions. Control over dollar clearing gives Washington sanctions power no other state has. - **Institutional:** The US was the chief architect of the IMF, World Bank, NATO, and much of the postwar order, and still holds outsized influence in them. - **Technological and cultural:** It leads in frontier tech, top universities, venture capital, and global media and platforms. **The case for "not anymore, or not fully":** - **China** is a peer or near-peer in manufacturing (around 30% of global output), shipbuilding, many supply chains, and increasingly in some technologies. Hegemony implies no serious rival, and that's no longer clearly true. - **The share of global GDP** has fallen from around 40-50% after WWII to about 25% nominal, and less at purchasing power parity. - **Limits of power:** Iraq and Afghanistan showed that military dominance doesn't translate into political outcomes. Much of the Global South now hedges rather than aligns. - **Domestic factors:** Polarization and policy swings between administrations make US commitments less predictable, which erodes the trust that hegemony partly runs on. **My assessment:** The US was clearly hegemonic from roughly 1991 to 2008. Today it's better described as the **preeminent power in an increasingly contested system**. It's still first in most dimensions that matter, but it no longer sets rules largely unchallenged. Many scholars would push back in both directions. Some argue US structural advantages (demography, geography, alliances, the dollar) are more durable than declinists think. Others argue the unipolar moment is already over. ## Will AI change that? Plausibly yes, and possibly a lot, but the direction is genuinely uncertain. Here are the main scenarios: 1. **AI entrenches US primacy.** The US currently leads in frontier models, top labs, talent, capital, and, through Nvidia and allied firms like TSMC and ASML, the compute supply chain. Export controls aim to lock in that lead. If AI produces large gains in productivity, science, and military capability, and the lead compounds, the US could regain a more decisive position than it had even in the 1990s. 2. **AI accelerates multipolarity.** Model capabilities have diffused fast. Open-weight models and Chinese labs like DeepSeek have closed gaps quickly, and algorithmic advances spread easily. If AI becomes a commodity, advantage shifts to whoever deploys it at scale in manufacturing, logistics, and the military. China's industrial base and state coordination could make that an edge for Beijing. 3. **AI disrupts the basis of power itself.** Cheap autonomous drones and AI-enabled cyber and information operations could favor offense and smaller actors, weakening expensive legacy platforms like carriers. This could erode the US military advantage even if the US "wins" at frontier AI. 4. **Domestic factors dominate.** How countries handle AI-driven labor disruption, energy demands (data centers need enormous power, an area where China is building much faster), and political stability may matter more than who has the best model. **Key variables to watch:** - Whether AI progress keeps scaling with compute, which favors the US and its controls, or plateaus and diffuses, which favors broader adoption. - Taiwan's security, since leading-edge chips are concentrated there. - Energy and grid build-out. - Whether AI transforms military balances before diplomacy or norms adapt. **Bottom line:** AI is probably the single biggest wildcard in the US-China balance. If its returns are concentrated and compounding, it could restore something closer to true US hegemony. If it diffuses quickly, it could hasten a multipolar world. Right now the US holds the lead in the technology itself, while China arguably holds advantages in deploying it physically at scale. Which of those matters more is one of the defining open questions of the next decade.

**Is the US a hegemon?** Partly. I think the label is defensible but increasingly needs qualifiers. The case for: - **Military:** The US has unmatched global power projection, with hundreds of overseas bases and alliance networks (NATO, Japan, South Korea, Australia) that no rival has. - **Finance:** The dollar dominates reserves, trade invoicing, and global payments, which gives Washington unusual leverage through sanctions. - **Technology and institutions:** The US leads in frontier technology, top universities, and capital markets, and has outsized influence in institutions it largely designed. The US itself has become more ambivalent about maintaining the order it built. # Is the US a Hegemon? Will AI Change That? ## The hegemony question "Hegemon" has a specific meaning: a state with preponderant power that sets the rules of the international system. By that standard, the US case is mixed: **Arguments for:** - Still the largest economy by nominal GDP, with the dollar as the dominant reserve currency (~58% of reserves) - Unmatched military: ~750 overseas bases, 11 carrier groups, global alliance network (NATO, Japan, South Korea, AUKUS) - Dominates key tech ecosystems (semiconductors design, cloud, AI frontier labs, major platforms) - English, Hollywood, universities — significant soft power **Arguments against:** - China rivals or exceeds the US in PPP GDP, manufacturing output, shipbuilding, and some tech domains - The US struggles to impose outcomes (Afghanistan, Ukraine, Red Sea shipping, Israel/Gaza diplomacy) - BRICS+ expansion, de-dollarization efforts, and middle powers hedging suggest a drift toward multipolarity - Domestic polarization undermines credible long-term commitments Most IR scholars now describe the system as **"unipolar in decline"** or **"bipolar/multipolar emerging"** rather than cleanly hegemonic. I'd lean toward: the US remains the single most powerful state, but no longer a hegemon in the full sense it was in the 1990s. ## Will AI change that? AI is a genuinely structural variable, but it cuts both ways. **Reasons AI could reinforce US primacy:** - Frontier labs (OpenAI, Anthropic, Google DeepMind, Meta, xAI) are overwhelmingly US-based - The US controls the chip supply chain chokepoints: Nvidia (design), TSMC (allied), ASML (allied), and uses export controls aggressively - Capital markets fund AI capex at a scale no rival matches - Military AI integration (DoD, Palantir, Anduril) could widen the capability gap **Reasons AI could erode it:** - China is closing the gap faster than expected (DeepSeek, Qwen, domestic chip progress) - Open-weight models diffuse capability globally — hegemony via tech scarcity is harder when models leak - AI could compress development timelines for middle powers (India, UAE, EU) to build sovereign capability - If AI drives a productivity boom, whoever deploys it best across the real economy wins — and that's not guaranteed to be the US - AI-enabled information warfare and cyber could level asymmetric playing fields **The wild card:** if transformative AI arrives, it may matter less which country "has" it and more who controls the compute, the models, and whether governance is national or corporate. A world where a few US-based labs hold decisive capabilities isn't quite "US hegemony" — it's something newer and less well theorized. ## My take The US is the leading power but not an unchallenged hegemon. AI will likely **extend US technological lead in the short term** (5–10 years) due to compute and capital advantages, but **won't restore 1990s-style hegemony** because (a) China is a peer competitor in a way the USSR wasn't economically, and (b) AI's diffusion dynamics favor eventual multipolarity. The more interesting question may be whether traditional state-centric framing even survives the AI transition. Happy to go deeper on any piece — the chip war, Chinese AI progress, dollar dynamics, or the theoretical debate.

Whether the United States is currently a hegemonic power is a subject of significant debate in international relations, and the impact of artificial intelligence on global power dynamics remains equally uncertain. Analysts generally agree that the US is the world's preeminent superpower, but there is no universal consensus on whether it qualifies as a "hegemon"—a term implying not just overwhelming power, but the ability to unilaterally shape international rules, norms, and outcomes. Here is a breakdown of the different perspectives on US hegemony today, and how AI might alter the global balance of power. ### Perspectives on Current US Hegemony **The Case for US Hegemony** Those who argue the US remains a hegemon point to its unmatched position across multiple domains: * **Military:** The US maintains a global network of bases, unparalleled power-projection capabilities, and a defense budget that exceeds those of the next several countries combined. * **Economic and Financial:** The US possesses the largest or second-largest GDP (depending on the measurement metric) and enjoys unique advantages through the US dollar, which remains the dominant global reserve currency, granting the US significant leverage over international financial systems. * **Soft Power and Alliances:** The US leads major global institutions (IMF, World Bank) and commands an unparalleled network of alliances (such as NATO) that amplify its global reach. * **Technological Innovation:** The US has historically been the epicenter of the digital revolution, home to the world's largest tech giants and leading research universities. **The Case Against US Hegemony (The Multipolar View)** Conversely, many scholars argue that US hegemony has eroded and the world is shifting toward multipolarity: * **The Rise of Competitors:** China's rapid economic growth, military modernization, and diplomatic initiatives (like the Belt and Road Initiative) present a systemic rival. Russia's actions and the growing influence of the BRICS nations also challenge a unipolar US-led order. * **Relative Economic Decline:** While the US economy is massive, its share of global GDP has decreased from its post-Cold War peak, as emerging markets develop. * **Domestic Constraints:** Intense political polarization and domestic policy challenges have, at times, constrained the US's ability to project consistent global leadership. * **Limits of Military Power:** Events in the Middle East and Afghanistan have demonstrated that overwhelming military superiority does not always translate into the ability to achieve desired political outcomes. ### Will AI Change This? AI is widely viewed as a "general-purpose technology" akin to electricity or the internet, meaning it will likely transform economies, militaries, and societies. Whether AI will reinforce US dominance or erode it is heavily debated. **How AI Could Reinforce US Hegemony** * **The Algorithm and Cloud Advantage:** The US currently leads in the development of foundational AI models (e.g., OpenAI, Google, Anthropic). If AI becomes the foundational layer of future economic and military power, the US could extend its lead. * **Hardware Chokepoints:** The US and its allies (specifically the Netherlands) dominate the design of advanced AI chips (like Nvidia's GPUs) and the semiconductor manufacturing equipment required to make them. US export controls on these technologies are currently used as a geopolitical tool to restrict competitors' AI advancements. * **Military Integration:** Integrating AI into military systems—such as autonomous vehicles, logistics optimization, and intelligence analysis—could widen the capability gap between the US military and its rivals. **How AI Could Erode US Hegemony** * **China's State-Backed AI Push:** China has made AI a top national priority, leveraging vast amounts of data, a massive pool of AI engineers, and state-directed investment. China is highly competitive in applied AI, manufacturing, and surveillance technologies, and could achieve parity or supremacy in certain critical domains. * **Democratization via Open Source:** The open-source AI movement is making powerful models widely available. If cutting-edge AI becomes commoditized and cheap, it could level the playing field, allowing smaller nations and non-state actors to access capabilities that were previously the exclusive domain of superpowers. * **Asymmetric Warfare:** AI lowers

Whether the United States is a hegemonic power is a central debate in international relations. As an AI, I don't have personal opinions or the capacity to "agree" or "disagree," but I can outline the arguments used by political scientists and analysts to answer that question, as well as the ways in which Artificial Intelligence is expected to reshape global power. ### Part 1: Is the US a Hegemonic Power? In political science, **hegemony** refers to the dominance of one state over others, not just through military force, but through economic, institutional, and cultural influence. #### Arguments that the US is a Hegemonic Power: * **Military Dominance:** The US maintains a global network of military bases, possesses the world's most advanced technology, and has a defense budget that exceeds the next several countries combined. Its ability to project power globally (blue-water navy, air superiority) is unmatched. * **Economic and Financial Power:** The US Dollar remains the world’s primary reserve currency, giving the US significant leverage over global finance. The US also hosts the world's largest capital markets and many of the most influential multinational corporations. * **Institutional Leadership:** The US has been the primary architect and leader of the post-WWII international order, including institutions like the United Nations, the IMF, the World Bank, and NATO. * **Soft Power:** Through Hollywood, Silicon Valley, and the English language, US culture and values exert a profound influence on global consumer habits and social norms. #### Arguments that US Hegemony is Waning: * **The Rise of China:** The shift toward a **multipolar world** is the most significant challenge. China’s economic growth, military modernization, and "Belt and Road Initiative" suggest a world where power is shared among several poles. * **Economic Shifts:** The rise of the BRICS nations (Brazil, Russia, India, China, South Africa) and the potential "de-dollarization" of global trade suggest a move away from US-centric economic structures. * **Internal Fragmentation:** Analysts often point to political polarization and social division within the US as factors that may diminish its ability to lead effectively on the world stage. --- ### Part 2: Will AI Change the Balance of Power? AI is often described as a "general-purpose technology," similar to electricity or the steam engine. It is expected to be a primary driver of the next era of geopolitics. There are two main ways AI could change the status of a hegemonic power: #### Scenario A: AI Reinforces US Hegemony (The "Technological Lead" Model) If the US maintains its current lead in AI development, it could cement its dominance: * **Economic Productivity:** AI-driven breakthroughs in medicine, energy, and manufacturing could lead to massive GDP growth, widening the gap between the US and its competitors. * **Military Superiority:** AI-integrated command systems, autonomous drones, and advanced cyber-warfare capabilities could make traditional military advantages even more insurmountable. * **Digital Hegemony:** If the world’s most influential AI models and platforms are American-owned (e.g., OpenAI, Google, Microsoft), the US will control the "digital infrastructure" of the 21st century, much like it controls the financial infrastructure of the 20th. #### Scenario B: AI Disrupts Hegemony (The "Multipolar/Asymmetric" Model) Conversely, AI could act as a "great equalizer" or a disruptor: * **The AI Arms Race:** If China or other nations achieve a "breakthrough" in AI (particularly in hardware like semiconductors or in data processing), the US could lose its technological edge, leading to a bipolar or multipolar world. * **Asymmetric Warfare:** AI lowers the barrier to entry for high-impact disruption. Small states or non-state actors could use AI-driven cyberattacks, deepfakes, or autonomous weapons to destabilize a much larger, more powerful hegemon. * **Decentralization:** If AI development becomes highly decentralized (open-source models), the ability of any single nation-state to control the technology diminishes, potentially shifting power away from traditional governments toward tech companies or global networks. ### Conclusion The consensus among many experts is that we are moving from an era of **unipolarity** (US dominance) toward an era of **technological competition**. Whether AI preserves or destroys US hegemony depends on three critical variables: 1. **Compute:** Who controls the high-end chips (semiconductors)? 2. **Data:** Who has access to the most diverse and massive datasets? 3. **Talent:** Which nation can attract and retain the world's best AI researchers? The outcome of this "AI race" will likely determine the structure of global power for the remainder of the century.
